# Tako Tank Daily Discovery: question bank export

608 questions. Status counts: 556 unverified, 27 review, 25 rejected. Only **verified** questions can be shown to players.

Each entry: the question, the three choices (the correct one in bold), the explanation, the source, and its checking status. `risk` is what the researcher flagged for the checker; the research note says what the search showed (it is not a quote).

## cephalopods (50)

### dd-0001 · easy · unverified

**How many hearts does an octopus have?**

- A. One
- B. Two
- C. **Three** ✓

Two hearts pump blood through the gills to pick up oxygen, and a third sends that oxygen-rich blood around the rest of the body.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/news/feb26/undersea-creatures-valentines-day.html) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/day-octopus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.heart-count` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says octopuses have three hearts: two pump blood to the gills and the third circulates oxygenated blood to the body; Monterey Bay Aquarium's day octopus page describes the same split.</sub>

### dd-0002 · easy · unverified

**Octopus blood is blue because its oxygen-carrying protein is built around which metal?**

- A. Iron
- B. **Copper** ✓
- C. Zinc

Our hemoglobin uses iron, but octopus haemocyanin uses copper, which turns blue when it carries oxygen.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.blood-copper` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM says the oxygen-carrying protein haemocyanin contains copper rather than iron; Smithsonian makes the same contrast with human iron-based blood.</sub>

### dd-0003 · medium · unverified

**Smithsonian scientists note that octopus blood's oxygen carrier, hemocyanin, loses its hold on oxygen in what kind of water?**

- A. Colder water
- B. **More acidic water** ✓
- C. Darker water

Hemocyanin works best in cold water but grips oxygen less well as water acidifies, so warming, acidifying seas could strain cephalopod circulation.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.hemocyanin-acidic-water` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Ocean says hemocyanin is most efficient in cold water but loses its hold on oxygen in more acidic water.</sub>

### dd-0004 · hard · unverified

**Roughly how many neurons does the common octopus have?**

- A. About 5 million
- B. **About 500 million** ✓
- C. About 50 billion

The common octopus has around 500 million neurons, and most of them are not in its head but in its arms.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3073264/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.neuron-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NHM says the common octopus (Octopus vulgaris) has roughly 500 million neurons. A PMC review gives about 50 million in the central brain plus about 300 million in the arms, broadly consistent.</sub>

### dd-0005 · medium · unverified

**Where are about two-thirds of an octopus's neurons found?**

- A. **In its arms** ✓
- B. In its head
- C. Around its gills

Roughly two-thirds of an octopus's neurons sit in its arms, which can sense and react to the world with a lot of local control.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.arm-neurons` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM and Smithsonian both say about two-thirds of an octopus's neurons are in its arms.</sub>

### dd-0006 · hard · unverified

**An octopus's ring-shaped brain wraps around which body part, so its food must be broken into small pieces?**

- A. Its heart
- B. Its ink sac
- C. **Its esophagus** ✓

The food tube runs right through the octopus brain, so prey has to be broken down finely before it is swallowed.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.brain-around-esophagus` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian says the esophagus runs through the brain so prey must be finely broken up before swallowing; NHM describes the central brain as ring-shaped around the esophagus.</sub>

### dd-0007 · medium · unverified

**What is the beak of a giant Pacific octopus made of?**

- A. Bone
- B. Coral-like shell
- C. **Chitin** ✓

The beak is made of chitin, a tough material that works much like the keratin in human hair and nails. It is not bone.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/giant-pacific-octopus/) · second source: [NOAA Ocean Today](https://oceantoday.noaa.gov/howtofeedagiantoctopus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.beak-material` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium says the beak is made of chitin, which functions similarly to keratin; a NOAA Ocean Today transcript says the beak is not bone but very tough.</sub>

### dd-0008 · medium · Field Guide: escape · unverified

**When a giant Pacific octopus squeezes through a tight gap, which body part limits how small the gap can be?**

- A. Its mantle
- B. **Its beak** ✓
- C. Its suckers

With a soft, boneless body, the hard beak is the main limit. If the beak fits through, the rest of the octopus can follow.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/giant-pacific-octopus/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://www.ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/beautiful-and-deadly-octopus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.beak-limits-squeeze` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium says the main restriction in a tight space is the beak and that if the beak fits, the body will too; Smithsonian notes that with no bones except the beak, octopuses fit through almost any hole.</sub>

### dd-0009 · medium · Field Guide: escape · review

**About how small a hole can a fully grown giant Pacific octopus squeeze through?**

- A. **About the size of a lemon** ✓
- B. About the size of a dinner plate
- C. About the size of a car tire

An adult giant Pacific octopus can weigh dozens of pounds yet pour itself through an opening about the size of a lemon.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/giant-pacific-octopus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.lemon-sized-hole` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium says a fully grown giant Pacific octopus can fit through a hole about the size of a lemon.</sub>

### dd-0010 · medium · unverified

**How long does a giant Pacific octopus typically live?**

- A. About 6 months
- B. About 30 years
- C. **About 3 to 5 years** ✓

Three to five years is short for an animal this big, but it is long by octopus standards.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-pacific-octopus) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/giant_pacific_octopus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.lifespan` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium says the species lives about three to five years, long for an octopus; Aquarium of the Pacific gives two to five years.</sub>

### dd-0011 · medium · unverified

**A giant Pacific octopus, which can grow to many feet across, hatches from an egg about the size of what?**

- A. **A grain of rice** ✓
- B. A golf ball
- C. A chicken egg

One of the biggest octopuses in the world starts life in an egg about the size of a grain of rice.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-pacific-octopus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.egg-size` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium says the giant Pacific octopus hatches from an egg the size of a rice grain.</sub>

### dd-0012 · easy · unverified

**What happens to a female giant Pacific octopus after she lays her eggs?**

- A. She leaves to find a new mate
- B. She lays another clutch next year
- C. **She enters senescence and dies** ✓

She stays to guard and clean her eggs for months, hardly eating, and dies around the time they hatch. She never breeds again.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-pacific-octopus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.female-senescence` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium says that after laying eggs, female giant Pacific octopuses enter senescence, the end-of-life process that ends the female's life.</sub>

### dd-0013 · medium · unverified

**Roughly how many suckers can each arm of a giant Pacific octopus carry?**

- A. A couple dozen
- B. **A couple hundred** ✓
- C. A couple thousand

Each arm can carry a couple of hundred suckers, which adds up to roughly 2,000 across all eight arms.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-pacific-octopus) · second source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/giant-pacific-octopus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.suckers-per-arm` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search summary: sources give up to about 240 suckers on each arm, and Monterey Bay Aquarium gives roughly 2,240 total for females. Verifier should confirm which page carries the per-arm figure.</sub>

### dd-0014 · medium · unverified

**Roughly how much can an adult giant Pacific octopus weigh?**

- A. A pound or two
- B. **Tens of pounds, up to around 100** ✓
- C. Over 1,000 pounds

Adults commonly weigh tens of pounds, and large ones approach 100 pounds or more, making this one of the biggest octopuses.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/giant-pacific-octopus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.gpo.adult-weight` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium says adults can weigh from 40 to 100 pounds with a relaxed tip-to-tip span of 12 to 14 feet. Record weights differ between sources, so the question asks only about typical adults.</sub>

### dd-0015 · medium · Field Guide: camo · unverified

**How does an octopus make a chromatophore, a tiny pigment sac in its skin, show its color?**

- A. It pumps blood into the sac
- B. **Muscles stretch the sac open** ✓
- C. It grows new pigment overnight

Each chromatophore is an elastic bag of pigment. Nerve-controlled muscles pull it wide to show color and relax to hide it.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9590006/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.chromatophore-muscles` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The octopus spectral-data study describes chromatophores as pigment-filled elastic sacs: muscle contraction stretches the sac to show pigment and relaxation lets it retract. Smithsonian notes thousands of pigment cells cover the body.</sub>

### dd-0016 · hard · Field Guide: camo · unverified

**Octopuses match colorful backgrounds even though their eyes have how many types of visual pigment?**

- A. **One** ✓
- B. Three
- C. Twelve

With only one visual pigment, octopuses are colorblind, so their color-changing skin likely tracks brightness rather than hue.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9590006/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.colorblind-pigment` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: The spectral camouflage study says octopuses are colour-blind because their eyes have only one type of visual pigment, so chromatophores likely respond to brightness rather than chromaticity.</sub>

### dd-0017 · medium · Field Guide: camo · unverified

**What do octopuses control to change their skin from smooth to bumpy or spiky?**

- A. **Small skin projections called papillae** ✓
- B. Rows of hard scales
- C. The suckers on their arms

By raising or flattening skin projections called papillae, an octopus can mimic the texture of rock, algae or coral.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9590006/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6059360/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.octopus.papillae-texture` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The spectral study says octopuses modulate texture by controlling the size of projections (papillae) on their skin, from smooth to bumps and spikes.</sub>

### dd-0018 · hard · Field Guide: camo · unverified

**Once a cuttlefish raises the bumps in its skin, what lets it hold that textured disguise using little energy?**

- A. **The bumps lock in place** ✓
- B. It stops breathing to stay still
- C. It fills the bumps with sand

Research on cuttlefish skin found that extended papillae become locked, so the animal can stay textured without constant effort.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6059360/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.cuttlefish.papillae-lock` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The cuttlefish papillae study found that once papillae extend they become locked in place, letting the cuttlefish hold a textured disguise while expending minimal energy.</sub>

### dd-0019 · medium · Field Guide: ink · unverified

**What is a cephalopod 'pseudomorph'?**

- A. A newly hatched octopus
- B. **A mucus-rich ink blob shaped roughly like the animal** ✓
- C. A layer of skin the animal sheds

Some squid and octopuses squirt a mucus-thickened blob of ink about their own size. A predator may attack the decoy while the real animal slips away.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4052311/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopuses-squids-and-relatives)

Fact id: `cephalopods.ink.pseudomorph` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The Marine Drugs ink review describes pseudomorphs as darkened blobs with relatively more mucus, named because they resemble the form of a cephalopod; Smithsonian says ink can act as a look-alike the predator attacks.</sub>

### dd-0020 · medium · Field Guide: ink · unverified

**What pigment gives cephalopod ink its dark color?**

- A. Chlorophyll
- B. Hemocyanin
- C. **Melanin** ✓

Cephalopod ink gets its color from melanin, the same family of pigment that colors human skin and hair.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4052311/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.ink.melanin` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The ink review says the ink gland makes the dark pigment melanin, while the funnel organ adds mucus.</sub>

### dd-0021 · medium · Field Guide: ink · unverified

**The brown art pigment 'sepia', used from Greco-Roman times into the 1800s, came from the ink of which animal?**

- A. **Cuttlefish** ✓
- B. Squid
- C. Octopus

Sepia is the name for ink from cuttlefish of the genus Sepia. Artists and writers prized it for its rich brown tones and permanence.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4052311/) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/common-cuttlefish)

Fact id: `cephalopods.ink.sepia-pigment` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The ink review says sepia, the name for the ink from Sepia, was used from Greco-Roman times through the 19th century as an ink and pigment. A Monterey Bay Aquarium result says the common cuttlefish's dark-brown ink, called sepia, was once widely used for writing and drawing.</sub>

### dd-0022 · hard · Field Guide: ink · unverified

**Lab tests on European cuttlefish ink found that, apart from its pigment, it contains which cell-damaging enzyme?**

- A. Lactase
- B. **Tyrosinase** ✓
- C. Amylase

Even with the melanin removed, cuttlefish ink harmed cells in lab tests. The active ingredient turned out to be the enzyme tyrosinase.

Source: [PubMed (NIH)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12901867/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4052311/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.ink.tyrosinase` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A PubMed study of Sepia officinalis found melanin-free ink contains a heat-labile protein toxic to several cell lines and identified it as tyrosinase.</sub>

### dd-0023 · medium · Field Guide: mimic · unverified

**The mimic octopus is known for impersonating which venomous fish?**

- A. Pufferfish
- B. Stonefish
- C. **Lionfish** ✓

By spreading its striped arms, the mimic octopus can look like a lionfish, one of several animals it imitates along with flatfish and sea snakes.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/how-octopuses-and-squids-change-color) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10755188/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.mimic-octopus.lionfish` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian says the mimic octopus has been known to impersonate more than 15 marine species, including flounders, lionfish and sea snakes.</sub>

### dd-0024 · hard · Field Guide: mimic · unverified

**Field researchers saw the mimic octopus imitate a banded sea snake in response to attacks by which animal?**

- A. **Territorial damselfish** ✓
- B. Reef sharks
- C. Sea eagles

Researchers noted the mimic octopus's sea snake act appeared when territorial damselfish attacked, as if picking a disguise for that audience.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1088805/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10755188/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.mimic-octopus.sea-snake-damselfish` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: The 2001 dynamic mimicry study reports sea snake mimicry was only observed in response to attacks by territorial damselfish. A later review suggests mimicry may be an automatic threat response, so the question only states what was observed.</sub>

### dd-0025 · medium · Field Guide: mimic · unverified

**How does the mimic octopus impersonate a flounder?**

- A. **It flattens its body and swims low with its arms trailing behind** ✓
- B. It puffs up into a ball and drifts
- C. It buries itself and leaves one arm sticking up

To pass as a flatfish, the mimic octopus flattens out, draws all its arms behind it and glides just above the seafloor.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10755188/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1088805/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.mimic-octopus.flounder-method` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A review describes the flounder disguise as flattening the body, draping all arms behind the body, and swimming low to the substrate.</sub>

### dd-0026 · hard · unverified

**Unlike many octopuses that hide in reefs, where does the mimic octopus forage?**

- A. Inside deep-sea vents
- B. **Out on open sand in daylight** ✓
- C. Only in caves at night

The mimic octopus hunts on exposed sand by day, in full view of predatory fish, which may be why its impersonations pay off.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1088805/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.mimic-octopus.daylight-sand` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The dynamic mimicry study says the mimic octopus emerges during daylight to forage on sand substrates in full view of pelagic fish predators.</sub>

### dd-0027 · hard · unverified

**Divers in Indonesia filmed which fish shadowing a mimic octopus while matching its banded pattern?**

- A. A moray eel
- B. A clownfish
- C. **A jawfish** ✓

A black-marble jawfish was seen sticking close to a mimic octopus and copying its colors, an animal mimicking the master mimic.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/blog-archive/science/fish-mimicking-ocotopus-mimicking-fish/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.mimic-octopus.jawfish-follower` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: The Australian Museum blog post 'Fish mimicking octopus mimicking fish!' describes a fish mimicking the mimic octopus. Identity as a black-marble jawfish and the Indonesia location come from my background knowledge of the report and must be checked on the page.</sub>

### dd-0028 · easy · Field Guide: den · unverified

**The veined octopus is known for carrying what to use as a portable shelter?**

- A. Kelp fronds
- B. Sea urchin spines
- C. **Halves of coconut shells** ✓

Veined octopuses gather split coconut shells, carry them across the seabed and then reassemble the halves and climb inside.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/veined-octopus-octopus-marginatus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.veined-octopus.coconut-shelter` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NHM and an ncbi review describe veined octopuses (Amphioctopus marginatus) gathering coconut halves, carrying them under their arms and reassembling them as a shelter.</sub>

### dd-0029 · medium · Field Guide: den · unverified

**Carrying coconut shells forces the veined octopus into a slow, awkward 'stilt walk.' What is the likely cost?**

- A. It cannot change color
- B. **It is more exposed to predators** ✓
- C. It cannot breathe while walking

The awkward stilt walk leaves the octopus more vulnerable, but it seems to accept the short-term risk for a ready-made shelter later.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/octopuses-keep-surprising-us-here-are-eight-examples-how.html) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5824970/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.veined-octopus.stilt-walk-risk` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NHM says the shell-carrying stilt walk makes the octopuses more vulnerable to predators but they seem willing to accept the short-term risk for future protection.</sub>

### dd-0030 · hard · Field Guide: den · review

**A 2025 study off northwest Spain found juvenile common octopuses on sandy seafloor using what as shelters?**

- A. **Empty clam-type shells** ✓
- B. Living sea anemones
- C. Floating seaweed mats

Where sand offers no rocky crevices, young common octopuses moved into empty bivalve shells such as clam shells as makeshift dens.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12081824/) · second source: [PubMed (NIH)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40386488/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.common-octopus.juvenile-bivalve-shelters` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A 2025 study from NW Spain reports juvenile Octopus vulgaris in sandy substrates using empty bivalve shells as shelters (nine juveniles found inhabiting bivalve shells).</sub>

### dd-0031 · medium · review

**What produces the tetrodotoxin in a blue-ringed octopus's bite?**

- A. **Symbiotic bacteria** ✓
- B. Its ink gland
- C. Pigment cells in its blue rings

The blue-ringed octopus gets its tetrodotoxin from bacteria living on and in its body, including its salivary glands.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/blue-ringed-octopus-small-vibrant-deadly.html) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6563023/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.blue-ringed.ttx-bacteria` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: NHM says symbiotic bacteria in the salivary glands make tetrodotoxin and that the octopus acquires it from bacteria living on and in its body. Note: which bacteria and how they are acquired is still researched.</sub>

### dd-0032 · medium · unverified

**How does the tetrodotoxin of a blue-ringed octopus endanger a person?**

- A. **It blocks nerve signals to muscles and can stop breathing** ✓
- B. It thins the blood so wounds keep bleeding
- C. It triggers a severe allergic rash

The toxin interferes with nerve signals to muscles. In a serious bite this can paralyze the muscles used for breathing.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/blue-ringed-octopus-small-vibrant-deadly.html)

Fact id: `cephalopods.blue-ringed.ttx-paralysis` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM says tetrodotoxin interferes with nerve signaling to muscles, which can paralyze breathing.</sub>

### dd-0033 · medium · review

**How does the greater blue-ringed octopus get its venom into shelled prey such as crabs?**

- A. It squirts ink into the gills
- B. **It dribbles saliva into a wound it makes in the shell** ✓
- C. It stings with spines on its arm tips

The octopus breaches the prey's shell and lets toxic saliva seep into the opening, subduing the animal.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/greater_blue_ringed_octopus1)

Fact id: `cephalopods.blue-ringed.saliva-into-shell` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific says the venom is used to subdue prey, with saliva dribbled into a wound the octopus has made in its prey's shell.</sub>

### dd-0034 · medium · unverified

**About how many chambers does a mature chambered nautilus shell have?**

- A. About 3
- B. **About 30** ✓
- C. About 300

A mature shell has around 30 chambers. The nautilus adds a new one as it grows and seals off the old ones behind it.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.chamber-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum (search summary) says the shell is divided into more than 30 chambers connected by a siphuncle.</sub>

### dd-0035 · medium · unverified

**Which part of its shell does a living chambered nautilus occupy?**

- A. **Only the outermost chamber** ✓
- B. Only the innermost chamber
- C. All the chambers at once

The animal lives in the newest, outermost chamber. The older chambers behind it hold gas and liquid.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/chambered_nautilus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.outer-chamber` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary: the animal occupies only the outermost chamber, which it can close for protection.</sub>

### dd-0036 · medium · unverified

**How does a chambered nautilus adjust its buoyancy?**

- A. By gulping air at the surface
- B. By shedding pieces of its shell
- C. **By changing the ratio of gas to liquid in its shell chambers** ✓

Shifting the mix of gas and liquid in its chambers changes the nautilus's weight, much like a submarine filling or emptying its dive tanks.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/chambered_nautilus) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.buoyancy-gas-liquid` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary (Aquarium of the Pacific, Australian Museum, NOAA): the siphuncle lets the nautilus change the ratio of liquid to gas, modifying its weight, like a submarine's dive tanks.</sub>

### dd-0037 · hard · unverified

**What is the tube of living tissue that links the chambers of a nautilus shell called?**

- A. The radula
- B. **The siphuncle** ✓
- C. The mantle

The siphuncle threads through every chamber of the shell and moves fluid in and out of them.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/chambered-nautilus)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.siphuncle` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum says the chambers are connected via a tube containing living tissue known as a siphuncle.</sub>

### dd-0038 · hard · unverified

**Experiments found chambered nautilus shells implode at roughly what depth?**

- A. 75 to 90 meters
- B. **750 to 900 meters** ✓
- C. 7,500 to 9,000 meters

The gas-filled chambers cannot withstand extreme pressure, and shells implode at roughly 750 to 900 meters, which limits how deep a nautilus can go.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.implosion-depth` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search summary: experiments on living nautilus found shells implode at depths between 750 and 900 m.</sub>

**How do the roughly 90 tentacles of a chambered nautilus differ from an octopus's arms?**

- A. They end in sharp hooks
- B. They are filled with venom
- C. **They have no suckers** ✓

A nautilus has dozens more tentacles than an octopus has arms, but none of them carry suckers.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.suckerless-tentacles` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary (Australian Museum): more than 90 suckerless tentacles protrude from the shell.</sub>

- B. A web of mucus
- C. **A leathery hood** ✓

When it pulls back inside, the nautilus seals the opening with a tough, leathery hood.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/chambered-nautilus/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.nautilus.leathery-hood` · risk: none

- A. Not listed
- B. **Threatened** ✓
- C. Extinct in the wild

NOAA Fisheries lists the chambered nautilus as threatened, largely because the shells are heavily harvested for the international shell trade.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/chambered-nautilus) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://media.fisheries.noaa.gov/dam-migration/final_nautilus_status_review_508_compliant.pdf)

**Which cephalopod has a W-shaped pupil?**

- A. Octopus
- B. Squid
- C. **Cuttlefish** ✓

Octopus pupils are rectangular, squid pupils are round, and cuttlefish pupils curve into a distinctive W.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/octopus-eyes)

Fact id: `cephalopods.cuttlefish.w-pupil` · risk: none

### dd-0043 · medium · unverified

**What is a cuttlefish's 'cuttlebone'?**

- A. A true bone like a fish's backbone
- B. A hardened beak
- C. **An internal chambered shell used to control buoyancy** ✓

The cuttlebone is an internal, chambered shell of calcium carbonate. Moving liquid in and out of it lets the cuttlefish rise or sink.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7407613/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4471886/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.cuttlefish.cuttlebone-buoyancy` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary: cuttlebones are internal chambered calcium-carbonate structures, not true bones, used for buoyancy by pumping liquid in and out of the chambers.</sub>

### dd-0044 · hard · review

**A study of newborn cuttlefish found that the share of hatchlings 'floating' was greatest under which condition?**

- A. **Starvation** ✓
- B. Overfeeding
- C. Bright light

Cuttlebone shape and weight affect buoyancy, and newborn cuttlefish that went without food were the most likely to end up floating.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7407613/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.cuttlefish.hatchlings-float-starved` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: The cuttlefish buoyancy study linked cuttlebone weight-to-area ratio to buoyancy control in newborns and found the proportion of floating newborns was greatest under starvation.</sub>

### dd-0045 · medium · unverified

**What makes the light in the Hawaiian bobtail squid's light organ?**

- A. **Glowing bacteria** ✓
- B. Its own glowing cells
- C. Glowing algae it eats

The squid houses Vibrio fischeri bacteria in its light organ, and they produce the glow in exchange for food and shelter.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3187790/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC106330/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.bobtail-squid.vibrio-fischeri` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC papers describe Vibrio fischeri as the only bacterium that colonizes the light organ of Euprymna scolopes and the source of its luminescence.</sub>

### dd-0046 · medium · unverified

**What does the Hawaiian bobtail squid use its bacterial light for at night?**

- A. To attract mates
- B. To lure prey closer
- C. **To match moonlight from above and hide its silhouette** ✓

By glowing downward to match moonlight and starlight, the squid erases the shadow it would cast to predators below.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4000693/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13200733/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.bobtail-squid.counterillumination` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A PMC paper describes the squid using the symbionts' light as counterillumination camouflage that matches downwelling moonlight and starlight; another notes the squid is nocturnal.</sub>

### dd-0047 · hard · unverified

**Each morning, roughly what share of its light-organ bacteria does the Hawaiian bobtail squid expel?**

- A. About 5%
- B. About 50%
- C. **About 95%** ✓

At dawn the squid vents about 95% of its glowing bacteria. The rest regrow during the day, ready to light up again by night.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3187790/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5216023/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.bobtail-squid.morning-expulsion` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: A PMC paper says each morning the squid expels about 95% of its bacterial population along with host cells; the remaining bacteria regrow on a daily cycle.</sub>

### dd-0048 · hard · unverified

**When does a Hawaiian bobtail squid acquire the bacteria for its light organ?**

- A. They are passed inside the egg
- B. **Soon after hatching, from seawater** ✓
- C. Only once it reaches adulthood

Hatchlings start out without their glowing partners and pick up Vibrio fischeri from the surrounding seawater soon after hatching.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3187790/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC106330/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.bobtail-squid.colonized-after-hatching` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A PMC paper says the light organ's crypts are the site of colonization and the squid is colonized soon after hatching. The 'from seawater' detail is from background knowledge; verify.</sub>

### dd-0049 · hard · unverified

**If a Hawaiian bobtail squid's bacteria cannot glow, what happens to them over time?**

- A. **The squid's light organ gradually loses them** ✓
- B. They switch to making ink
- C. They spread through the squid's body

Non-glowing mutant bacteria can colonize at first but are not kept for long, as if the squid only keeps partners that pay their way in light.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13200733/)

Fact id: `cephalopods.bobtail-squid.dark-symbiont-rejected` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search summary: dark mutants can colonize at 24 hours but show defects by 48 hours and are undetectable after a few weeks; a symbiont that stops luminescing is rejected by the host.</sub>

- C. **Two long feeding tentacles** ✓

Squid and cuttlefish have eight arms plus two longer tentacles that shoot out to snag prey. Octopuses have arms only.

### dd-0135 · easy · unverified

**What protects a clownfish from the stings of its host anemone?**

- A. **A layer of mucus on its skin** ✓
- B. Extra-thick armored scales
- C. Venom-proof blood

A clownfish usually gets used to its anemone by gently touching the tentacles over hours or days. Its coat of mucus then protects it from the stings.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/clownfish) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/discover-fish/species-profiles/clown-anemonefish/)

### dd-0136 · medium · unverified

**If the breeding female in a clownfish group dies, what usually happens?**

- A. The group abandons its anemone
- B. **The breeding male becomes a female** ✓
- C. A newcomer female moves in

Clownfish all start life as males. When the female is lost, the breeding male changes sex and grows, and the next-largest male becomes the new breeding male.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/clown_anemonefish) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/teachers/learning/sleek-geeks-videos/sex-change-in-clownfish/)

Fact id: `fish.clownfish.protandry` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: clownfish are protandrous; if the female dies the breeding male changes to female and the next largest male becomes breeding male. Australian Museum page on clownfish sex change covers the same.</sub>

### dd-0137 · medium · unverified

**In clown anemonefish, which parent does most of the work of guarding and tending the eggs?**

- A. The female
- B. Neither; the eggs are left alone
- C. **The male** ✓

Clown anemonefish lay their eggs on a hard surface right beside the anemone, and the male does most of the fanning and guarding until they hatch.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/discover-fish/species-profiles/clown-anemonefish/)

Fact id: `fish.clown-anemonefish.male-egg-care` · risk: species-specific

Verifier must confirm on the page.</sub>

### dd-0138 · medium · unverified

**In the clownfish and sea anemone partnership, what does the clownfish do for the anemone?**

- A. **Drives off fish that nibble anemones** ✓
- B. Carries the anemone to new rocks
- C. Stings prey for the anemone

The anemone shelters the clownfish, and in return the clownfish chases away fish such as butterflyfish that would eat the anemone's tentacles.

Source: [Natural History Museum (London)](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/mutualism-examples-of-species-that-work-together.html)

### dd-0139 · medium · unverified

**Some parrotfish wrap themselves in a mucus cocoon at night. What is its main known job?**

- A. Keeping their bodies warm
- B. **Shielding them from blood-sucking parasites** ✓
- C. Storing food for the morning

Like a mosquito net, the cocoon protects a sleeping parrotfish from parasites such as blood-sucking isopods that are active after dark.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/parrotfish-mucus-cocoon) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/aquarium-faq-do-fishes-sleep)

Fact id: `fish.parrotfish.cocoon-purpose` · risk: none

### dd-0140 · hard · unverified

**Roughly how much of a parrotfish's daily energy goes into making its nightly mucus cocoon?**

- A. About 25 percent
- B. About 50 percent
- C. **About 2.5 percent** ✓

Researchers estimate the cocoon costs only about 2.5 percent of the fish's daily energy budget, a modest price for a night free of parasites.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/parrotfish-mucus-cocoon)

Fact id: `fish.parrotfish.cocoon-energy-cost` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian parrotfish cocoon page estimates the nightly cocoon costs about 2.5 percent of the fish's daily energy budget.</sub>

**What does a sleeping parrotfish do if something disturbs its mucus cocoon?**

- A. **Wakes up and dashes away** ✓
- B. Squirts a cloud of ink
- C. Plays dead on the bottom

The cocoon may double as an alarm: if it is disturbed, the parrotfish immediately wakes and flees.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/southflorida/habitats/corals/reef-communities/)

### dd-0142 · easy · unverified

**How do parrotfish help make the white sand on many tropical beaches?**

- A. They crush seashells with their fins
- B. **They grind up reef rock and excrete it** ✓
- C. They dig sand up from the deep sea

Parrotfish bite into coral and rock while feeding, grind the hard calcium carbonate in their guts, and pass it out as fine white sand.

Source: [Natural History Museum (London)](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/parrotfish-and-sand.html) · second source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/sand.html)

Fact id: `fish.parrotfish.sand-production` · risk: none

### dd-0143 · medium · unverified

**What happens when the front teeth of a parrotfish wear down?**

- A. The fish switches to soft food
- B. The fish can no longer feed
- C. **A new row of teeth moves in to replace them** ✓

A parrotfish's beak is built from many rows of teeth. As the front ones wear away from scraping rock, the next row moves up to take over.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/tough-teeth-and-parrotfish-poop)

Fact id: `fish.parrotfish.tooth-replacement` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 'Tough Teeth and Parrotfish Poop': parrotfish have roughly 1,000 teeth in 15 rows; when front teeth wear out another row takes over.</sub>

### dd-0144 · easy · unverified

**How does parrotfish feeding help coral reefs?**

- A. **It keeps algae from overgrowing coral** ✓
- B. It spreads coral larvae between reefs
- C. It adds oxygen to the water

Parrotfish graze algae and other tiny growth off reef surfaces. That grazing helps stop algae from crowding out corals for light and space.

Source: [Natural History Museum (London)](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/parrotfish-and-sand.html)

Fact id: `fish.parrotfish.algae-grazing` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM parrotfish page: parrotfish mostly feed on algae, which stops it growing too much and outcompeting coral for resources such as light.</sub>

Stoplight parrotfish females can transform into large, brightly colored 'terminal phase' males, the reverse of the clownfish pattern.

### dd-0146 · medium · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**How does the bluestreak cleaner wrasse, also called the common cleanerfish, attract larger fish to its cleaning station?**

- A. It flashes a glowing light
- B. It releases a sweet-smelling scent
- C. **It swims with a bobbing, dancing motion** ✓

From a cave or overhang that serves as its station, the cleanerfish swims in a bobbing dance that draws in bigger fish, then picks off and eats their parasites.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/common-cleanerfish-labroides-dimidiatus/)

Fact id: `fish.cleaner-wrasse.bobbing-display` · risk: species-specific

### dd-0147 · hard · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**The bluestreak cleaner wrasse gets most of its food from which parasites?**

- A. **Gnathiid isopods, tiny crustaceans** ✓
- B. Tapeworms from fish intestines
- C. Leeches clinging to scales

Gnathiid isopods are tiny crustaceans that feed on fish blood and body fluids. Cleaner wrasses pluck them off the fish that visit their stations.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9213755/)

Fact id: `fish.bluestreak-cleaner-wrasse.gnathiid-diet` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed paper on cleaning services: the bluestreak cleaner wrasse diet is mostly gnathiid isopods, body-fluid-feeding ectoparasites removed from clients.</sub>

**Roughly how many parasites can a single bluestreak cleaner wrasse eat in a day?**

- A. About 10
- B. **More than 1,000** ✓
- C. About 100

One study estimated that a single wrasse has more than 2,000 cleaning visits and eats over 1,000 parasites a day, a very busy reef doctor.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9213755/)

Fact id: `fish.bluestreak-cleaner-wrasse.parasites-per-day` · risk: numeric

- A. To lay their eggs in safety
- B. To hunt the small fish gathered there
- C. **To have parasites and dead tissue removed** ✓

At cleaning stations, small cleaner fish such as gobies and wrasses pick parasites and dead tissue from a Nassau grouper's gills and body, and the big predator lets them work.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/discover-fish/species-profiles/nassau-grouper/)

- C. No measurable change occurred

In removal experiments, reefs without cleaner wrasses ended up with less healthy, less diverse fish communities.

Clients hold still with mouths open and fins spread so the bluehead can reach their gills and fins to pick off parasites.

### dd-0154 · medium · unverified

**How do Atlantic bluefin tuna keep parts of their body warmer than the surrounding water?**

- A. A thick layer of blubber
- B. **A blood vessel network that holds in heat** ✓
- C. Basking at the surface in the sun

A countercurrent exchanger, a special arrangement of blood vessels, keeps muscle heat from being lost at the gills, so bluefin run warmer than the sea.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/fun-facts-about-atlantic-tunas) · second source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6340463/)

Fact id: `fish.atlantic-bluefin-tuna.countercurrent-heat` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Fisheries Atlantic tunas facts: bluefin keep body temperature warmer than water thanks to a specialized blood vessel structure called a countercurrent exchanger. Phylotranscriptomic paper: Thunnus tunas keep locomotor muscle, viscera, brain and eyes warm.</sub>

### dd-0155 · hard · unverified

**Pacific bluefin tuna keep their swimming muscles warm. Which organ works at about the temperature of the surrounding sea?**

- A. The brain
- B. The eyes
- C. **The heart** ✓

Bluefin warm their muscles, gut, brain and eyes, but the heart runs at sea temperature, so it has to keep beating well even in cold water.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7499911/) · second source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6340463/)

Fact id: `fish.pacific-bluefin-tuna.cold-heart` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Search summary: in bluefin tunas the heart operates at ambient temperature while oxidative muscle, viscera, brain and eyes are warmed (phylotranscriptomic paper). Attributed the heart point to the Pacific bluefin cardiac transcriptomics paper; verifier should confirm which paper states it.</sub>

### dd-0156 · medium · unverified

**Why does an albacore tuna swim with its mouth open?**

- A. **To push water over its gills to breathe** ✓
- B. To scoop up plankton as it swims
- C. To cool its warm muscles

Like other tunas, albacore can't pump enough water over their gills while still, so they swim with mouths open to keep water flowing through.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/pacific-albacore-tuna) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/yellowfin-tuna)

Fact id: `fish.albacore.ram-ventilation` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA albacore page: albacore lack structures needed to pump oxygen-rich water over gills, so they keep their mouths open while moving. MBA yellowfin page describes water passing over gills as the fish moves.</sub>

**When do salmon learn the smell of the home stream they return to as adults?**

- A. **As juveniles migrating downstream** ✓
- B. During their final year at sea
- C. From older salmon guiding them

Young salmon memorize the scent of their home stream on the way to the sea, then use that odor memory years later to find it again.

### dd-0160 · hard · unverified

The strongest response was in a forebrain region thought to match the mammalian hippocampus, a center for memory, which fits the idea that homing is learned.

- A. Into its swim bladder

Throat muscles pump water or air into a sac branching from the stomach, swelling the puffer into a ball that's hard for predators to swallow.

Source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/33313/noaa_33313_DS1.pdf) · second source: [Natural History Museum (London)](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/pufferfish-underwater-balloon-of-death.html)

### dd-0170 · medium · rejected

**Where does the tetrodotoxin in marine pufferfish originally come from?**

- A. Venom glands the fish grows at birth
- B. Jellyfish that the puffer eats
- C. **Bacteria, passed up the food chain** ✓

Pufferfish don't make the toxin themselves. It is produced by marine bacteria and builds up in puffers through the food they eat.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2525488/) · second source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3658506/)

Fact id: `fish.pufferfish.ttx-bacterial-origin` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: TTX review: TTX is originally produced by marine bacteria, and pufferfish are intoxicated through the food chain that starts with the bacteria.</sub>

### dd-0171 · medium · rejected

**How does tetrodotoxin, the pufferfish poison, harm the body?**

- A. **It blocks nerve signals and can stop breathing** ✓
- B. It thins the blood and causes bleeding
- C. It slowly destroys the liver over weeks

Tetrodotoxin blocks sodium channels that nerves and muscles need to fire, which can lead to paralysis of the breathing muscles.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3658506/)

Fact id: `fish.pufferfish.ttx-mechanism` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: TTX poisoning paper: TTX inhibits nerve and muscle conduction by selectively blocking sodium channels, resulting in respiratory paralysis that causes death.</sub>

### dd-0172 · hard · unverified

**In marine pufferfish, which body parts usually hold the most tetrodotoxin?**

- A. The fins and tail
- B. **The liver and ovaries** ✓
- C. The gills and blood

In marine puffers the toxin generally concentrates in the liver and ovaries. In freshwater species the skin tends to be the most toxic.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2525488/)

Fact id: `fish.marine-pufferfish.ttx-organs` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: TTX distribution review: in marine pufferfish toxicity is generally high in liver and ovary, whereas in freshwater species toxicity is higher in the skin.</sub>

- C. Its long tongue drags prey inward

After the outer jaws grab prey, toothed jaws in the throat lunge forward, seize it and haul it back for swallowing, all in a fraction of a second.

Source: [UC Davis](https://www.ucdavis.edu/news/alien-jaws-help-moray-eels-feed) · second source: [UC Davis](https://www.ucdavis.edu/news/eels-really-put-bite-2nd-set-jaws-lunges-forward-carries-food-back)

### dd-0176 · medium · unverified

**In the Red Sea, how do groupers recruit giant moray eels to hunt with them?**

- A. By nudging the eel with their tails
- B. By dropping food at the eel's crevice
- C. **By shaking their heads rapidly in front of the eel** ✓

Groupers visit a resting moray and shake their heads several times a second right in front of it. Often the eel then leaves its crevice to hunt together.

Source: [PLOS Biology (peer-reviewed)](https://journals.plos.org/plosbiology/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pbio.0040431) · second source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC1750927/)

Fact id: `fish.red-sea-grouper.head-shake-signal` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: PLOS Biology study: groupers visit morays and shake the head at high frequency (3-6 shakes per second) a few centimetres from the moray's head; morays left crevices in 58% of cases.</sub>

### dd-0177 · medium · unverified

**Why do groupers and giant moray eels make effective hunting partners?**

- A. **The grouper hunts open water; the eel probes holes** ✓
- B. The eel's venom stuns prey for the grouper
- C. They take turns sleeping and keeping watch

Prey fleeing the grouper into a crack meets the moray, and prey fleeing the moray into open water meets the grouper. There is nowhere safe to hide.

Source: [PLOS Biology (peer-reviewed)](https://journals.plos.org/plosbiology/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pbio.0040431)

Fact id: `fish.grouper-moray.complementary-hunting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PLOS Biology study: groupers hunt in open water while morays corner prey in holes, so coordinated hunts confront prey with a multipredator attack.</sub>

### dd-0178 · hard · unverified

**In the Red Sea study of groupers and giant morays, when did groupers recruit morays most often?**

- A. Right after the groupers had eaten
- B. **When the groupers were hungry** ✓
- C. Only during the full moon

Groupers signaled for moray partners more often when hungry, suggesting the teamwork is a deliberate strategy for getting a meal.

Source: [PLOS Biology (peer-reviewed)](https://journals.plos.org/plosbiology/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pbio.0040431)

Fact id: `fish.red-sea-grouper.hunger-recruitment` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PLOS Biology study: groupers showed recruitment (head-shaking) more often when hungry.</sub>

By hanging head-down over the crevice where prey hides, a grouper points it out to a partner that can reach inside.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8441117/) · second source: [University of Cambridge](https://www.cam.ac.uk/research/news/memory-like-a-fish)

Male yellowhead jawfish brood the eggs in their mouths until they hatch, keeping them safe from egg-eating fish.

Source: [Georgia Aquarium](https://www.georgiaaquarium.org/animal/yellowhead-jawfish/)

Fact id: `fish.yellowhead-jawfish.mouthbrooding` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Georgia Aquarium yellowhead jawfish page; expected to describe males incubating eggs in their mouths. NOTE: the search snippet did not show this specific claim (search budget ran out before a targeted search); the URL did appear in a search result for this topic. Verifier must confirm on the page.</sub>

## sharks-rays (25)

### dd-0245 · easy · unverified

**Which of these is the biggest fish in the ocean?**

- A. **Whale shark** ✓
- B. Basking shark
- C. Giant manta ray

Despite its name, the whale shark is a fish, not a whale, and it is the biggest fish in the sea. Some estimates put it at over 12 meters long.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/discover-fish/species-profiles/whale-shark/)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.whale-shark.largest-fish` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's shark facts page says whale sharks are the biggest fish in the ocean, growing to 12.2 m and up to 40 tons by some estimates.</sub>

### dd-0246 · easy · unverified

**How do researchers tell individual whale sharks apart?**

- A. By the calls they make
- B. **By their unique spot patterns** ✓
- C. By counting their teeth

No two whale sharks share the same pattern of spots and stripes, so photos of a shark's skin work much like a fingerprint for identifying it.

Source: [Georgia Aquarium](https://www.georgiaaquarium.org/animal/whale-shark/) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.whale-shark.spot-pattern-id` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Georgia Aquarium says each whale shark's spot-and-stripe pattern is unique, like a human fingerprint, and is used to tell individuals apart. NOAA's shark facts page says the same.</sub>

### dd-0247 · hard · unverified

**To estimate the ages of Greenland sharks, a 2016 study radiocarbon-dated which part of their bodies?**

- A. Their vertebrae
- B. Their teeth
- C. **The lenses of their eyes** ✓

The center of the eye lens forms before birth and never changes afterward, so dating its carbon reveals when the shark was born.

Source: [University of Copenhagen](https://researchprofiles.ku.dk/en/publications/eye-lens-radiocarbon-reveals-centuries-of-longevity-in-the-greenl/)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.greenland-shark.age-method-eye-lens` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The 2016 study, titled 'Eye lens radiocarbon reveals centuries of longevity in the Greenland shark', dated eye lens tissue from 28 females; the lens nucleus does not change after birth.</sub>

### dd-0248 · medium · unverified

**A 2016 study estimated that the oldest Greenland shark it examined was at least how old?**

- A. About 72 years
- B. **At least 272 years** ✓
- C. At least 1,200 years

The oldest shark in the study was estimated at 272 to 512 years old, with about 390 years as the most likely figure. That makes Greenland sharks extraordinarily long-lived.

Source: [University of Copenhagen](https://researchprofiles.ku.dk/en/publications/eye-lens-radiocarbon-reveals-centuries-of-longevity-in-the-greenl/) · second source: [University of Copenhagen](https://researchprofiles.ku.dk/en/publications/the-greenland-shark-isomniosus-microcephalusi-diet-tracking-and-r/)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.greenland-shark.oldest-age-estimate` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search results on the 2016 eye-lens study said the oldest of 28 female sharks was estimated at 272 to 512 years, with a best estimate of about 390-392 years; 512 is an upper bound, not a confirmed age.</sub>

### dd-0249 · medium · unverified

**What do the tiny pores on a shark's snout, called ampullae of Lorenzini, detect?**

- A. **Weak electric fields** ✓
- B. Changes in light
- C. Odors in the water

These jelly-filled pores sense the faint electric fields made by other animals' muscles, letting sharks find prey hidden in the sand.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/discover-fish/sharks/shark-biology/) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.ampullae.detect-electric-fields` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results (Florida Museum and NOAA shark pages) describe the ampullae of Lorenzini as electroreceptors that sense weak electric fields, mainly used to locate hidden prey such as stingrays buried in sand.</sub>

### dd-0250 · hard · unverified

**Each pore of a shark's electric-sensing ampullae of Lorenzini leads into a canal filled with what?**

- A. Air
- B. **A jelly-like gel** ✓
- C. Blood

Each pore opens to the water and leads through a gel-filled canal to a sensory sac under the skin, which is wired to the nervous system.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12782034/)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.ampullae.gel-filled-canals` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The search summary described each ampulla as a pore open to the water connected to a gel-filled canal ending in a sensory sac under the skin; the paper's title refers to the 'jelly' of the ampullae.</sub>

### dd-0251 · medium · unverified

- A. It holds air that keeps the shark afloat
- B. It is used to dig burrows
- C. **It spreads electric-sensing pores over a wider area** ✓

A hammerhead's broad head spreads its electric-sensing pores across a wide area, letting it sweep more seafloor for prey buried in the sand.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/scalloped-hammerhead-sphyrna-lewini/)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.hammerhead.head-electroreceptors` · risk: species-specific

Fact id: `sharks-rays.sawfish.electric-field-detection` · risk: species-specific

### dd-0253 · medium · unverified

**About how long is a spiny dogfish's pregnancy?**

- A. About 3 months
- B. About 9 months
- C. **18 to 24 months** ✓

Spiny dogfish carry their young for 18 to 24 months, one of the longest pregnancies known in any animal with a backbone. They give birth to live pups.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/atlantic-spiny-dogfish) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/pacific-spiny-dogfish)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.spiny-dogfish.gestation-length` · risk: numeric

### dd-0254 · medium · unverified

**Which shark was shown in 2018 to digest seagrass and absorb its nutrients?**

- A. Lemon shark
- B. **Bonnethead** ✓
- C. Nurse shark

A 2018 study found bonnetheads don't just swallow seagrass by accident: they digest it and absorb its nutrients, alongside crabs, shrimp and fish.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6158537/) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/bonnethead-shark)

### dd-0255 · easy · unverified

**What are sharks' skeletons made of?**

- A. Bone
- B. Chitin
- C. **Cartilage** ✓

Sharks have no bones. Their skeletons are made of cartilage, the same flexible tissue found in your nose and ears, which is much lighter than bone.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.shark.skeleton-cartilage` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's shark facts page says sharks have no bones; their skeletons are cartilage, which is much lighter than true bone.</sub>

### dd-0256 · medium · unverified

**Sharks have no gas-filled swim bladder. What helps keep them buoyant instead?**

- A. Hollow, air-filled bones
- B. Thick layers of blubber
- C. **A large liver full of oil** ✓

Along with a lightweight cartilage skeleton, sharks have large livers full of low-density oils that help them stay afloat.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.shark.liver-oil-buoyancy` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says sharks' cartilage skeletons are lighter than bone and their large livers are full of low-density oils, both of which help them be buoyant.</sub>

### dd-0257 · hard · unverified

**Shark skeletons are made of cartilage, yet they can still become fossils. Why?**

- A. **Parts of their cartilage are hardened with calcium salts** ✓
- B. Shark cartilage turns into bone after death
- C. Sharks swallow stones that harden their skeletons

Much of a shark's cartilage is reinforced with calcium salts, and this hardened cartilage can fossilize. Teeth, though, are by far the most common shark fossils.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.shark.calcified-cartilage-fossils` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's shark facts page says that as most sharks age they deposit calcium salts in their skeletal cartilage to strengthen it, which is why their skeletons can fossilize.</sub>

- A. **It is covered in tiny tooth-like scales** ✓
- B. It is coated with salt crystals
- C. It is lined with stiff bristles

Shark skin is covered in placoid scales, also called dermal denticles: tiny structures built much like teeth.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

Fact id: `sharks-rays.shark.dermal-denticles` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says shark skin feels like sandpaper because it is made up of tiny teeth-like structures called placoid scales, or dermal denticles.</sub>

- C. It rapidly changes color

Turning a shark upside down can put it into a trance-like state called tonic immobility, which researchers sometimes use to handle sharks calmly.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/12-shark-facts-may-surprise-you)

- A. About 2 million years ago
- B. About 66 million years ago

Fossil scales suggest shark relatives were swimming around 450 million years ago, long before dinosaurs, though whether the oldest scales are from true sharks is debated.

Decades of underwater recordings show humpback males also sing on their feeding grounds and while migrating between feeding and breeding areas.

### dd-0271 · easy · unverified

**In group bubble-net feeding, how do humpback whales finally catch the fish trapped by their bubbles?**

- A. **They lunge upward through the net** ✓
- B. They scoop them off the seafloor
- C. They herd them onto the beach

Curtains of rising bubbles bunch the fish together. The whales then push them toward the surface and lunge up through the circular net to engulf them.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/humpback-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.humpback-whale.bubble-net-lunge` · risk: none

- A. Their lower jaws
- B. Their tongues
- C. **Their long pectoral fins** ✓

NOAA notes humpbacks corral prey using bubbles, sounds, the seafloor and even their huge pectoral fins, a tactic researchers have called pectoral herding.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/humpback-whale) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6837203/)

- A. Shark teeth
- B. **Human fingernails** ✓
- C. Fish scales

Baleen is made of keratin, the same tough but flexible protein found in our fingernails and hair.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/right-whale-baleen) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/baleen-whales-filter-feeding-explained.html)

- A. An incisor
- B. A molar
- C. **A canine** ✓

Researchers traced the tusk to the maxillary bone, where mammal canine teeth form, showing it is a canine rather than an incisor.

Source: [Smithsonian Institution](https://www.si.edu/stories/understanding-narwhals-smile) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/narwhals-mysterious-unicorns-of-the-sea.html)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.narwhal.tusk-canine` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian results say the tusk is one of a pair of canine teeth; it was identified as a canine, not an incisor, because it originates in the maxillary bone. NHM London also calls it a canine tooth.</sub>

**Which narwhals usually grow the long, visible tusk?**

- A. **Males** ✓
- B. Females
- C. Young calves of both sexes

The tusk usually erupts in males. In females it rarely does; they typically keep two small teeth embedded in the upper jaw.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/why-tusk-real-life-unicorns-sea-and-tusks-make-them-famous) · second source: [Smithsonian National Museum of Natural History](https://naturalhistory.si.edu/exhibits/narwhal-revealing-arctic-legend)

**What makes the narwhal's tusk unusual compared with most teeth?**

- A. It grows from the lower jaw
- B. **Tiny channels link its nerve-filled core to the surface** ✓
- C. It is made of solid bone

Millions of tiny fluid-filled channels run from the tusk's surface to its nerve-filled core, which has led researchers to propose it works as a sensory organ.

Source: [Smithsonian Insider](https://insider.si.edu/2014/03/researchers-map-pathway-narwhals-sensitive-tusk-brain/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/why-tusk-real-life-unicorns-sea-and-tusks-make-them-famous)

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian results say the tusk's inner nerve system connects directly to the outside, with an estimated 10 million fluid-filled tubules from base to tip; the tusk originates in the upper (maxillary) bone.</sub>

Fact id: `marine-mammals.beluga.unfused-neck-vertebrae` · risk: species-specific

- C. **Make different facial expressions** ✓

The beluga's melon can change shape, which lets the whale make a range of different facial expressions.

Source: [Georgia Aquarium](https://www.georgiaaquarium.org/animal/beluga-whale/) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/animals/belugas)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.beluga.flexible-melon` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Beluga search results said the bulbous forehead, called a melon, is very flexible, allowing many different facial expressions. Verifier: confirm the Georgia Aquarium page (or Shedd's belugas page) carries this.</sub>

### dd-0280 · easy · unverified

**How does a gray whale usually feed?**

- A. Lunging through schools of herring
- B. **Rolling on its side to suck up seafloor mud** ✓
- C. Diving deep to hunt squid

A gray whale turns on its side and sucks in sediment from the seafloor, then strains out the mud and water through its baleen, keeping the small animals inside.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/gray-whale) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/eastern_pacific_gray_whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.gray-whale.bottom-feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA describes the gray whale turning on its side and scooping the top few inches of seafloor sediment; baleen holds back food while mud and water pass out.</sub>

### dd-0281 · medium · unverified

**In its Arctic feeding grounds, what is the gray whale's favorite food?**

- A. **Amphipods, small shrimp-like crustaceans** ✓
- B. Arctic cod
- C. Young ringed seals

Gray whales feeding in the Arctic mainly target amphipods, tiny crustaceans that live in the seafloor sediment the whales suck up.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/sentinels-change-gray-whales-arctic)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.gray-whale.amphipod-prey` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA Arctic feature describes amphipods as the gray whales' favorite food in the Arctic.</sub>

### dd-0282 · medium · unverified

**About how long do studies suggest a bowhead whale may live?**

- A. About 50 years
- B. **Over 200 years** ✓
- C. Over 1,000 years

New ways of estimating age suggest bowheads may live for more than 200 years, making them the longest-lived marine mammal known.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/bowhead.html) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/bowhead-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bowhead-whale.lifespan` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Service fact page on the longest-lived marine mammal is about the bowhead; NOAA's species page says studies suggest bowheads may live over 200 years.</sub>

### dd-0283 · medium · unverified

**What can a bowhead whale do with its huge, thick skull?**

- A. **Break through sea ice to breathe** ✓
- B. Stun fish with head-butts
- C. Dig clams from the seafloor

Bowheads live in icy Arctic waters, and their large, thick skulls let them break through sea ice to reach air.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/bowhead-whale) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/alaska/outreach-and-education/5-icebreakers-about-bowhead-whales)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bowhead-whale.ice-breaking` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA species page: the bowhead's large, thick skull allows it to break through 8-inch-thick sea ice; a Sea Grant article says it uses its head to break ice from below to breathe.</sub>

### dd-0284 · medium · unverified

**How many blowholes does a baleen whale, such as a humpback, have?**

- A. One
- B. **Two** ✓
- C. Three

Baleen whales have two blowholes side by side, while toothed whales such as dolphins and sperm whales have just one.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Today](https://oceantoday.noaa.gov/whaleanatomy/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/whales)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.whales.baleen-whale-blowhole-count` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA whale anatomy resource: Mysticeti (baleen whales) have two blowholes, while toothed whales (Odontoceti) have only one.</sub>

### dd-0285 · medium · unverified

**About how much of a sperm whale's total body length is taken up by its head?**

- A. A tenth
- B. Two-thirds
- C. **A third** ✓

A sperm whale's enormous, boxy head makes up about one-third of its body length.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/sperm-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.sperm-whale.head-length` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA sperm whale page says the head is about one-third of the total body length.</sub>

### dd-0286 · easy · unverified

**Remains found in sperm whale stomachs show they eat which deep-sea animal?**

- A. Walruses
- B. Sea turtles
- C. **Giant squid** ✓

Giant squid beaks have turned up in sperm whale stomachs, and sucker scars on some whales suggest that big squid fight back.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/giant-squid) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/giant-squid-vs-sperm-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.sperm-whale.giant-squid-prey` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian says giant squid beaks and other undigested remains have been found in sperm whale stomachs; stranded sperm whales carry large sucker scars.</sub>

### dd-0287 · easy · unverified

**The killer whale, or orca, is the largest member of which animal family?**

- A. **The oceanic dolphin family** ✓
- B. The sperm whale family
- C. The rorqual whale family

Despite its name, the orca belongs to Delphinidae, the oceanic dolphin family, and is its largest member.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/killer-whale) · second source: [NOAA Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/dolphin_porpoise.html)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.killer-whale.dolphin-family` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA: the killer whale is the largest member of the Delphinidae family, or dolphins; NOAA Ocean Service says the orca is actually the largest member of the dolphin family.</sub>

### dd-0288 · medium · unverified

**In the Pacific Northwest, what do 'resident' killer whales mainly eat?**

- A. Harbor seals
- B. Sea turtles
- C. **Salmon** ✓

Resident killer whales in the Pacific Northwest eat fish, mainly salmon, while the Bigg's (transient) killer whales in the same waters hunt marine mammals.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/killer-whale) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/new-research-reveals-full-diversity-killer-whales-two-species-come-view-pacific-coast)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.killer-whale.resident-diet` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA says one Pacific Northwest ecotype eats fish, mainly salmon; transient (Bigg's) killer whales prey on marine mammals, most often harbor seals in one study.</sub>

### dd-0289 · hard · unverified

**What mainly determines the diet of each killer whale ecotype (a distinct regional type of orca)?**

- A. **Learned hunting traditions** ✓
- B. Water temperature
- C. Body size

Killer whale groups pass down their hunting tactics, so culture largely decides whether an ecotype specializes in fish, seals or other prey.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/killer-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.killer-whale.diet-learned` · risk: disputed-check
