### dd-0290 · hard · unverified

**Compared with fish-eating 'resident' killer whales, how often do mammal-hunting 'transient' killer whales use echolocation clicks?**

- A. Far more often
- B. **Far less often** ✓
- C. About as often

One study found residents produced echolocation click trains about 27 times more often than the marine-mammal-eating transients.

Source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/27306/noaa_27306_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.killer-whale.transient-quiet-echolocation` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA bibliography entry: resident killer whales produced trains of sonar clicks on average 4% of the time, 27 times more often than marine-mammal-eating transients.</sub>

### dd-0291 · medium · unverified

**What makes the rough patches, or callosities, on a right whale's head look white?**

- A. Crusts of sea salt
- B. Patches of pale fur
- C. **Colonies of whale lice** ✓

Callosities are patches of rough skin on an otherwise dark whale. They look white because they are covered in whale lice, tiny crustaceans called cyamids.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/whale-lice) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/tale-right-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.right-whale.callosity-color` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results: right whales have knobby white patches of rough skin that appear white because of whale lice (cyamids) covering the otherwise black skin.</sub>

### dd-0292 · easy · unverified

**How do researchers tell individual North Atlantic right whales apart?**

- A. **By the pattern of callosities on their heads** ✓
- B. By counting their baleen plates
- C. By the pitch of their songs

Each right whale has a unique pattern of callosities, rough skin patches on its head, that researchers match against photo catalogs, much like fingerprints.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/north-atlantic-right-whale)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.right-whale.callosity-identification` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA: callosities occur in unique patterns on North Atlantic right whales and help researchers identify and track individuals via photo catalogs.</sub>

### dd-0293 · hard · review

**What do the whale lice living on a right whale's callosities mainly feed on?**

- A. The whale's blood
- B. **Algae settling on the skin** ✓
- C. Krill the whale swallows

Whale lice are small crustaceans that eat algae settling on the callosities, causing only minor damage to the whale's skin.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/whale-lice)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.right-whale.whale-lice-diet` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian whale lice page: the cyamid amphipods on callosities eat algae that settles there and cause only minor skin damage.</sub>

### dd-0294 · medium · unverified

**How do porpoise teeth typically differ from the teeth of most dolphins?**

- A. They are long and tusk-like
- B. **They are flat and spade-shaped** ✓
- C. They are cone-shaped and pointed

Most dolphins have cone-shaped teeth, while porpoise teeth are flattened and spade-shaped, one way to tell the two apart up close.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/dolphin_porpoise.html) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/it-dolphin-or-porpoise)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.porpoises.teeth-shape` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Service: most dolphins have cone-shaped teeth while porpoise teeth are flattened; NOAA Fisheries calls porpoise teeth flat and spade shaped.</sub>

### dd-0295 · medium · unverified

**What dorsal fin shape is typical of porpoises?**

- A. Tall and sickle-curved
- B. Long and flag-shaped
- C. **Triangular** ✓

Porpoises usually have triangular dorsal fins, while most dolphins have curved, sickle-like fins, though NOAA notes there are exceptions.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/it-dolphin-or-porpoise)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.porpoises.dorsal-fin-shape` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Fisheries: dolphins have hooked or curved (falcate) dorsal fins like a sickle; porpoises have triangular dorsal fins.</sub>

**Which dolphin is nicknamed the 'hookfin porpoise' for its large, curved dorsal fin?**

- A. Risso's dolphin
- B. **Pacific white-sided dolphin** ✓
- C. Spinner dolphin

The Pacific white-sided dolphin's big, hooked fin earned it the nickname 'hookfin porpoise', even though it is a dolphin, not a porpoise.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/pacific-white-sided-dolphin)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.pacific-white-sided-dolphin.hookfin-nickname` · risk: species-specific

**Where does the vaquita, the world's smallest porpoise, live?**

- A. The Baltic Sea
- B. The Amazon River
- C. **The northern Gulf of California** ✓

The vaquita lives only in the northern Gulf of California, Mexico, giving it one of the smallest ranges of any marine mammal.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/vaquita)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.vaquita.range` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA: the vaquita (Phocoena sinus) is the world's smallest porpoise and lives only in the northern Gulf of California, mostly east of San Felipe, Baja California.</sub>

### dd-0298 · medium · unverified

**What is the main threat to the endangered vaquita?**

- A. **Drowning in fishing gillnets** ✓
- B. Oil spills
- C. Collisions with ships

Vaquitas get tangled and drown in gillnets, especially illegal nets set for a large fish called the totoaba.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/vanishing-vaquita) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/endangered-vaquita-remain-genetically-healthy-even-low-numbers-new-analysis-shows)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.vaquita.gillnet-threat` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian and NOAA results: the decline is due to bycatch in gillnets, especially an illegal fishery for the totoaba; NOAA genetic study says recovery is possible if illegal gillnetting stops.</sub>

### dd-0299 · easy · unverified

**What is a bottlenose dolphin's 'signature whistle'?**

- A. A call that warns of sharks
- B. A song that only males sing
- C. **A call that identifies the individual** ✓

Each bottlenose dolphin develops its own learned whistle, and other dolphins can recognize who is calling from the whistle's shape alone.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1472465/)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bottlenose-dolphin.signature-whistle` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Study shows bottlenose dolphins extract identity information from signature whistles even with voice features removed; whistles are learned and individually distinctive.</sub>

### dd-0300 · hard · unverified

**When their calves are nearby, how do bottlenose dolphin mothers change their signature whistles?**

- A. **They use higher, wider-ranging pitches** ✓
- B. They stop whistling altogether
- C. They copy their calf's whistle

Rather like human baby talk, dolphin mothers in a long-term study whistled with higher maximum pitches and a wider pitch range when with their calves.

Source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/59408/noaa_59408_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bottlenose-dolphin.mother-whistle-change` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Only the result title was shown ('Bottlenose dolphin mothers modify signature whistles in...'). Verifier: confirm the paper reports higher maximum frequency and wider frequency range when calves are present.</sub>

### dd-0301 · easy · unverified

**How do dolphins manage to sleep while still surfacing to breathe?**

- A. **They rest one half of the brain at a time** ✓
- B. They hold their breath for hours
- C. They sleep only on the seafloor

In unihemispheric sleep, one half of the brain rests while the other stays alert, letting dolphins keep moving, surface for air and stay watchful.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/ocean-learning-hub/ocean-facts/how-do-marine-animals-sleep/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8665646/)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.dolphins.unihemispheric-sleep` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results: dolphins sleep with one brain hemisphere at a time (unihemispheric slow-wave sleep); the awake side lets them surface for air and stay vigilant.</sub>

### dd-0302 · hard · unverified

**In one study, how long did bottlenose dolphins keep up alert echolocation without a break or signs of mental fatigue?**

- A. 15 minutes
- B. **15 days** ✓
- C. 15 hours

Bottlenose dolphins in the study kept detecting targets with echolocation for 15 days straight without signs of cognitive impairment.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3474785/)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bottlenose-dolphin.echolocation-vigilance` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Paper title states dolphins maintained vigilant behavior through echolocation for 15 days without interruption or cognitive impairment. Verifier: confirm species is bottlenose dolphin.</sub>

### dd-0303 · medium · unverified

**Some bottlenose dolphins in Shark Bay, Australia, carry sea sponges on their snouts. Why?**

- A. To attract a mate
- B. To carry food to their calves
- C. **To protect them while probing the seafloor** ✓

A dolphin breaks off a cone-shaped sponge and wears it over its beak, apparently as protection while it probes the sandy seafloor for hidden fish.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/sponge-wielding-bottlenose-dolphin) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2587914/)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bottlenose-dolphin.sponging-purpose` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Results: a dolphin breaks a sponge off the seafloor and wears it over its closed rostrum to probe the substrate for fish; dolphins seeking burrowing prey benefit from protective sponges.</sub>

### dd-0304 · hard · unverified

**How is sponge tool use mainly passed on among Shark Bay bottlenose dolphins?**

- A. **From mothers to their offspring** ✓
- B. Between rival adult males
- C. By instinct, with no learning

Sponging is passed down mainly from mothers to their offspring, especially daughters, which researchers called the first known material culture in a marine mammal.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1157020/)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.bottlenose-dolphin.sponging-transmission` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Paper found sponging shows almost exclusive vertical social transmission within a single matriline from mother to female offspring, called the first case of material culture in a marine mammal.</sub>

**What does a Dall's porpoise create when it surfaces quickly while swimming fast?**

- A. A ring of bubbles
- B. A loud trumpeting blow
- C. **A 'rooster tail' of spray** ✓

Dall's porpoises surface so briskly that they throw up a 'rooster tail' of water spray, a trademark of the species.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/dalls-porpoise) · second source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/kefj/learn/nature/dalls-porpoise.htm)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.dalls-porpoise.rooster-tail` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA: Dall's porpoises briskly surface while swimming, creating a rooster tail of water spray that is a unique characteristic of the species.</sub>

### dd-0306 · medium · unverified

**What can scientists learn from the layers in a baleen whale's earwax plug?**

- A. **The whale's age** ✓
- B. The whale's top speed
- C. The depth of its deepest dive

Earwax builds up in layers over a baleen whale's life, a bit like tree rings, so a plug from a museum specimen can reveal the whale's age.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/marine-mammals/whale-earwax-what-you-can-learn-strange-collections)

Fact id: `marine-mammals.whales.earwax-plug-age` · risk: surprising

## sea-turtles (55)

### dd-0190 · easy · unverified

**What sets the leatherback apart from the other sea turtle species?**

- A. **It has no hard shell** ✓
- B. It never leaves the tropics
- C. It lays its eggs at sea

Leatherbacks are the only sea turtles without a hard shell. Their shell is covered in tough, rubbery skin, which gives them their name.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/leatherback-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.no-hard-shell` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the leatherback is the only sea turtle species that lacks scales and a hard shell; its rubbery skin gives it its name.</sub>

### dd-0191 · easy · unverified

**What is the main food of the leatherback sea turtle?**

- A. Seagrass and algae
- B. **Jellyfish and other soft-bodied animals** ✓
- C. Crabs and clams

Leatherbacks feed on soft-bodied prey such as jellyfish and salps, which are drifting, barrel-shaped animals of the open ocean.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/leatherback-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.diet-jellyfish` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's leatherback page says it eats soft-bodied prey such as jellyfish and salps.</sub>

### dd-0192 · medium · unverified

**How does a leatherback turtle keep hold of slippery jellyfish?**

- A. Sticky saliva on its tongue
- B. Hooked claws on its front flippers
- C. **Backward-pointing spines in its mouth and throat** ✓

Backward-facing spines lining the leatherback's mouth and throat help it grip soft, slippery prey like jellyfish.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/leatherback-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.mouth-spines` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA describes pointed tooth-like cusps and backward-facing spines in the leatherback's mouth that help it hold slippery food.</sub>

### dd-0193 · hard · unverified

**About how deep can a leatherback sea turtle dive?**

- A. About 400 feet
- B. **About 4,000 feet** ✓
- C. About 14,000 feet

Leatherbacks dive deeper than any other sea turtle, to about 4,000 feet (roughly 1,200 meters).

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/leatherback-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.dive-depth` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA material says leatherbacks dive deeper than other sea turtles, to about 4,000 feet, staying under up to 85 minutes.</sub>

### dd-0194 · medium · unverified

**Which reptile has the widest global distribution?**

- A. **Leatherback sea turtle** ✓
- B. Saltwater crocodile
- C. Yellow-bellied sea snake

Leatherbacks roam the Atlantic, Pacific and Indian Oceans, giving them the widest global distribution of any reptile.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/leatherback-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.widest-range` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA's leatherback page says it is found across the Atlantic, Pacific and Indian Oceans and has the widest global distribution of any reptile.</sub>

### dd-0195 · hard · review

**Leatherbacks need big salt glands because jellyfish are mostly seawater. How big are the glands?**

- A. About the size of a pea
- B. About the size of its brain
- C. **More than twice the size of its brain** ✓

Eating jellyfish means swallowing lots of salt, so leatherbacks have the largest salt glands of any sea turtle, more than twice the size of their brains.

Source: [NOAA Office of Response and Restoration](https://response.restoration.noaa.gov/sites/default/files/Oil_Sea_Turtles_2021.pdf) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/reptiles/sea-turtles)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.leatherback.salt-gland-size` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search summary: leatherbacks have the largest salt glands, more than twice the size of their brains, because jellies are mostly seawater and they ingest more salt than other sea turtles. Verifier: confirm which of the two pages carries the brain comparison.</sub>

### dd-0196 · medium · unverified

**Why do sea turtles often look like they are crying?**

- A. Sand is irritating their eyes
- B. **Glands by their eyes are getting rid of salt** ✓
- C. They are releasing stress hormones

Sea turtles take in salty water as they eat. Modified tear glands behind the eyes pour out the extra salt, and the discharge looks like tears.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/reptiles/sea-turtles) · second source: [NOAA Office of Response and Restoration](https://response.restoration.noaa.gov/sites/default/files/Oil_Sea_Turtles_2021.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.salt-glands-tears` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Sources say sea turtles excrete excess salt through modified tear (lachrymal) glands behind the eyes, and the excretions can make them look like they are crying.</sub>

### dd-0197 · easy · unverified

**What do adult green sea turtles mainly eat?**

- A. Jellyfish
- B. Sponges
- C. **Seagrasses and algae** ✓

Adult green turtles are unusual among sea turtles because they are herbivores, grazing mostly on seagrasses, seaweed and algae.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/green-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.green-turtle.herbivore` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says green turtles are unique among sea turtles as herbivores, eating mostly seaweed, seagrasses and algae.</sub>

### dd-0198 · medium · unverified

**Where does the green sea turtle get its name?**

- A. **The color of its body fat** ✓
- B. The color of its shell
- C. Green algae growing on its back

The name comes from the greenish color of the turtle's fat, not its shell. That color was once credited to its diet, though other sea turtles have greenish fat too.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/10-tremendous-turtle-facts) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/green-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.green-turtle.name-fat` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the name comes from the color of their fat, not their shells; this was attributed to diet, but other sea turtles have greenish fat as well.</sub>

### dd-0199 · medium · unverified

**What makes up most of the hawksbill sea turtle's diet?**

- A. Seagrass
- B. **Sponges** ✓
- C. Fish

Hawksbills feed mainly on sponges growing on coral reefs, and also eat algae, corals, mollusks, sea urchins and other invertebrates.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/hawksbill-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hawksbill.diet-sponges` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the hawksbill's diet consists mainly of sponges that live on coral reefs, plus algae, corals, mollusks, tunicates, crustaceans and others.</sub>

### dd-0200 · medium · unverified

**How is the hawksbill's mouth suited to feeding on coral reefs?**

- A. A broad jaw built for crushing shells
- B. A long snout for sucking up sand
- C. **A narrow beak that reaches into crevices** ✓

The hawksbill's narrow, pointed beak lets it probe cracks and crevices in the reef to pull out sponges and other prey.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/hawksbill-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hawksbill.narrow-beak` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the hawksbill's narrow beak lets it reach into reef crevices to extract sponges and other prey.</sub>

### dd-0201 · hard · unverified

**In the eastern Pacific, adult hawksbill turtles eat large amounts of which unusual food?**

- A. **Mangrove seeds** ✓
- B. Kelp holdfasts
- C. Fish eggs

Hawksbill diets vary by region. In the eastern Pacific, adults eat large quantities of mangrove seeds along with marine algae and invertebrates.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/s3/2023-09/Sea-Turtle-Framework-for-ESA-Sections-7.pdf) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/west-coast/science-data/hawksbill-turtles-eastern-pacific)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hawksbill.eastern-pacific-mangrove-seeds` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA's sea turtle framework notes regional diet differences: in the eastern Pacific, adult hawksbills consume large quantities of mangrove seeds plus algae and invertebrates.</sub>

### dd-0202 · hard · unverified

**How does the hawksbill's choosy sponge-eating benefit coral reefs?**

- A. It spreads coral larvae across the reef
- B. **It keeps common sponges from crowding out rarer reef species** ✓
- C. It clears sand off the reef surface

Because hawksbills feed mostly on certain sponge types, they keep those sponges in check, letting rarer species get established and helping the reef stay diverse.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/hawksbill-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hawksbill.reef-diversity` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA says that by eating only specific sponge types, hawksbills allow rarer species to become established and promote a healthy, diverse reef.</sub>

### dd-0203 · easy · unverified

**The loggerhead is named for its big head. What do its powerful jaw muscles let it do?**

- A. Tear up tough seagrass
- B. Dig deep nests in sand
- C. **Crush hard-shelled prey like conch** ✓

A loggerhead's large head anchors strong jaw muscles for cracking hard-shelled prey such as whelks, conch, crabs and horseshoe crabs.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/loggerhead-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.loggerhead.name-jaws` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the loggerhead is named for its large head, which supports powerful jaw muscles for feeding on hard-shelled prey such as whelks and conch.</sub>

### dd-0204 · medium · unverified

**Which sea turtle is the most common species nesting on U.S. beaches?**

- A. **Loggerhead** ✓
- B. Leatherback
- C. Hawksbill

Loggerheads are the most common sea turtle in U.S. coastal waters and the species that nests most often on U.S. beaches.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/loggerhead-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.loggerhead.most-common-us-nester` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says loggerheads are the most common sea turtle in U.S. coastal waters and the most common species nesting on U.S. beaches.</sub>

### dd-0205 · medium · unverified

**What does an adult loggerhead's top shell look like?**

- A. Black, smooth and ridged lengthwise
- B. **Reddish-brown and slightly heart-shaped** ✓
- C. Bright green and almost perfectly round

Adult loggerheads have a slightly heart-shaped, reddish-brown carapace (top shell) and a pale yellowish plastron (bottom shell).

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/loggerhead-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.loggerhead.shell-color` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the loggerhead's carapace is slightly heart-shaped and reddish-brown in adults; the plastron is pale yellowish.</sub>

### dd-0206 · medium · unverified

**Some sea turtles nest in mass events called arribadas. What does the Spanish word mean?**

- A. Storm
- B. Full moon
- C. **Arrival** ✓

Arribada is Spanish for 'arrival.' Females gather offshore near the nesting beach, then come ashore together in large groups to nest.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pais/learn/nature/nesters.htm)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.ridley.arribada-meaning` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS says Kemp's ridleys nest in arribadas, the Spanish word for 'arrival'; females gather offshore and come ashore together.</sub>

### dd-0207 · medium · unverified

**Which two sea turtle species nest in mass arribadas?**

- A. **Kemp's ridley and olive ridley** ✓
- B. Green turtle and hawksbill
- C. Loggerhead and flatback

Kemp's ridleys and olive ridleys are the two sea turtles known for arribadas, when thousands of females can come ashore together to nest.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pais/learn/nature/nesters.htm) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/olive-ridley-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.ridley.arribada-species` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NPS says Kemp's ridleys are one of two species that nest in arribadas, the other being the olive ridley; NOAA's olive ridley page describes its arribadas of thousands of turtles.</sub>

### dd-0208 · hard · unverified

**What is unusual about when Kemp's ridley turtles nest?**

- A. They nest only in midwinter
- B. **They routinely nest during the day** ✓
- C. They nest only on moonless nights

Kemp's ridleys are the only sea turtles that routinely nest in daylight, though some do nest at night.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pais/learn/nature/nesters.htm)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.kemps-ridley.daytime-nesting` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NPS Padre Island says Kemp's ridleys are the only sea turtles that routinely nest during the day, though some nest at night.</sub>

### dd-0209 · medium · unverified

**Where does most of the world's Kemp's ridley nesting take place?**

- A. Florida's Atlantic coast
- B. Costa Rica's Pacific coast
- C. **Tamaulipas, Mexico** ✓

About 95 percent of Kemp's ridley nesting happens in the Mexican state of Tamaulipas, at Gulf of Mexico beaches such as Rancho Nuevo.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pais/learn/nature/kridley.htm) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/kemps-ridley-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.kemps-ridley.tamaulipas` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NPS says 95 percent of worldwide Kemp's ridley nesting occurs in Tamaulipas, Mexico; main beaches Rancho Nuevo, Tepehuajes and Barra del Tordo.</sub>

### dd-0210 · hard · unverified

**Which U.S. park unit has worked to re-establish a nesting colony of Kemp's ridley turtles?**

- A. **Padre Island National Seashore** ✓
- B. Cape Hatteras National Seashore
- C. Everglades National Park

Padre Island National Seashore in Texas has worked to build a secondary Kemp's ridley colony, and more of the species' nests are found there than anywhere else in the U.S.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pais/learn/nature/kridley.htm)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.kemps-ridley.padre-island` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NPS Padre Island says it has worked to re-establish a secondary Kemp's ridley colony there, where more nests are found than anywhere else in the U.S.</sub>

### dd-0211 · easy · unverified

**Where does the olive ridley sea turtle get its name?**

- A. The greenish color of its body fat
- B. **The olive-green color of its shell** ✓
- C. Algae that grow on its back

The olive ridley is named for the olive-green color of its heart-shaped shell. It is one of the smallest sea turtles.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/olive-ridley-turtle)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.olive-ridley.name` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the olive ridley's common name comes from the olive-green color of its heart-shaped shell, and it is one of the smallest sea turtles.</sub>

### dd-0212 · easy · unverified

**Where does the flatback sea turtle nest?**

- A. Across the Caribbean
- B. Around the Mediterranean
- C. **Only in Australia** ✓

Flatbacks nest only in Australia, on inshore islands and mainland beaches from southern Queensland to northern Western Australia.

Source: [Australian Government DCCEEW](https://www.dcceew.gov.au/environment/marine/marine-species/marine-turtles/flatback)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.flatback.australia-only` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: DCCEEW says flatbacks nest on inshore islands and the mainland from Mon Repos in southern Queensland to Exmouth in northern Western Australia; summary states they nest only in Australia.</sub>

### dd-0213 · hard · unverified

**Flatback turtles lay unusually few eggs. About how many are in a typical nest?**

- A. **About 50** ✓
- B. About 150
- C. About 500

Female flatbacks lay about 50 eggs per nest, the fewest of any sea turtle, but the eggs are large for the species' body size.

Source: [Australian Government DCCEEW](https://www.dcceew.gov.au/environment/marine/marine-species/marine-turtles/flatback) · second source: [Australian Government DAWE](https://www.awe.gov.au/environment/biodiversity/threatened/publications/flatback-turtle-natator-depressus-2008)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.flatback.clutch-size` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Australian government material says female flatbacks lay approximately 50 eggs per nest, the fewest of any marine turtle, with eggs large relative to body size. Verifier: confirm which page carries this.</sub>

### dd-0214 · hard · unverified

**On some Cape York beaches in Australia, which introduced animal eats up to 90 percent of flatback turtle eggs?**

- A. **Feral pigs** ✓
- B. Cane toads
- C. European rabbits

Feral pigs dig up turtle nests on Cape York, and on some beaches they have been reported to eat up to 90 percent of the eggs.

Source: [Australian Government DCCEEW](https://www.dcceew.gov.au/environment/marine/marine-species/marine-turtles/flatback)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.flatback.feral-pigs` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search summary of Australian flatback pages: feral pigs are a major problem on Cape York, reportedly eating up to 90 per cent of eggs on some beaches.</sub>

### dd-0215 · easy · unverified

**Can a sea turtle pull its head and flippers into its shell?**

- A. Yes, just like a tortoise
- B. **No, they stay outside the shell** ✓
- C. Only its head, not its flippers

Unlike freshwater turtles and tortoises, sea turtles cannot withdraw their head or flippers. Instead they have streamlined shells and paddle-like flippers for swimming.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/10-tremendous-turtle-facts) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/reptiles/sea-turtles)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.cannot-retract` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says unlike other turtles, sea turtles cannot retract their flippers and head into their shells; Smithsonian says head and limbs are fixed outside the shell.</sub>

### dd-0216 · easy · unverified

**In sea turtle nests, what do warmer incubation temperatures tend to produce?**

- A. More male hatchlings
- B. Faster hatching, equal sexes
- C. **More female hatchlings** ✓

A sea turtle's sex is set by nest temperature. Warmer sand yields mostly females and cooler sand mostly males, so climate warming is a concern.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7288305/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7060753/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.tsd-warm-female` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed sources describe temperature-dependent sex determination in sea turtles, with warmer incubation producing more females.</sub>

### dd-0217 · hard · unverified

**During which part of incubation does temperature decide a sea turtle embryo's sex?**

- A. **The middle third of incubation** ✓
- B. The first day after laying
- C. The last hours before hatching

The thermosensitive period is roughly the middle third of incubation; temperatures then largely decide whether an embryo becomes male or female.

Source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/50641/noaa_50641_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.tsd-middle-third` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: A NOAA-hosted loggerhead paper says temperatures below a pivotal ~29 °C in the middle third of incubation produce mainly males, above it mainly females.</sub>

### dd-0218 · medium · unverified

**What is the 'pivotal temperature' of a sea turtle nest?**

- A. The temperature at which eggs stop developing
- B. **The temperature giving equal males and females** ✓
- C. The temperature that makes hatchlings emerge

At the pivotal temperature about half the hatchlings are female. It differs between species and even between populations of the same species.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7060753/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7288305/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.pivotal-temperature` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed sources define the pivotal temperature as the temperature at which 50% of hatchlings are female, and note it varies between populations.</sub>

### dd-0219 · medium · Field Guide: turtle-home · unverified

**What does research suggest loggerheads imprint on as hatchlings to find their home beach years later?**

- A. The sound of waves on that beach
- B. The pattern of stars above it
- C. **The beach's magnetic signature** ✓

Evidence suggests loggerheads imprint on the unique magnetic field of their natal beach and use it to return as adults to nest.

Source: [PubMed (NCBI)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25601546/) · second source: [UNC-Chapel Hill College of Arts & Sciences](https://magazine.college.unc.edu/college-news-notes/turtles/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.magnetic-imprinting` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Lohmann and colleagues propose loggerheads imprint on the geomagnetic signature of their natal area and use it to return; the evidence is correlational.</sub>

### dd-0220 · hard · Field Guide: turtle-home · rejected

**A 19-year study of Florida loggerheads found nesting shifted along the coast in step with what?**

- A. **Slow drift in Earth's magnetic field** ✓
- B. Changes in the color of the sand
- C. Rising numbers of beach visitors

As Earth's magnetic field slowly drifted, loggerhead nesting shifted to match, as expected if turtles imprint on the magnetic signature of their home beach.

Source: [PubMed (NCBI)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25601546/) · second source: [UNC-Chapel Hill (Lohmann Lab)](https://lohmannlab.web.unc.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/8154/2026/08/Evidence-for-geomagnetic-imprinting-and-magnetic-navigation-in-the-natal-homing-of-sea-turtles.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.nesting-follows-field-drift` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: A 19-year analysis of Florida loggerhead nesting found nest distribution tracked small shifts in Earth's magnetic field, as geomagnetic imprinting predicts.</sub>

### dd-0221 · hard · Field Guide: turtle-home · unverified

**Sea turtles appear to tell coastal locations apart using which pair of cues?**

- A. Water saltiness and depth
- B. **Magnetic field strength and inclination angle** ✓
- C. Air temperature and humidity

Each stretch of coast has its own mix of magnetic field strength and inclination, the angle at which field lines meet Earth's surface. Turtles seem to read both.

Source: [UNC-Chapel Hill](https://endeavors.unc.edu/sea-turtle-secrets/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8918461/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.magnetic-cues` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: UNC research describes turtles reading magnetic intensity and inclination angle (angle at which field lines intersect Earth's surface), with each coastline having a distinct combination.</sub>

### dd-0222 · hard · Field Guide: turtle-home · unverified

**A 2018 study compared loggerhead nesting beaches. What did similar magnetic fields between beaches predict?**

- A. Similar sand temperatures
- B. The same nesting month
- C. **Genetically similar turtles** ✓

Magnetic differences between loggerhead nesting beaches predicted genetic differences better than distance or environment did, supporting magnetic homing.

Source: [UNC-Chapel Hill (Lohmann Lab)](https://lohmannlab.web.unc.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/8154/2019/03/2018_MagneticGenetics_BrothersLohmann_CB.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.turtles.magnetic-genetics` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Brothers and Lohmann 2018 report that spatial variation in Earth's magnetic field strongly predicts genetic differentiation between nesting beaches, even after accounting for distance and environment.</sub>

### dd-0223 · medium · unverified

**What mainly prompts sea turtle hatchlings to leave the nest at night?**

- A. **A drop in sand temperature** ✓
- B. The rising tide
- C. Moonlight on the sand

Hatchlings stay quiet while the sand near the surface is hot. As it cools after dark they become active, so most emerge at night.

Source: [Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission](https://myfwc.com/research/wildlife/sea-turtles/florida/faq/) · second source: [National Park Service](https://home.nps.gov/caha/learn/nature/seaturtles.htm)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hatchlings.night-emergence-cue` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search summary: a temperature cue prompts hatchlings to emerge primarily at night, with some late-afternoon and early-morning emergences; emerging only at full moon is a myth. Verifier: confirm the cue is described as cooling sand.</sub>

### dd-0224 · easy · unverified

**On a natural beach, how do newly emerged sea turtle hatchlings find the ocean?**

- A. They follow the sound of the waves
- B. **They head toward the brightest direction** ✓
- C. They follow their mother's tracks

Hatchlings instinctively crawl toward the brightest direction, normally the open night sky over the sea, which is why artificial lights can lure them inland.

Source: [Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission](https://myfwc.com/research/wildlife/sea-turtles/threats/artificial-lighting/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.hatchlings.brightest-direction` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: FWC says hatchlings have an inborn tendency to move in the brightest direction, usually the night sky over the ocean, and to move away from dark dune silhouettes.</sub>

### dd-0225 · medium · unverified

**During their early 'lost years' at sea, young loggerheads are often found among what?**

- A. Deep-sea coral reefs
- B. Tangled mangrove roots
- C. **Floating mats of sargassum seaweed** ✓

Hatchling loggerheads seem to seek out ocean fronts where floating sargassum seaweed collects, which may give them food and shelter.

Source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/9151/noaa_9151_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.lost-years.sargassum` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: A NOAA technical memo says hatchling turtles are thought to actively seek out frontal zones and sargassum rafts, used for forage and protection during the 'lost year'.</sub>

### dd-0226 · hard · Field Guide: turtle-home · unverified

**Young green turtles tagged and released off Florida's Atlantic coast were tracked to which region?**

- A. **The Sargasso Sea** ✓
- B. The Mediterranean Sea
- C. The Red Sea

University of Central Florida researchers tracked young green turtles from Florida into the Sargasso Sea, a seaweed-rich part of the North Atlantic, during their 'lost years'.

Source: [University of Central Florida](https://www.ucf.edu/news/legendary-sargasso-sea-may-be-sea-turtles-destination-during-mysterious-lost-years/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.lost-years.sargasso-sea` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: UCF reported evidence of baby green sea turtles arriving at the Sargasso Sea after entering the ocean off Florida's east coast; earlier work tracked loggerheads there too.</sub>

### dd-0227 · hard · unverified

**Tracking of young 'toddler' sea turtles challenged which long-held assumption?**

- A. That they stay near their nesting beach
- B. **That they simply drift with currents** ✓
- C. That they feed only at night

It was widely assumed young sea turtles just drift wherever currents take them. Tracking showed yearlings are active swimmers instead.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/media-release/ocean-myth-busted-toddler-sea-turtles-are-very-active-swimmers)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.lost-years.active-swimmers` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: NOAA media release: it had been widely assumed turtles simply drift with ocean currents, but toddler sea turtles proved to be very active swimmers.</sub>

### dd-0228 · easy · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**Which sea turtle is well known for visiting reef 'cleaning stations' in Hawaii?**

- A. Leatherback
- B. Kemp's ridley
- C. **Green turtle** ✓

Green turtles in Hawaiian waters visit reef spots where fish pick algae and parasites off their shells, a stop NOAA has called the turtle spa.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/magazine/5/day-at-the-turtle-spa/) · second source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2025/sanctuary-symbioses.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.clean.hawaii-green-turtle` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA sanctuary pages describe green sea turtles in Hawaiian waters visiting 'turtle cleaning stations' where reef fish clean their shells.</sub>

### dd-0229 · medium · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**Which fish is often seen picking at green turtles at Hawaiian cleaning stations?**

- A. **Convict tang** ✓
- B. Moray eel
- C. Great barracuda

Convict tangs and other reef fish nibble algae and parasites off resting green turtles, getting a meal while the turtle gets cleaned.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2025/sanctuary-symbioses.html) · second source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/magazine/5/day-at-the-turtle-spa/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.clean.convict-tang` · risk: species-specific
