### dd-0230 · easy · Field Guide: clean · rejected

**At turtle cleaning stations, what do reef fish remove from sea turtles?**

- A. Sand and grit
- B. **Algae and parasites** ✓
- C. Shed skin from the eyes

Grazing reef fish keep turtles free of algae and parasites and get a meal in return. The stations also help lower parasite loads on the reef.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/magazine/5/day-at-the-turtle-spa/) · second source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2025/sanctuary-symbioses.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.clean.what-is-removed` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says reef fish at cleaning stations keep turtles free of algae and other organisms while the fish get a meal, and the stations lower parasite loads.</sub>

### dd-0231 · easy · unverified

**What makes the Galápagos marine iguana unusual among lizards?**

- A. It glides between trees
- B. It eats only insects
- C. **It feeds in the sea** ✓

The marine iguana is the only lizard known to forage in the sea. It lives on rocky Galápagos shores and grazes algae underwater.

Source: [Galápagos Conservancy](https://www.galapagos.org/newsroom/surprising-facts-galapagos-species/) · second source: [bioRxiv (preprint)](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/2020.05.16.099184.full.pdf)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.marine-iguana.only-marine-lizard` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Galápagos Conservancy says it is the only lizard known to forage underwater; a 2020 paper calls it the world's only marine lizard.</sub>

### dd-0232 · medium · unverified

**How does the marine iguana get rid of the extra salt it swallows while feeding?**

- A. **It sneezes it out of its nostrils** ✓
- B. It sweats it out through its skin
- C. It passes it in its urine

Eating seaweed brings in a lot of salt. A special gland clears it from the blood, and the iguana expels it through its nostrils in a sneeze-like burst.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/shrinking-galapagos-marine-iguanas.html) · second source: [Galápagos Conservancy](https://www.galapagos.org/newsroom/surprising-facts-galapagos-species/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.marine-iguana.salt-sneeze` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM says a special gland removes excess salt, which the iguana pushes out through its nostrils in a sneeze.</sub>

### dd-0233 · hard · unverified

**What can adult marine iguanas do when El Niño cuts their food supply?**

- A. Switch to eating fish
- B. **Shrink in body size** ✓
- C. Hibernate in burrows

When El Niño warming reduces algae, adult marine iguanas can shrink, which may cut their energy needs. Smaller iguanas tend to survive these shortages better.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/shrinking-galapagos-marine-iguanas.html) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12450463/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.marine-iguana.shrinking` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NHM describes marine iguanas as 'incredible shrinking lizards'; a 2025 study notes adults may shrink during El Niño food shortages, possibly to reduce energy needs.</sub>

### dd-0234 · medium · unverified

**Why do marine iguanas bask on rocks after diving to feed?**

- A. To dry out their salt glands
- B. To attract birds that clean them
- C. **To warm up after the cold water** ✓

Marine iguanas dive into cool water to graze on algae, then return to land to warm their bodies in the sun before feeding again.

Source: [Galápagos Conservancy](https://www.galapagos.org/about_galapagos/about-galapagos/biodiversity/reptiles/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.marine-iguana.basking` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Galápagos Conservancy says marine iguanas dive to graze seaweed and return to land to warm up.</sub>

### dd-0235 · easy · unverified

**What is the largest living reptile?**

- A. **Saltwater crocodile** ✓
- B. Leatherback sea turtle
- C. Komodo dragon

The saltwater, or estuarine, crocodile is the largest living reptile. It lives in coastal rivers, estuaries and seas, including across northern Australia.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/reptiles/estuarine-crocodile/) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/reptiles/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.saltwater-crocodile.largest-reptile` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum describes the saltwater (estuarine) crocodile as the largest living reptile, with adults typically 3 to 5 metres.</sub>

### dd-0236 · medium · unverified

**How do saltwater crocodiles appear to make long journeys at sea?**

- A. By swimming fast against the current
- B. **By riding surface ocean currents** ✓
- C. By drifting on floating logs

Tracking studies suggest saltwater crocodiles ride surface currents on long trips, which could explain how they reach distant islands.

Source: [The University of Queensland](https://news.uq.edu.au/2010-06-04-crocodiles-ride-ocean-currents-travel-high-seas)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.saltwater-crocodile.ride-currents` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: UQ reports tracked estuarine crocodiles appear to ride surface currents over long distances, potentially allowing movement between oceanic islands.</sub>

### dd-0237 · medium · unverified

**When scientists moved tagged saltwater crocodiles far from where they were caught, what did the crocodiles do?**

- A. Settled in their new location
- B. Moved upriver into fresh water
- C. **Swam back to where they were caught** ✓

In a satellite-tracking study, all three relocated male crocodiles eventually returned to their capture sites; one swam over 400 km in 20 days.

Source: [PLOS ONE](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0000949) · second source: [The University of Queensland](https://news.uq.edu.au/2007-09-26-marathon-swimming-crocs-find-their-way-home)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.saltwater-crocodile.homing` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: PLoS ONE 2007: three translocated adult males all eventually returned to their capture sites; UQ news says one covered more than 400 km in 20 days.</sub>

### dd-0238 · medium · unverified

**Which crocodilian is the most widely distributed, ranging from Sri Lanka through Southeast Asia to northern Australia?**

- A. **Saltwater crocodile** ✓
- B. Nile crocodile
- C. American alligator

Saltwater crocodiles range from Sri Lanka through Southeast Asia to northern Australia, the widest distribution of any living crocodilian.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6946029/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.saltwater-crocodile.range` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Genome paper describes C. porosus as the largest and most widely distributed living crocodilian, from Sri Lanka through Southeast Asia to northern Australia.</sub>

### dd-0239 · easy · unverified

**How do true sea snakes get most of the oxygen they need?**

- A. Through gills behind the head
- B. **By surfacing to breathe air** ✓
- C. By filtering it from their food

Sea snakes have no gills. They come to the surface to fill their lungs, then dive again.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-snakes-sea-kraits-and-their-aquatic-adaptations.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.sea-snakes.breathe-air` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM says sea snakes lack gills and surface periodically to fill their lungs before diving again.</sub>

### dd-0240 · easy · unverified

**What body feature helps true sea snakes swim efficiently?**

- A. Small fins along the sides
- B. Webbed skin between the ribs
- C. **A flattened, paddle-like tail** ✓

True sea snakes have tails flattened like paddles, and their bodies are squeezed flat from side to side, helping them move through water.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-snakes-sea-kraits-and-their-aquatic-adaptations.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.sea-snakes.paddle-tail` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM says sea snake tails are flattened like paddles to help them move efficiently, and belly scales maintain a laterally compressed shape.</sub>

### dd-0241 · hard · unverified

**Besides using their lungs, some sea snakes take in oxygen in what other way?**

- A. **Through their skin** ✓
- B. Through their tongue
- C. Through their eyes

Highly vascular skin lets some sea snakes absorb part of their oxygen directly from seawater, though they still need to surface to breathe.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6774945/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12395837/)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.sea-snakes.skin-oxygen` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Sources say sea snakes can take up oxygen from seawater through their skin, aided by highly vascularised skin; lungs still do most of the work.</sub>

### dd-0242 · medium · unverified

**How does a true sea snake, such as the dusky sea snake, have its young?**

- A. It lays eggs on sandy beaches
- B. **It gives birth to live young underwater** ✓
- C. It lays eggs in floating seaweed

True sea snakes like the dusky sea snake develop their young inside the body and give birth underwater, so they can breed without coming ashore.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/dusky-sea-snake) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-snakes-sea-kraits-and-their-aquatic-adaptations.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.sea-snakes.live-birth` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's dusky sea snake review says it is viviparous, conceiving and bearing young fully underwater; NHM says true sea snakes give birth in water.</sub>

### dd-0243 · medium · unverified

**Where does the banded sea krait lay its eggs?**

- A. In floating rafts of seaweed
- B. In crevices on the reef underwater
- C. **On land, out of the water** ✓

Unlike true sea snakes, which give birth at sea, banded sea kraits come ashore to lay their eggs.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/banded_sea_krait) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-snakes-sea-kraits-and-their-aquatic-adaptations.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.sea-kraits.eggs-on-land` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific says the banded sea krait lays eggs on land, on the sand or just under it; NHM says sea kraits come onto land to lay eggs.</sub>

### dd-0244 · hard · unverified

**How does the yellow-bellied sea snake get drinking water?**

- A. **It drinks rainwater floating on the sea** ✓
- B. It drinks seawater and removes the salt
- C. It gets all its water from its prey

These snakes dehydrate in seawater. Rain is less dense than seawater, so it can float as a fresh layer, and the snakes surface to drink it during or after storms.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6366689/) · second source: [University of Florida](https://archive.news.ufl.edu/articles/2014/03/sea-snakes-need-fresh-water-for-drinking-uf-researcher-finds.html)

Fact id: `sea-turtles.yellow-bellied-sea-snake.drinks-rain` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: A 2019 study and UF research found yellow-bellied sea snakes drink only fresh or highly diluted water, from freshwater lenses that float on the sea surface after rain.</sub>

## crustaceans (29)

### dd-0106 · easy · Field Guide: shell · unverified

**Why does a hermit crab need to live in a borrowed shell?**

- A. **Its abdomen lacks hard armor** ✓
- B. Its gills only work in a shell
- C. It stores its food in the shell

Unlike other crabs, a hermit crab's abdomen isn't covered by a hard exoskeleton. It's soft and delicate, so the borrowed shell is its protection.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/hermit-crab-without-shell) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/crustaceans/hermit-crab/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hermit-crab.soft-abdomen` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results said hermit crabs differ from other crabs because the abdomen has no exoskeleton covering and is soft, so a discarded shell protects it; a hermit crab cannot survive long without one.</sub>

### dd-0107 · easy · Field Guide: shell · unverified

**What usually makes a hermit crab go looking for a new shell?**

- A. Its shell has faded in color
- B. **It has outgrown its current shell** ✓
- C. The tide has gone out

A hermit crab keeps growing, but its borrowed shell doesn't. Once the old shell feels cramped, the crab has to find a roomier one.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/hermit-crab)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hermit-crab.outgrows-shell` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The Monterey Bay Aquarium hermit crab page says hermit crabs grow and, when the shell becomes cramped, must search for a bigger one.</sub>

### dd-0108 · easy · unverified

**How does the hard-covered front half of a hermit crab get bigger as it grows?**

- A. The old armor slowly stretches
- B. New plates are glued onto the old ones
- C. **It sheds its exoskeleton and forms a new one** ✓

Like other crustaceans, hermit crabs molt: they shed their old exoskeleton and form a new, larger one to fit their bigger bodies.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/hermit-crab)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hermit-crab.molting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results said hermit crabs molt as they grow, shedding their exoskeletons and creating new, larger ones for their larger bodies.</sub>

### dd-0109 · medium · Field Guide: shell · unverified

**Before moving into an empty shell, what do hermit crabs often do first?**

- A. **Hold it and turn it over to inspect it** ✓
- B. Bury it in sand for a day
- C. Coat it in mucus to soften it

Hermit crabs check a shell by handling and turning it before leaving their old one. If the new shell isn't right, they may move back and keep searching.

Source: [Smithsonian Institution](https://www.si.edu/stories/it-would-be-shellfish-not-share-these-hermit-crab-facts)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hermit-crab.shell-inspection` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results said hermit crabs inspect shells by holding them up and turning them over before leaving their current home, and sometimes return to their original shell if the new one is not right.</sub>

### dd-0110 · medium · Field Guide: shell · unverified

**When a large empty shell turns up, hermit crabs sometimes form a line beside it. How is the line ordered?**

- A. By age, oldest crab first
- B. **By size, largest crab first** ✓
- C. By arrival, first crab first

The crabs line up from largest to smallest. When the biggest moves into the empty shell, each crab behind it climbs into the shell just vacated ahead of it.

Source: [Tufts Journal (Tufts University)](https://tuftsjournal.tufts.edu/2010/05_2/features/02/) · second source: [UC Berkeley Biodesign](https://biodesign.berkeley.edu/2022/04/22/bio-discoveries-hermit-crabs-line-up-by-size-to-exchange-shells//)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hermit-crab.vacancy-chain` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Tufts describes a synchronous vacancy chain: crabs arrange themselves in a line of decreasing size with the largest holding the empty shell, then swap one after another into the shells just vacated.</sub>

### dd-0111 · hard · Field Guide: shell · review

**How do striped hermit crabs usually handle shell swaps with others of their own species?**

- A. They fight until one crab flees
- B. They trade only at full moon
- C. **They exchange shells without violence** ✓

Some hermit crab species fight over good shells, but striped hermit crabs cooperate with members of their own species and swap shells peacefully.

Source: [Smithsonian's National Zoo](https://nationalzoo.si.edu/animals/striped-hermit-crab)

Fact id: `crustaceans.striped-hermit-crab.cooperative-swap` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search results said some hermit crab species fight over shells, while striped hermit crabs cooperate with members of their species and exchange shells without getting violent.</sub>

### dd-0112 · hard · unverified

**In a 2004 UC Berkeley study, roughly how fast did a peacock mantis shrimp's club move at its peak?**

- A. About 2 meters per second
- B. **About 23 meters per second** ✓
- C. About 230 meters per second

High-speed video clocked the club at about 23 meters per second, which is remarkable underwater, where moving fast means shoving dense water aside.

Source: [UC Berkeley News](https://newsarchive.berkeley.edu/news/media/releases/2004/04/21_shrimp.shtml) · second source: [Patek et al. 2004, Nature (UC Berkeley lab copy)](https://ib.berkeley.edu/labs/patek/shrimpMechanics/Pateketal2004Nature.pdf)

Fact id: `crustaceans.peacock-mantis-shrimp.strike-speed` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: The 2004 Berkeley release reported peak strike speeds of 23 m/s; later work cites a range of about 12 to 23 m/s for the club in water.</sub>

### dd-0113 · medium · unverified

**When a peacock mantis shrimp smashes a snail shell, what hits the shell a second time right after the club?**

- A. **Collapsing vapor bubbles** ✓
- B. A second, smaller club
- C. A squirt of venom

The club moves so fast that the water vaporizes into cavitation bubbles. When they collapse, they deliver a second force on the prey.

Source: [UC Berkeley Biodesign](https://biodesign.berkeley.edu/2022/03/04/paper-extreme-impact-and-cavitation-forces-of-a-biological-hammer-strike-forces-of-the-peacock-mantis-shrimp-odontodactylus-scyllarus/) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/mantis-shrimps.html)

Fact id: `crustaceans.peacock-mantis-shrimp.cavitation-second-blow` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Researchers measured two force peaks per strike: the first from the club hitting the snail shell, the second from the collapse of a cavitation bubble formed because the water vaporizes at that speed.</sub>

### dd-0114 · hard · unverified

**What lets a mantis shrimp store up energy for its ultrafast strike?**

- A. A pocket of compressed gas in its limb
- B. Extra-thick muscles that fire twice
- C. **A saddle-shaped spring in its exoskeleton** ✓

A saddle-shaped part of the exoskeleton flexes like a spring, storing energy and then releasing it all at once to power the strike.

Source: [Patek Lab, Duke University](https://pateklab.biology.duke.edu/research/mechanics-of-ultrafast-movement/mechanics-of-movement-mantis-shrimp/) · second source: [Patek et al. 2004, Nature (UC Berkeley lab copy)](https://ib.berkeley.edu/labs/patek/shrimpMechanics/Pateketal2004Nature.pdf)

Fact id: `crustaceans.mantis-shrimp.saddle-spring` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search results said a saddle-shaped exoskeletal structure stores and releases energy for the strike, described as the first biological hyperbolic-paraboloid spring.</sub>

### dd-0115 · medium · unverified

**Humans see color with three types of cones. About how many classes of photoreceptors do mantis shrimp use to sample color?**

- A. About 4
- B. **About 12** ✓
- C. About 40

Mantis shrimp use as many as 12 photoreceptor classes to sample colors from deep ultraviolet to far red. Exactly how they use them is still being studied.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4441025/) · second source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11744319/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.mantis-shrimp.color-photoreceptor-count` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed sources say stomatopods use as many as 12 classes of photoreceptors for color, spanning about 300 to 750 nm, versus three cone classes in primates.</sub>

### dd-0116 · medium · unverified

**In many mantis shrimp, a band of specialized rows across the middle of the eye detects color and what other property of light?**

- A. **Polarization** ✓
- B. Magnetism
- C. Temperature

In many species, six rows of eye units run across the middle of each eye: four handle color, and two are devoted to polarized light, the direction in which light waves vibrate.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11744319/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.mantis-shrimp.polarization-vision` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The paper describes six central rows of ommatidia (the midband) along the eye's equator: rows 1 to 4 for color discrimination and rows 5 and 6 for polarization vision.</sub>

### dd-0117 · hard · unverified

**In the mantis shrimp Neogonodactylus oerstedii, several ultraviolet receptors share one visual pigment. What tunes them to different wavelengths?**

- A. Lenses that rotate inside the eye
- B. **Filters that act like biological sunscreens** ✓
- C. Pigments that change with the seasons

Several of this species' UV receptor types share a visual pigment. Filters in the eye, which researchers liken to sunscreens, tune each type to a different slice of UV.

Source: [PubMed (peer-reviewed)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24998530)

Fact id: `crustaceans.mantis-shrimp.uv-sunscreen-filters` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The 2014 study found that the UV receptor set in Neogonodactylus oerstedii rests on only two visual pigments, with five of six UV receptor types sharing one pigment, and that biological sunscreens tune the UV vision.</sub>

### dd-0118 · medium · rejected

**Where does the loud snap of a snapping (pistol) shrimp actually come from?**

- A. Two claw parts clacking together
- B. Air forced out of its gills
- C. **A collapsing bubble of vapor** ✓

The claw shuts so fast that it shoots out a water jet, forming a cavitation bubble. The sound comes from that bubble collapsing, not from the claw itself.

Source: [Texas A&M University at Galveston](https://txmarspecies.tamug.edu/invertfamilydetails.cfm?famnameID=Alpheidae) · second source: [arXiv (Lohse group, physics)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/0712.2257)

Fact id: `crustaceans.snapping-shrimp.bubble-snap` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search results said the snapping shrimp closes its claw so quickly that a fast water jet develops; high-speed imaging showed the sound comes from a cavitation bubble formed in that jet and its collapse.</sub>

### dd-0119 · hard · unverified

**Besides a loud snap, what else does a snapping shrimp's collapsing bubble give off?**

- A. **A brief flash of light** ✓
- B. A puff of dark ink
- C. A small electric shock

Researchers recorded a short, intense flash as the bubble collapses, a sign of extreme pressure and heat inside. They named it shrimpoluminescence.

Source: [NASA/ADS abstract of a 2001 Nature paper](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2001Natur.413..477L/abstract)

Fact id: `crustaceans.snapping-shrimp.shrimpoluminescence` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: The 2001 Nature paper reported a short flash of light emitted as the snapping shrimp's cavitation bubble collapses, indicating extreme pressures and temperatures; the authors called it shrimpoluminescence.</sub>

### dd-0120 · medium · unverified

**Why do NOAA researchers listen to the snapping of tiny shrimp in marine sanctuaries?**

- A. To guide ships into port
- B. **To learn about habitat health** ✓
- C. To predict the next high tide

Snapping shrimp are small but very noisy, and their crackling sounds can give researchers clues about the health of the habitats where they live.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/dec21/oh-snap.html)

Fact id: `crustaceans.snapping-shrimp.habitat-health-sound` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The NOAA sanctuaries article (title in search result) is about what snapping shrimp sounds can tell researchers about habitat health. Verifier: confirm body text supports the explanation wording.</sub>

### dd-0121 · medium · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**What does the Hawaiian cleaner shrimp use to pick parasites off visiting fish?**

- A. Its long antennae
- B. Its fan-shaped tail
- C. **Its two front legs** ✓

Hawaiian cleaner shrimp run a cleaning station where they use their two front legs to scavenge and pick parasites off the fish that visit.

Source: [Georgia Aquarium](https://www.georgiaaquarium.org/animal/hawaiian-cleaner-shrimp/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.hawaiian-cleaner-shrimp.front-legs` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Georgia Aquarium says Hawaiian cleaner shrimp form a cleaning station where they use their two front legs to feed by scavenging or cleaning parasites off fish.</sub>

### dd-0122 · easy · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**How does a Pacific cleaner shrimp usually find fish to clean?**

- A. **It waits at a station on coral or rock** ✓
- B. It rides on a shark's back all day
- C. It drifts near the surface at night

Pacific cleaner shrimp set up a cleaning station on coral or rock and wait for fish to stop by. Parasites and dead tissue make up much of their diet.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/pacific_cleaner_shrimp)

Fact id: `crustaceans.pacific-cleaner-shrimp.station-waiting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific says Pacific cleaner shrimp set up a cleaning station on coral or a rock and wait for fish; parasites and dead tissue are a large share of their diet.</sub>

### dd-0123 · medium · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**On Caribbean reefs, Pederson cleaner shrimp run their cleaning stations together with which partner?**

- A. Sea cucumbers
- B. **Sea anemones** ✓
- C. Sea sponges

Pederson cleaner shrimp depend on sea anemones, such as the corkscrew anemone. Shrimp and anemone together form stations that fish visit to have parasites removed.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6030538/) · second source: [Lamar University](https://www.lamar.edu/arts-sciences/biology/study-abroad-belize/marine-critters/marine-critters-3/perdersons-cleaner-shrimp.html)

Fact id: `crustaceans.pederson-cleaner-shrimp.anemone-host` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search results said Pederson cleaner shrimp (Ancylomenes pedersoni) are obligate associates of sea anemones, especially corkscrew anemones, and together they form cleaning stations visited by client fish for parasite removal.</sub>

### dd-0124 · hard · Field Guide: clean · unverified

**What signal from a Pederson cleaner shrimp reliably tells a fish that cleaning is about to start?**

- A. Flashing a glowing spot
- B. Clicking its claws loudly
- C. **Whipping its antennae** ✓

Researchers found that when a Pederson cleaner shrimp whips its antennae at a fish, cleaning reliably follows, a visual signal between shrimp and client.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6030538/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.pederson-cleaner-shrimp.antenna-whipping` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The mutual visual signalling study found that cleaner antenna whipping reliably predicts subsequent cleaning by Pederson cleaner shrimp.</sub>

### dd-0125 · easy · unverified

**Horseshoe crabs aren't true crabs. Which animals are they more closely related to?**

- A. **Spiders and scorpions** ✓
- B. Lobsters and shrimp
- C. Snails and clams

Despite the name, horseshoe crabs aren't crustaceans. They're an ancient group of arthropods more closely related to spiders and scorpions.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/horseshoe-crab.html) · second source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/species/atlantic-horseshoe-crab-limulus-polyphemus)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.not-true-crab` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Service says horseshoe crabs are not really crabs (crustaceans) and are more closely related to spiders and scorpions; FWS calls them a member of an ancient group of arthropods.</sub>

### dd-0126 · hard · unverified

**Like spiders, horseshoe crabs have a pair of small appendages for moving food to the mouth. What are they called?**

- A. Antennae
- B. **Chelicerae** ✓
- C. Mandibles

Horseshoe crabs, which aren't true crabs, share chelicerae with spiders. These small appendages move food into the mouth and are a clue to their kinship.

Source: [Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission](https://myfwc.com/research/saltwater/crustaceans/horseshoe-crabs/facts/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.chelicerae` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Florida FWC says that like spiders, horseshoe crabs have a pair of chelicerae, small appendages for moving food into the mouth.</sub>

### dd-0127 · hard · unverified

**Counting its small light-sensitive eyes as well as its two big ones, about how many eyes does the Atlantic horseshoe crab have?**

- A. Two
- B. **About ten** ✓
- C. About forty

Besides two large compound eyes, the horseshoe crab has several smaller light-sensitive eyes, for around ten in all.

Source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/sites/default/files/documents/Horseshoecrab.pdf) · second source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/articles/ncbn-species-spotlight-atlantic-horseshoe-crab.htm)

Fact id: `crustaceans.atlantic-horseshoe-crab.eye-count` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Search results said horseshoe crabs have 10 eyes in total: two large compound eyes plus eight others that are very light-sensitive and keep the crab oriented. Some popular sources give other totals (e.g. nine), so the verifier should confirm which page states ten.</sub>

### dd-0128 · easy · unverified

**What does an Atlantic horseshoe crab mainly use its long, pointed tail for?**

- A. Stinging predators
- B. **Flipping itself upright** ✓
- C. Spearing small fish

The tail, called a telson, helps the crab flip back over when waves turn it upside down. Horseshoe crabs, which aren't true crabs, are harmless to people.

Source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/sites/default/files/documents/Horseshoecrab.pdf) · second source: [Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission](https://myfwc.com/research/saltwater/crustaceans/horseshoe-crabs/facts/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.atlantic-horseshoe-crab.telson-righting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: FWS says the telson helps the crab flip itself over if waves turn it over on the beach; Florida FWC says the sharp tails are totally harmless.</sub>

### dd-0129 · medium · unverified

**Why do medical companies collect blood from Atlantic horseshoe crabs?**

- A. **To check drugs for bacterial toxins** ✓
- B. To make human blood transfusions
- C. To produce snakebite antivenom

An extract of horseshoe crab blood cells, called LAL, clots when bacterial endotoxins are present, making it a very sensitive test for contamination.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/horseshoe-crabs-managing-resource-birds-bait-and-blood) · second source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8150811/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.lal-endotoxin-test` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The PMC review says LAL is an aqueous extract of Limulus polyphemus blood cells and the LAL test is the most sensitive, reliable in vitro method for detecting bacterial endotoxins; endotoxin triggers clotting in the lysate.</sub>

### dd-0130 · hard · unverified

**Which lab-made test can stand in for horseshoe crab blood when checking for bacterial toxins?**

- A. Synthetic hemoglobin
- B. Cloned insulin
- C. **Recombinant Factor C** ✓

Recombinant Factor C (rFC) is made without bleeding horseshoe crabs, and reviews have found it detects bacterial endotoxin about as well as the blood-based LAL test.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7612741/) · second source: [U.S. Food and Drug Administration](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/drug-alerts-and-statements/international-collaboration-advance-new-testing-methods-reduce-reliance-horseshoe-crab-blood)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.recombinant-factor-c` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The review found rFC equivalent to or better than LAL for detecting and measuring bacterial endotoxin; FDA has assessed a recombinant endotoxin test as an alternative to LAL.</sub>

### dd-0131 · medium · unverified

**Which migrating shorebird refuels on horseshoe crab eggs on Delaware Bay beaches each spring?**

- A. The brown pelican
- B. **The red knot** ✓
- C. The snowy owl

Red knots stop at Delaware Bay in May and feast on horseshoe crab eggs, fattening up fast for the rest of their migration.

Source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/media/hungry-red-knots-feed-eggs-horseshoe-crabs-may-delaware-bay) · second source: [U.S. Geological Survey](https://www.usgs.gov/publications/stable-isotope-and-pen-feeding-trial-studies-confirm-value-horseshoe-crab-limulus)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.red-knot-eggs` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: FWS media item says red knots feed on horseshoe crab eggs in May on Delaware Bay; USGS pen-feeding trials confirmed the eggs drive rapid fattening in spring migrant shorebirds.</sub>

### dd-0132 · medium · unverified

**In the 1990s, a sharp rise in horseshoe crab harvests around Delaware Bay was driven mainly by demand for what?**

- A. **Bait for fisheries** ✓
- B. Seafood for restaurants
- C. Pets for aquariums

Horseshoe crabs were caught in large numbers for fishing bait, which cut the supply of eggs that migrating shorebirds rely on at Delaware Bay.

Source: [U.S. Geological Survey](https://www.usgs.gov/publications/effects-horseshoe-crab-harvest-delaware-bay-red-knots-are-harvest-restrictions-working) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/horseshoe-crabs-managing-resource-birds-bait-and-blood)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.bait-harvest` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results said biologists linked red knot declines to a tenfold rise in horseshoe crab harvesting for bait during the 1990s, which greatly reduced egg availability.</sub>

### dd-0133 · medium · unverified

**Horseshoe crab ancestors appear in the fossil record about how long ago?**

- A. About 4.5 million years ago
- B. About 45 million years ago
- C. **About 445 million years ago** ✓

Horseshoe crabs, which aren't true crabs, are often called living fossils. NOAA dates their ancestors to about 445 million years ago.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/horseshoe-crabs-managing-resource-birds-bait-and-blood) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/100-years/object/horseshoe-crab/)

Fact id: `crustaceans.horseshoe-crab.lineage-age` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA Fisheries dates horseshoe crab ancestors to around 445 million years ago; the Florida Museum says today's horseshoe crabs look much like those of 480 million years ago. Both are far from the distractors.</sub>

### dd-0134 · medium · unverified

**Barnacles have hard shells, but what kind of animal are they?**

- A. Mollusks, like clams
- B. **Crustaceans, like crabs** ✓
- C. Corals, like sea fans

Barnacles were long mistaken for mollusks because of their shells, but 19th-century scientists showed they are crustaceans, related to crabs and shrimp.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/places/barnacles.htm) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/resources-rx/files/10feat_secret_life_of_barnacles-3061.pdf)

Fact id: `crustaceans.barnacle.is-crustacean` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS says barnacles are crustaceans and related to crabs and shrimp; the NHM article says barnacles were once thought to be molluscs because of their shells until 19th-century scientists found they are crustaceans.</sub>

## seahorses (55)

### dd-0051 · easy · Field Guide: seabirth · unverified

**During seahorse mating, how do the eggs end up in the brood pouch?**

- A. **The female deposits them in the male's pouch** ✓
- B. The male scoops them off the seafloor
- C. The female keeps them in her own pouch

The female places her eggs in the male's pouch, where he fertilizes them and carries the young until birth.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/pregnant-seahorse) · second source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10253279/)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.egg-transfer` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary of the Smithsonian page: during mating the female deposits eggs into the male's pouch and he fertilizes them.</sub>

### dd-0052 · medium · Field Guide: seabirth · unverified

**Roughly how long after the eggs enter a male seahorse's pouch do the young typically emerge?**

- A. A couple of days
- B. **A few weeks** ✓
- C. About six months

Depending on species and water temperature, the young typically emerge from the father's pouch after a few weeks.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/10-things-you-never-knew-about-seahorses) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/pregnant-seahorse)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.pregnancy-length` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian pages say the young emerge from the male's pouch after about two weeks; one summary notes timing varies by species.</sub>

### dd-0053 · hard · Field Guide: seabirth · review

**Is a juvenile male pot-bellied seahorse (Hippocampus abdominalis) already equipped with a brood pouch?**

- A. Yes, every seahorse is born with one
- B. **No, it forms as he grows beyond the juvenile stage** ✓
- C. Yes, but it shrinks away as he matures

A study of pot-bellied seahorses found the brood pouch is absent in juveniles and develops during growth after the juvenile stage.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5646163/)

Fact id: `seahorses.pot-bellied-seahorse.pouch-development` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC study on pouch formation in H. abdominalis: the brood pouch is not present in juveniles and develops during post-juvenile growth.</sub>

### dd-0054 · medium · Field Guide: seabirth · unverified

**A pregnant male seahorse's brood pouch does more than hold the embryos. What else does it do?**

- A. Stores food for the male through winter
- B. Pumps the eggs out to hatch in open water
- C. **Supplies the embryos with nutrients and oxygen** ✓

Researchers compare the pouch to a mammal's placenta: it nourishes and aerates the embryos, regulates their salt balance and helps protect them from disease.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10253279/)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.pouch-placenta` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC review: male seahorses carry embryos in a brood pouch that incubates and nourishes them, similar to a placenta, supplying nutrients and oxygen; it provides protection, aeration, osmoregulation, nutrition and immune defense.</sub>

### dd-0055 · medium · unverified

**In the family that includes seahorses, pipefishes and seadragons, which group has the most complex brood pouch?**

- A. Leafy seadragons
- B. **Seahorses** ✓
- C. Trunk-brooding pipefishes

Seahorses have the most elaborate, enclosed pouch in the family. Some relatives simply carry eggs glued to their skin or tucked under skin flaps.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10253279/) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-or-seadragon-what-s-the-difference)

Fact id: `seahorses.syngnathids.most-complex-pouch` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC review: among syngnathids, seahorses (Hippocampus) have the most complex brood pouches; others have eggs attached to skin or covered by skin flaps. Shedd notes seadragons lack a pouch.</sub>

### dd-0056 · hard · unverified

**A genetic study found that seahorses functionally lack which organ, a change linked to immune gene losses?**

- A. **The spleen** ✓
- B. The liver
- C. The heart

Researchers found seahorses have functional asplenia (no working spleen), part of a set of immune gene losses that arose alongside male pregnancy.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9734139/)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.no-spleen` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Title and summary of PMC paper: immunogenetic losses co-occurred with seahorse male pregnancy, and a mutation in tlx1 accompanied functional asplenia (lack of a spleen).</sub>

### dd-0057 · hard · unverified

**What did researchers find inside the brood pouch of pregnant pot-bellied seahorses (Hippocampus abdominalis)?**

- A. No microbes of any kind
- B. **A microbe community distinct from the skin** ✓
- C. Exactly the same microbes as the skin

The pouch hosts its own community of microbes that differs from the male's skin, and that community shifts over the course of pregnancy.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11906128/)

Fact id: `seahorses.pot-bellied-seahorse.pouch-microbiome` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC study title: changes to the reproductive microbiome of the brood pouch during male pregnancy in H. abdominalis; summary says the pouch has its own microbiome differing from the male's skin.</sub>

### dd-0058 · easy · Field Guide: eyes · unverified

**Like a chameleon, what can a seahorse do with its eyes?**

- A. Retract them into its head
- B. **Move each one independently** ✓
- C. Close them with eyelids

Each eye can swivel on its own, letting a seahorse track prey with one eye while watching elsewhere with the other. NOAA notes this aids feeding accuracy.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/exhibits/tropical_pacific_gallery/what_are_seahorses_and_seadragons) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/s3/dam-migration/2017-dwarf-seahorse-status-review-final-508-1-.pdf)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.independent-eyes` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: their eyes can move independently like a chameleon's to help spot prey. NOAA dwarf seahorse review: eyes move independently, allowing better accuracy during feeding.</sub>

### dd-0059 · medium · Field Guide: hitch · unverified

**What unusual shape does a seahorse's tail have in cross-section?**

- A. **Square** ✓
- B. Perfectly round
- C. Flat like a ribbon

A 2015 study suggested the square cross-section could improve the tail's grip by increasing how much of it touches the object it holds.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/are-seahorses-the-quirkiest-fish.html)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.square-tail` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM article: seahorse tails are square in cross-section; a 2015 study suggested this could enhance grip by increasing surface area in contact with an object.</sub>

### dd-0060 · easy · Field Guide: hitch · unverified

**What do seahorses mainly use their flexible, monkey-like tails for?**

- A. Swimming in fast bursts
- B. Stinging predators
- C. **Holding on to seagrass and corals** ✓

Seahorses are weak swimmers, so they anchor themselves by wrapping their grasping tails around seagrass, corals and other objects.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/seahorses-have-monkey-tails) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-or-seadragon-what-s-the-difference)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.prehensile-tail` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: seahorses use monkey-like (prehensile) tails to hold onto corals, seagrass and other objects. Shedd: seahorses are poor swimmers that latch on with prehensile tails.</sub>

### dd-0061 · medium · Field Guide: hitch · unverified

**How can seahorses travel across the ocean farther than they could ever swim?**

- A. By swimming in fast-moving schools
- B. By burrowing along the seafloor
- C. **By clinging to floating seaweed and debris** ✓

Seahorses can hitch a ride by gripping drifting seaweed or debris, letting currents carry them long distances.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/hitchhiking-seahorses)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.rafting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: seahorses can travel long distances, farther than they can swim, by attaching themselves to floating seaweed and debris.</sub>

### dd-0062 · easy · Field Guide: blend · unverified

**Seahorses can change color to blend in. Besides hiding from predators, how does this help them?**

- A. It keeps their bodies warm
- B. **It lets them ambush prey** ✓
- C. It attracts cleaner shrimp

Shifting color to match their surroundings helps seahorses avoid predators and also lets them lie in wait for passing prey.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/10-things-you-never-knew-about-seahorses)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.color-change-ambush` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 10 Things: seahorses can change color to blend in, which helps them ambush prey and avoid predators.</sub>

### dd-0063 · medium · unverified

**Instead of scales, what covers a seahorse's body?**

- A. A crab-like outer shell
- B. Only a coat of slime
- C. **Bony plates under thin skin** ✓

Seahorses have no scales. Their armor is a series of bony plates sitting just beneath a thin layer of skin.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/bigbelly-seahorse)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.no-scales` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian (bigbelly seahorse page): seahorses lack scales and have bony plates under a thin skin.</sub>

### dd-0064 · medium · unverified

**About how fast can a seahorse's small dorsal fin flutter?**

- A. About once a second
- B. About once a minute
- C. **Dozens of times a second** ✓

The dorsal fin can beat dozens of times a second, too fast for the eye to follow.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/exhibits/tropical_pacific_gallery/what_are_seahorses_and_seadragons)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.fin-beat-rate` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: seahorses beat their fins up to forty times a second, too fast to be visible to the human eye.</sub>

### dd-0065 · medium · unverified

**Adult seahorses lack a fin that most fish rely on. Which one?**

- A. **The tail (caudal) fin** ✓
- B. The fin on the back (dorsal fin)
- C. The fins behind the head (pectoral fins)

Seahorses lost the tail fin over evolution; a skeletal study traced its progressive loss. They swim with dorsal and pectoral fins instead.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4407769/) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-or-seadragon-what-s-the-difference)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.no-caudal-fin` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC study title: comparative developmental osteology of the seahorse skeleton reveals progressive caudal fin loss. Other sources note dorsal and pectoral fins are used for swimming.</sub>

### dd-0066 · hard · unverified

**What sound do seahorses typically make while feeding and courting?**

- A. **Sharp clicks** ✓
- B. Long, high whistles
- C. Deep, booming calls

Seahorses make clicking sounds, produced by bones at the back of the skull, when they eat and during courtship.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/10-things-you-never-knew-about-seahorses)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.sounds` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 10 Things: seahorses make lip-smacking sounds during feeding and courtship.</sub>

### dd-0067 · easy · Field Guide: snick · unverified

**How does a seahorse catch its tiny prey?**

- A. It bites prey with sharp teeth
- B. **It flicks its head up and sucks prey in** ✓
- C. It stuns prey with an electric pulse

In a strike called pivot feeding, the seahorse rotates its long snout up toward the prey very fast, then widens the snout to suck it in.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2665837)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.pivot-feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC (newborn seahorses): adult seahorses capture prey by rotating the long-snouted head quickly toward it, then generating powerful suction by widening the snout.</sub>

### dd-0068 · hard · Field Guide: snick · unverified

**Can newborn seahorses of the species Hippocampus reidi already use the fast head-flick-and-suction strike?**

- A. No, they filter-feed for months
- B. No, their father feeds them at first
- C. **Yes, they can from birth** ✓

Researchers found newborn H. reidi already feed with a rapid snout rotation and high-volume suction, powered by elastic recoil like the adults.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2665837)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.newborn-feeding` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC 'Suction is kid's play': newborn Hippocampus reidi already feed with rapid snout rotation and high-volume suction using elastic recoil.</sub>

### dd-0069 · medium · Field Guide: snick · unverified

**What powers much of a seahorse's ultra-fast feeding strike?**

- A. **Elastic energy stored in tendons** ✓
- B. A jet of water from its gills
- C. Muscles in its curled tail

Like a drawn bow, a seahorse stores elastic energy in tendons and releases it at once, letting the head snap toward prey faster than muscle alone could.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8443865/) · second source: [PubMed (peer-reviewed)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37040808/)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.elastic-strike` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC: elastic energy storage in seahorses leads to unique suction flow. PubMed abstract: tendons in the sternohyoideus supply on average about 72% of the suction power.</sub>

### dd-0070 · hard · Field Guide: snick · review

**In seahorses, what comes with having a longer snout?**

- A. Slower head rotation and faster suction
- B. **Faster head rotation but slower suction** ✓
- C. Stronger teeth for crushing prey

A study found a trade-off: longer snouts swing toward prey faster, but the suction flow they generate is slower.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8443865/)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.snout-length-tradeoff` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: PMC study: in seahorses, longer snouts were linked to faster head rotation but slower suction flows, a trade-off between pivot and suction feeding.</sub>

### dd-0071 · easy · unverified

**What do seahorses lack that many other fish use for eating?**

- A. **Teeth** ✓
- B. Gills
- C. Eyes

Seahorses have no teeth. They suck up tiny prey such as small shrimp and other plankton whole.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/are-seahorses-the-quirkiest-fish.html)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.no-teeth` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM: seahorses have no teeth and feed by sucking up tiny planktonic prey such as small shrimp.</sub>

### dd-0072 · easy · Field Guide: dance · unverified

**In seahorse species that form pairs, when do partners typically perform their greeting dance?**

- A. Once a year at full moon
- B. Only after dark
- C. **Every morning** ✓

Bonded pairs meet each morning to promenade and pirouette together, sometimes changing color, before going their separate ways for the day.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/dancing-seahorse-pairs)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.morning-greeting` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: couples greet each other every morning with a dance that sometimes involves color change, then separate for the rest of the day.</sub>

### dd-0073 · medium · Field Guide: dance · unverified

**According to Shedd Aquarium, how long does a seahorse pair's daily greeting display last?**

- A. About 6 to 10 seconds
- B. **About 6 to 10 minutes** ✓
- C. About 3 days

Partners twirl around a holdfast such as seagrass, then promenade tail in tail. The display lasts 6 to 10 minutes and ends when the female swims off to feed.

Source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-valentines-together-forever)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.greeting-duration` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Shedd: pairs entwine tails around a holdfast and twirl, then promenade; the display lasts 6 to 10 minutes, ending when the female swims away to forage.</sub>

### dd-0074 · medium · Field Guide: dance · unverified

**Besides reinforcing the pair bond, what does the seahorse daily greeting appear to do?**

- A. **Bring the pair into reproductive sync** ✓
- B. Warm their bodies in the sun
- C. Drive away rival fish

The ritual seems to synchronize the pair's reproductive cycles, and Smithsonian notes it may also confirm that the partner is still alive.

Source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-valentines-together-forever) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/dancing-seahorse-pairs)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.greeting-sync` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Shedd: the greeting ritual reinforces the pair's relationship and appears to bring male and female into reproductive synchrony. Smithsonian: they greet to confirm partner is alive, reinforce the bond and synchronize reproductive cycles.</sub>

### dd-0075 · hard · Field Guide: dance · unverified

**Before the female transfers her eggs, potbelly seahorse pairs may swim together with tails intertwined for up to how long?**

- A. Eight seconds
- B. **Eight hours** ✓
- C. Eight days

This marathon courtship lets the pair line up so the male is ready at the exact moment the female deposits her eggs.

Source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-or-seadragon-what-s-the-difference)

Fact id: `seahorses.potbelly-seahorse.courtship-length` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Shedd: potbelly seahorses intertwine tails and swim together for up to eight hours before the female deposits eggs.</sub>

### dd-0076 · medium · unverified

**Smithsonian Ocean notes that many seahorse species keep the same partner for how long?**

- A. **A single breeding season** ✓
- B. A single day
- C. Exactly ten years

Many species stay faithful for one breeding season. Lifelong pairing is the exception, believed to occur in one Australian species.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/it-love-why-some-ocean-animals-sort-mate-life)

Fact id: `seahorses.seahorse.pair-bond-length` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian results: many seahorse species bond for a single breeding season, and one Australian species is believed to mate for life.</sub>

### dd-0077 · medium · Field Guide: friend · unverified

**How do White's seahorses (Hippocampus whitei) of eastern Australia pair up?**

- A. They pick a new partner every day
- B. Females raise the young alone
- C. **They form long-lasting pairs** ✓

White's seahorses form long-term pairs; researchers in Port Stephens and Sydney Harbour followed pairs that stayed together for multiple breeding seasons.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/whites-seahorse-hippocampus-whitei-bleeker-1855/) · second source: [PubMed (peer-reviewed)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22957871/)

Fact id: `seahorses.whites-seahorse.permanent-pairs` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum: White's seahorse forms permanent pairs. PubMed 2012 study reported life-long monogamy, with three pairs staying bonded over three consecutive breeding years.</sub>

### dd-0078 · hard · unverified

**In Sydney Harbour, which human-made structures became important habitat for endangered White's seahorses?**

- A. Oil-rig legs
- B. Floating plastic bags
- C. **Nets around harbour swimming areas** ✓

With much of their natural habitat lost, White's seahorses in Sydney Harbour came to rely heavily on the nets that enclose harbour swimming areas.

Source: [NSW Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development](https://www.dpird.nsw.gov.au/fishing/threatened-species/what-current/endangered-species2/whites-seahorse)

Fact id: `seahorses.whites-seahorse.swimming-nets` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search summary (NSW government source): in Sydney Harbour, White's seahorse is now predominantly found on man-made swimming nets; habitat loss is the main threat.</sub>

### dd-0079 · medium · unverified

**Satomi's pygmy seahorse, one of the tiniest seahorses, is about how long?**

- A. About 13 centimeters
- B. About 30 centimeters
- C. **About 13 millimeters** ✓

Satomi's pygmy seahorse measures only about 13 mm. The big-bellied seahorse, by contrast, can grow past 35 cm.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/10-things-you-never-knew-about-seahorses)

Fact id: `seahorses.satomis-pygmy-seahorse.size` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 10 Things: Satomi's pygmy seahorse is about 13 mm long; the big-bellied seahorse can exceed 35 cm.</sub>

### dd-0080 · medium · unverified

- A. Over 2 meters
- B. **More than 35 centimeters** ✓
- C. About 3 centimeters

Big-bellied seahorses can exceed 35 cm (about 14 inches), many times the length of the smallest pygmy seahorses.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/10-things-you-never-knew-about-seahorses)

- A. **Gorgonian sea fans** ✓
- B. Giant kelp

This species is found on gorgonian sea fans, mainly of the genus Muricella, and spends its life clinging to them.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/pygmy-seahorse-hippocampus-bargibanti-whitley-1970/) · second source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12415253/)

### dd-0082 · medium · unverified

**How does Bargibant's pygmy seahorse blend in with its coral home?**

- A. It buries itself in sand
- B. **Its bumpy tubercles match the coral's polyps** ✓
- C. It hides inside empty shells

Its body is covered in bulbous bumps that take on the shape and color of the sea fan's polyps, making it nearly invisible.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/pygmy-seahorse-hippocampus-bargibanti-whitley-1970/) · second source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12415253/)

- A. About 50 centimeters

### dd-0085 · hard · review

**A 2025 genome study linked the pygmy seahorse's life on corals to the loss of which kind of genes?**

- B. Genes for hearing

Researchers found that losing immune genes and remodeling the body plan helped the pygmy seahorse live in close partnership with corals and mimic them.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12415253/)

### dd-0086 · hard · review

**Photos of a male red pipefish carrying eggs settled a long-standing question. What did they show?**

- A. Females carry the eggs, not males
- B. **It carries its eggs on its trunk, not its tail** ✓
- C. It gives birth without any eggs

Scientists had suspected the red pipefish was a tail brooder, but Australian Museum photos showed a male carrying eggs on his trunk (belly region).

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/blog/amri-news/rare-male-pipefish-carrying-eggs-spotted-in-sydney/)

Fact id: `seahorses.red-pipefish.trunk-brooder` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum blog: scientists suspected the red pipefish was a tail brooder; new photos of a brooding male showed eggs on its trunk, making it a trunk brooder.</sub>

### dd-0087 · medium · unverified

**How many eggs can a large male short-tail pipefish carry in his belly pouch?**

- A. Only two
- B. Around a million
- C. **Several hundred** ✓

Male short-tail pipefish carry the eggs in a pouch on the belly, and large males can hold several hundred at once.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/short-tail-river-pipefish-microphis-brachyurus-bleeker-1853/)

Fact id: `seahorses.short-tail-pipefish.egg-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum: short-tail pipefish males carry eggs in a belly pouch; large males can carry several hundred eggs.</sub>

### dd-0088 · hard · review

- B. Yes, survival roughly doubled

Surprisingly, the researchers found that embryo survival and growth did not improve with a pouch.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4864203/)

Fact id: `seahorses.pipefish.pouch-no-advantage` · risk: surprising

### dd-0089 · medium · unverified

Unlike a seahorse, a leafy seadragon cannot coil its tail, so it drifts and steers with tiny fins instead of anchoring.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/leafy_seadragon) · second source: [Shedd Aquarium](https://www.sheddaquarium.org/stories/seahorse-or-seadragon-what-s-the-difference)

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific leafy seadragon page: its tail cannot be coiled. Shedd: seadragons mostly drift and use tiny fins to steer.</sub>

- A. New Zealand
- B. South Africa
- C. **Australia** ✓

Leafy seadragons live only in the cool waters off southern and western Australia, usually on kelp-covered rocky reefs.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/leafy-seadragon-phycodurus-eques/) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/leafy_seadragon)

- A. Propelling it through the water
- B. Holding its eggs
- C. **Camouflage among seaweed** ✓

The frilly appendages make it look like drifting algae, so well that divers have missed one swimming right in front of their masks.

- B. **They feed almost constantly** ✓
- C. They digest food in the brood patch

Without a stomach to store food, leafy seadragons have to keep eating nearly all the time.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/leafy_seadragon) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/leafy-sea-dragon)

- A. **Mysid shrimp** ✓
- B. Sea urchins
- C. Kelp fronds

They mainly slurp up mysids, tiny shrimp-like animals that live among algae and seagrass.

- C. **The common seadragon** ✓

The Australian Museum lists Phyllopteryx taeniolatus as the common seadragon. It lives on temperate reefs of southern Australia.

Shedd Aquarium reports courting weedy seadragons copying each other's moves for up to a full day.

There are three: the leafy, the weedy (common) and the ruby seadragon, all from Australian waters.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/weedy_seadragon1/) · second source: [Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego](https://scripps.ucsd.edu/news/new-species-ruby-seadragon-discovered-scripps-researchers)

- A. Divers filmed a strange red fish
- B. It hatched from eggs in an aquarium
- C. **DNA analysis of museum specimens** ✓

Scripps researchers analyzed tissue samples supplied by the Western Australian Museum and found DNA evidence of a new species, described in 2015.

Source: [Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego](https://scripps.ucsd.edu/news/new-species-ruby-seadragon-discovered-scripps-researchers) · second source: [UC San Diego Today](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/new_species_the_ruby_seadragon_discovered_by_scripps_researchers)

Fact id: `seahorses.ruby-seadragon.dna-discovery` · risk: none

### dd-0102 · hard · unverified

**Before 2015, preserved ruby seadragon specimens had been mistaken for which animal?**

- A. **Common (weedy) seadragons** ✓
- B. Pipefishes
- C. Seahorses

Museum specimens collected decades earlier had been filed as common (weedy) seadragons until DNA work in 2015 showed they were a separate species.

Source: [Western Australian Museum](https://museum.wa.gov.au/research/collections/aquatic-zoology/fish-ichthyology-section/meet-ruby-wa-s-new-seadragon-discovery-s-been-100-years-making)

Fact id: `seahorses.ruby-seadragon.misidentified` · risk: none

### dd-0103 · medium · unverified

**Why might the ruby seadragon's deep red color help it hide?**

- A. It glows to mimic jellyfish
- B. **Red light is absorbed in deeper water** ✓
- C. It reflects sunlight like a mirror

Red light fades out quickly with depth, so a red animal can look dark and hard to see. Researchers suggest this is why the ruby seadragon is red.

Source: [Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego](https://scripps.ucsd.edu/news/new-species-ruby-seadragon-discovered-scripps-researchers)

Fact id: `seahorses.ruby-seadragon.red-camouflage` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Scripps: the team suggests the red color may serve as camouflage in deeper water, where red light is absorbed.</sub>

- A. **Off Western Australia** ✓
- B. Off Hawaii
- C. Off South Africa

Researchers filmed them near the Recherche Archipelago off Western Australia, in water more than 50 meters deep.

Source: [Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego](https://scripps.ucsd.edu/news/research-highlight-researchers-capture-first-glimpse-ruby-seadragons-wild)

Fact id: `seahorses.ruby-seadragon.first-wild-sighting` · risk: none

- C. They are only a centimeter long

The ruby seadragon has no leafy appendages, a feature that had been thought to define all seadragons.

Source: [Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego](https://scripps.ucsd.edu/news/research-highlight-researchers-capture-first-glimpse-ruby-seadragons-wild)
