## corals-reefs (55)

### dd-0307 · easy · unverified

**Corals stay fixed to the seafloor like plants. What are they actually?**

- A. **Animals** ✓
- B. Plants
- C. Algae

Corals are invertebrate animals. They look plant-like because they are anchored in place, but unlike plants they do not make their own food by photosynthesis.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/coral-animal.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/what-coral-coral-polyp-and-zooxanthellae)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.is-animal` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Exploration says corals are invertebrate animals in phylum Cnidaria, often mistaken for plants because they are attached to the seafloor; they do not photosynthesize themselves.</sub>

### dd-0308 · easy · unverified

**What is each of the many tiny individual animals that make up a coral colony called?**

- A. A medusa
- B. **A polyp** ✓
- C. A frond

What we call a coral is usually hundreds or thousands of small animals called polyps, living together as one colony.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/coral-animal.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/what-coral-coral-polyp-and-zooxanthellae)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.polyp-unit` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says a coral is made up of hundreds to thousands of individual animals called polyps.</sub>

### dd-0309 · easy · unverified

**Corals belong to the phylum Cnidaria. Which of these animals is also in that group?**

- A. Sponges
- B. Sea stars
- C. **Jellyfish** ✓

Corals share the phylum Cnidaria with jellyfish and sea anemones, animals that all carry stinging cells.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/coral-animal.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.phylum-cnidaria` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Exploration places corals in phylum Cnidaria and says they share it with sea anemones and jellies.</sub>

### dd-0310 · medium · unverified

**What are the zooxanthellae that live inside reef-building corals?**

- A. **Single-celled algae** ✓
- B. Tiny shrimp larvae
- C. Skeleton-building bacteria

Zooxanthellae are microscopic algae living in coral tissue. They photosynthesize and pass food to the coral, which gives them shelter in return.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/education/tutorial_corals/media/supp_coral02a.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/what-coral-coral-polyp-and-zooxanthellae)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.zooxanthellae.what-they-are` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's corals tutorial and Smithsonian Ocean describe zooxanthellae as single-celled algae living in coral tissue that supply the coral with food made by photosynthesis.</sub>

### dd-0311 · hard · unverified

**Up to roughly what share of the food made by a coral's zooxanthellae is passed to the coral?**

- A. About 40 percent
- B. **About 90 percent** ✓
- C. About 10 percent

NOAA estimates that as much as 90 percent of the organic material the algae make by photosynthesis goes to the host coral's tissue.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://cdn.oceanservice.noaa.gov/oceanserviceprod/education/tutorial_corals/lessons/corals_tutorial.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.zooxanthellae.food-share` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial says as much as 90 percent of the organic material produced photosynthetically by zooxanthellae is transferred to the coral tissue. (A Smithsonian-linked source cites up to 95%, also far from the distractors.)</sub>

### dd-0312 · medium · unverified

**Where does much of the color of many reef-building corals come from?**

- A. Its limestone skeleton
- B. Pigment from prey it eats
- C. **Algae living in its tissue** ✓

Zooxanthellae algae living in coral tissue supply much of many corals' color, which is why corals that lose them turn pale or white. Some bright colors come from the coral's own pigments.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/coral_bleach.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/zooxanthellae-and-coral-bleaching)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.color-source` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA's coral bleaching page says the algae living in coral tissue give corals their color; without them the white skeleton shows.</sub>

### dd-0313 · easy · unverified

**What is the most common cause of coral bleaching?**

- A. **Unusually warm water** ✓
- B. A lack of moonlight
- C. Too many reef fish

When water gets too warm, stressed corals expel the algae living in their tissues and turn white. Changes in light or nutrients can also trigger it.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/coral_bleach.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/zooxanthellae-and-coral-bleaching)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.bleaching.main-cause` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says corals stressed by changes in temperature, light or nutrients expel their symbiotic algae, and that warm water is the most common cause.</sub>

### dd-0314 · easy · unverified

**What is true of a coral that has just bleached white?**

- A. It has already died
- B. **It is alive but stressed** ✓
- C. It has become a new species

A bleached coral is not dead and can recover if conditions improve. If bleaching is severe or lasts too long, though, the coral can die.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/coral_bleach.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.bleaching.still-alive` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says a bleached coral is not dead and can survive bleaching, but becomes more vulnerable and may die if the stress is prolonged.</sub>

### dd-0315 · hard · unverified

**Besides heat, which of these has caused coral bleaching in the Florida Keys?**

- A. Bright full moonlight
- B. Large schools of fish
- C. **Unusually cold water** ✓

Bleaching is a stress response, and cold snaps can trigger it too. A cold-water event in the Florida Keys bleached corals and killed some of them.

Source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/pgallery/pgflorida/living/living_13.html) · second source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/coral_bleach.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.bleaching.cold-water` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: The Florida Keys NMS bleaching page says cold water temperatures caused a coral bleaching event that resulted in some coral death.</sub>

### dd-0316 · medium · unverified

**Many reef corals spawn by releasing eggs and sperm in what form?**

- A. **Bundles that float upward** ✓
- B. Egg cases glued to rock
- C. Live young that crawl away

Many spawning corals pack eggs and sperm together into small buoyant bundles. These float to the surface and break apart so eggs can be fertilized.

Source: [NOAA Flower Garden Banks National Marine Sanctuary](https://flowergarden.noaa.gov/education/coralspawning.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ecosystems/coral-reefs/coral-spawning)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.spawning.egg-sperm-bundles` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Flower Garden Banks says many hermaphroditic broadcast spawners release bundles of eggs and sperm that float to the surface and break apart.</sub>

### dd-0317 · hard · unverified

**At the Flower Garden Banks in the Gulf, when does the main coral spawning usually happen?**

- A. 1 to 2 nights before August's full moon
- B. **7 to 10 nights after August's full moon** ✓
- C. About 30 nights after August's full moon

Spawning there is tied to the moon. Different species release eggs and sperm on different nights in the week or so after the August full moon.

Source: [NOAA Flower Garden Banks National Marine Sanctuary](https://flowergarden.noaa.gov/education/spawningobservations.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.spawning.flower-garden-timing` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA Flower Garden Banks spawning observations say the main event occurs about seven to ten nights after the August full moon, with species spawning on different nights.</sub>

### dd-0318 · hard · unverified

**Elkhorn coral in the Caribbean spawns for just a few nights each summer. At about what time?**

- A. Around midday
- B. Just after sunrise
- C. **About three hours after sunset** ✓

Elkhorn coral spawns on only a few summer nights after a full moon, typically about three hours after sunset, so researchers must be ready to collect it.

Source: [Smithsonian's National Zoo](https://nationalzoo.si.edu/news/spawning-hope)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.elkhorn.spawn-time` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian's National Zoo says elkhorn coral spawns only a few nights each summer after a full moon, typically about three hours after sunset.</sub>

### dd-0319 · hard · unverified

**Some coral species are 'gonochoric.' What does that mean?**

- A. **Each colony is either male or female** ✓
- B. They reproduce only by budding
- C. They spawn only in winter

In gonochoric corals, female colonies release egg bundles and male colonies release clouds of sperm, rather than each colony making both.

Source: [NOAA Flower Garden Banks National Marine Sanctuary](https://flowergarden.noaa.gov/education/coralspawning.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.spawning.separate-sexes` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Flower Garden Banks says some species are gonochoric: female colonies release egg bundles while male colonies release sperm.</sub>

### dd-0320 · medium · unverified

**What is a free-swimming coral larva called?**

- A. A nauplius
- B. **A planula** ✓
- C. A veliger

A fertilized coral egg develops into a tiny larva called a planula, which drifts and swims until it finds a place to settle and become a polyp.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/education/tutorial_corals/coral06_reproduction.html) · second source: [NOAA Coral Disease & Health Consortium](https://cdhc.noaa.gov/coral-biology/coral-reproduction/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.planula-name` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial and the NOAA CDHC reproduction page describe coral larvae as planulae that settle and metamorphose into polyps.</sub>

### dd-0321 · hard · unverified

**What is thought to be the strongest cue telling a coral larva where to settle?**

- A. Warmth from sunlit sand
- B. The shadow of passing fish
- C. **A chemical from coralline algae** ✓

Larvae respond to several signals, but a chemical released by certain crusty, pink coralline algae is considered the most potent settlement cue.

Source: [NOAA Coral Disease & Health Consortium](https://cdhc.noaa.gov/coral-biology/coral-reproduction/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.coralline-algae-cue` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA CDHC says a variety of cues guide larvae to suitable substrate, and the most potent is a chemical signal released by certain crustose coralline algae.</sub>

### dd-0322 · hard · unverified

**In experiments, settling coral larvae moved toward which reef cue?**

- A. **Reef sounds from fish and shrimp** ✓
- B. Magnetic pulses from rocks
- C. Scent of sea turtles

In experiments, settling coral larvae swam toward recorded reef sounds, which are made mainly by fish and crustaceans such as snapping shrimp.

Source: [PLOS ONE via NCBI PMC](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2871043/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.reef-sounds` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: PLOS ONE study: settling coral larvae were attracted to reef sounds produced mainly by fish and crustaceans, the first auditory response described in Cnidaria.</sub>

### dd-0323 · medium · unverified

**For most coral species, how soon do larvae settle after spawning?**

- A. After about six months
- B. **Within days** ✓
- C. After about a year

Most coral larvae settle within days of spawning, though some can keep swimming for weeks before choosing a spot.

Source: [NOAA Coral Disease & Health Consortium](https://cdhc.noaa.gov/coral-biology/coral-reproduction/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.settle-timing` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA CDHC says in most species larvae settle within two days, though some swim for up to three weeks, and one known case two months.</sub>

### dd-0324 · hard · unverified

**Compared with broadcast-spawned larvae, larvae released by brooding corals usually do what?**

- A. Drift for months across oceans
- B. Never leave the parent colony
- C. **Settle sooner and nearer the parent** ✓

Brooding corals keep developing young inside, so their larvae are released ready to settle quickly and tend to land close to the parent colony.

Source: [NOAA Coral Disease & Health Consortium](https://cdhc.noaa.gov/coral-biology/coral-reproduction/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.brooder-settlement` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA CDHC says brooding species release larvae that are ready to settle sooner and usually settle closer to the parent colony.</sub>

### dd-0325 · medium · unverified

**How does a coral larva's swimming speed compare with the water currents around reefs?**

- A. **Far slower than the currents** ✓
- B. Much faster than the currents
- C. About the same speed

A review found coral larvae swim orders of magnitude slower than reef water flow, so they have very little ability to steer themselves to a settling spot.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5440398/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.larvae.poor-swimmers` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed meta-analysis: coral larval swimming speeds are orders of magnitude lower than water flow on and off reefs, so navigation ability is extremely limited.</sub>

### dd-0326 · medium · unverified

**A broken piece of coral reattaches and keeps growing. What is this an example of?**

- A. Mass spawning
- B. **Asexual reproduction** ✓
- C. Coral bleaching

Corals can reproduce asexually by budding new polyps or by fragmentation, when a broken piece survives and grows into a new colony.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/education/tutorial_corals/coral06_reproduction.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.asexual.fragmentation` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial reproduction page describes asexual reproduction in corals by budding and by fragmentation of broken pieces.</sub>

### dd-0327 · easy · unverified

**What do stony corals build their hard skeletons from?**

- A. Silica, like glass
- B. Keratin, like hair
- C. **Calcium carbonate (limestone)** ✓

Each stony coral polyp secretes a skeleton of calcium carbonate. Over many generations these skeletons build the massive structure of a reef.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/coralmadeof.html) · second source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.skeleton-material` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Service says reef structures form when each stony coral polyp secretes a skeleton of calcium carbonate.</sub>

### dd-0328 · medium · unverified

**When do most coral polyps stretch out their tentacles to feed?**

- A. **At night** ✓
- B. At midday
- C. Only at low tide

Most coral polyps feed at night, emerging from their skeletons to snag drifting zooplankton. By day, many rely on food from their algae.

Source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html) · second source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://cdn.oceanservice.noaa.gov/oceanserviceprod/education/tutorial_corals/lessons/corals_tutorial.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.night-feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Florida Keys and the NOAA corals tutorial say most polyps feed at night, coming out of their skeletons to catch floating prey.</sub>

### dd-0329 · easy · unverified

**What do coral polyps use to capture prey such as zooplankton?**

- A. Tiny teeth around the mouth
- B. **Stinging cells on their tentacles** ✓
- C. Sticky silk threads

Coral tentacles carry stinging cells called nematocysts. When prey brushes against them, they fire and help hold the food.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://cdn.oceanservice.noaa.gov/oceanserviceprod/education/tutorial_corals/lessons/corals_tutorial.pdf) · second source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.nematocysts` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial says nematocysts are stinging cells in coral tentacles and outer tissues used to capture food, triggered when prey contacts the tentacles.</sub>

### dd-0330 · hard · unverified

**Besides catching prey with tentacles, how can some corals collect fine food particles?**

- A. By filtering water through gills
- B. By absorbing them through roots
- C. **By trapping them in mucus** ✓

Some corals produce mucus that traps fine bits of organic matter, which they then draw into their mouths.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://cdn.oceanservice.noaa.gov/oceanserviceprod/education/tutorial_corals/lessons/corals_tutorial.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.coral.mucus-feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial says some corals trap fine organic particles in mucus, then draw them into their mouths.</sub>

### dd-0331 · medium · unverified

**Deep-sea corals live where sunlight does not reach. How do they get food?**

- A. **Catching plankton and particles drifting past** ✓
- B. Using algae that glow in the dark
- C. Absorbing minerals from rock

Deep-sea corals do not host the sunlight-using algae of shallow reefs. They feed on plankton and organic matter sinking from above or carried by currents.

Source: [NOAA National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2025/deep-sea-corals.html) · second source: [Smithsonian](https://3d.si.edu/corals/depth-distribution)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.deep-sea.feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search results: cold-water corals like Lophelia lack symbiotic algae and feed on sinking particulate matter and organic matter carried by currents. Verify which of the two pages states it.</sub>

### dd-0332 · medium · unverified

**How many tentacles does each polyp of a soft coral (octocoral) have?**

- A. Five
- B. **Eight** ✓
- C. Ten

Soft corals are also called octocorals because each polyp has eight tentacles, which are often feathery.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/education/tutorial_corals/media/supp_coral03c.html) · second source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.soft-coral.eight-tentacles` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA corals tutorial and Florida Keys pages say soft coral polyps have eight (often feathery, pinnate) tentacles.</sub>

### dd-0333 · medium · unverified

**Stony coral polyps grow tentacles in multiples of which number?**

- A. Five
- B. Eight
- C. **Six** ✓

Hard, reef-building corals have tentacles in multiples of six, such as 6, 12 or 18, which sets them apart from eight-tentacled soft corals.

Source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.stony-coral.six-multiples` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Florida Keys page says stony coral polyps have tentacles in multiples of six (6, 12, 18...).</sub>

### dd-0334 · hard · unverified

**Instead of rigid limestone, what supports the flexible skeleton of a sea fan?**

- A. **A flexible core of gorgonin** ✓
- B. A shell of silica glass
- C. Hollow tubes of bone

Sea fans and their relatives the sea whips have a bendable internal skeleton built around gorgonin, a tough protein, which lets them sway in currents.

Source: [NOAA Florida Keys National Marine Sanctuary](https://floridakeys.noaa.gov/corals/coralreefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.gorgonian.gorgonin-skeleton` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA Florida Keys page says soft corals have a more flexible skeleton with a gorgonin core rather than the rigid calcium carbonate of stony corals.</sub>

### dd-0335 · hard · unverified

**Black corals are often mistaken for soft corals. How many tentacles do their polyps have?**

- A. Eight
- B. **Six** ✓
- C. Twelve

Despite looking like soft corals, black corals are hexacorals with six tentacles per polyp, unlike the eight-tentacled octocorals.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/get-involved/amri/amri-seminars-and-lectures/species-identification-of-black-corals/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.black-coral.hexacoral` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum says black corals have six tentacles, which is why they are often mistaken for soft corals.</sub>

### dd-0336 · medium · unverified

**Fire coral is a stinging reef builder. What kind of animal is it?**

- A. A type of reef sponge
- B. A colonial sea squirt
- C. **A hydrozoan, not a true coral** ✓

Fire corals build hard skeletons like corals but are hydrozoans, a different cnidarian class. Their name comes from their painful sting.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/southflorida/habitats/corals/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.fire-coral.hydrozoan` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Florida Museum page on South Florida corals describes fire corals (Millepora) as hydrozoans rather than true (scleractinian) corals, named for their stinging polyps.</sub>

### dd-0337 · hard · unverified

**Corals and sea anemones belong to which class of animals?**

- A. **Anthozoa** ✓
- B. Hydrozoa
- C. Scyphozoa

Corals and sea anemones are grouped together in the class Anthozoa, a name meaning 'flower animals.' Jellyfish and hydroids belong to other classes.

Source: [Smithsonian's National Zoo](https://nationalzoo.si.edu/animals/corals-and-sea-anemones-anthozoa)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.anthozoa.class` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian's National Zoo page is titled 'Corals and sea anemones (anthozoa)', grouping both in class Anthozoa.</sub>

### dd-0338 · medium · unverified

**Which type of coral reef is the most common?**

- A. Barrier reef
- B. **Fringing reef** ✓
- C. Atoll

Fringing reefs grow seaward directly from the shore, bordering coastlines and islands. They are the most common of the three main reef types.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/threecorals.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.reef-types.fringing-most-common` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the three main types are fringing, barrier and atoll, and fringing reefs are the most common, growing seaward directly from shore.</sub>

### dd-0339 · easy · unverified

**What is a ring-shaped coral reef with an open lagoon in the middle called?**

- A. A fringing reef
- B. A patch reef
- C. **An atoll** ✓

An atoll forms when a reef keeps growing upward around a volcanic island that slowly sinks, leaving a ring of reef around a central lagoon.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/threecorals.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.reef-types.atoll` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says atolls are rings of coral around a central lagoon, formed when a fringing reef keeps growing up from a volcanic island that subsides.</sub>

### dd-0340 · medium · unverified

**What separates a barrier reef from the shore?**

- A. **A lagoon of open water** ✓
- B. A strip of sand dunes
- C. A mangrove forest

Barrier reefs sit farther from land than fringing reefs, with a lagoon of open, often deep water between the reef and the coast.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/threecorals.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.reef-types.barrier-lagoon` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says barrier reefs are separated from the shore by a lagoon of open, often deep water.</sub>

### dd-0341 · medium · unverified

**Who proposed that reefs develop from fringing reef to barrier reef to atoll as volcanic islands sink?**

- A. Jacques Cousteau
- B. **Charles Darwin** ✓
- C. Alfred Wegener

Charles Darwin's subsidence theory linked the three reef types in one sequence, with reefs growing upward as the islands they surround slowly sink.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4028904/) · second source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/threecorals.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.atoll.darwin-theory` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The PMC Tahiti study refers to 'Darwin's reef types' and his subsidence theory that reefs form in sequence from fringing to barrier to atoll.</sub>

### dd-0342 · medium · unverified

**Under good conditions, about how fast can branching corals grow?**

- A. Up to about 1 meter a year
- B. Less than 1 mm a year
- C. **Up to about 10 cm a year** ✓

Branching corals can add up to about 4 inches (10 cm) a year, while dense, boulder-shaped massive corals add only up to about 2 cm.

Source: [NOAA National Marine Sanctuary of American Samoa](https://americansamoa.noaa.gov/explore/coral-reefs.html)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.growth.branching-rate` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA American Samoa says under optimum conditions massive corals may grow up to 2 cm a year and branching corals up to 10 cm a year.</sub>

### dd-0343 · hard · unverified

**A study of branching corals on the Great Barrier Reef found growth was highest in the north. How did it change southward?**

- A. **It decreased** ✓
- B. It increased
- C. It stayed the same

Growth of branching corals was highest at northern Lizard Island and fell with increasing latitude, matching cooler water temperatures farther south.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5462780/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.growth.gbr-latitude` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC study: annual growth in branching species was highest at Lizard Island and dropped with increasing latitude, tracking temperature.</sub>

### dd-0344 · medium · unverified

**What does a sea anemone use to anchor itself and slowly glide along?**

- A. Suction cups on its tentacles
- B. **A sticky base called a pedal disc** ✓
- C. Tiny hooked spines

Anemones hold on with a sticky base called the pedal disc. If conditions turn bad, many can slowly slide along the seafloor on it.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/anemones/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.anemone.pedal-disc` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium says anemones attach with a sticky pedal disc and can slowly slide along the ocean floor on it when conditions are poor.</sub>

### dd-0345 · medium · unverified

**How does a sea anemone get rid of waste after digesting a meal?**

- A. Through a separate anal pore
- B. Through pores in its tentacles
- C. **It expels it through its mouth** ✓

An anemone's gut has a single opening. Food goes in through the mouth, and once it is digested the leftovers come back out the same way.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/animal/anemones/) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/green_sea_anemone)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.anemone.single-opening` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search result summary: after food is digested, anemones expel waste products out of the mouth. Verify which of the two pages states this.</sub>

### dd-0346 · hard · unverified

**Some sea anemones reproduce by 'pedal laceration.' What happens?**

- A. **Base fragments grow into new anemones** ✓
- B. Eggs hatch inside the tentacles
- C. Two anemones fuse together

In pedal laceration, bits of an anemone's base tear off and are left behind as it moves. Each piece can grow into a new anemone.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8757374/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.anemone.pedal-laceration` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PMC study on Exaiptasia describes pedal lacerates: fragments of the pedal disc left behind that develop into new anemones.</sub>

### dd-0347 · medium · unverified

**What do aggregating anemones use to fight anemones from rival clones?**

- A. Their sticky pedal discs
- B. **Special stinging bulbs near their tentacles** ✓
- C. Clouds of toxic ink

Aggregating anemones inflate special bulbs called acrorhagi, which sit just outside the ring of tentacles and are packed with stinging cells, and press them onto rivals from other clones.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/aggregating-anemone) · second source: [UC Davis Grosberg Lab (Animal Behaviour, 2005)](https://grosberglab.faculty.ucdavis.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/453/2017/05/2005-Ayre-D.-J.-R.-K.-Grosberg.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.aggregating.acrorhagi` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium and the Grosberg lab paper say contact between non-clonemates triggers aggression using acrorhagi packed with nematocysts.</sub>

### dd-0348 · hard · unverified

**When two clones of aggregating anemones meet, what usually forms between them?**

- A. A mixed zone of both clones
- B. A ridge of fused anemones
- C. **An empty strip with no anemones** ✓

After fighting, rival clones draw apart and leave a bare neutral zone between them, a border you can see in tide pools.

Source: [University of Puget Sound, Slater Museum of Natural History](https://www.pugetsound.edu/puget-sound-museum-natural-history/exhibits/marine-panel/aggregating-anemone) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/aggregating-anemone)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.aggregating.neutral-zone` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Puget Sound museum and MBA pages: colonies draw away from each other, leaving an anemone-free neutral zone between clones.</sub>

### dd-0349 · hard · unverified

**In a clone of aggregating anemones, where are the 'warrior' polyps found?**

- A. **Along the clone's edges** ✓
- B. In the clone's center
- C. Scattered randomly

Polyps on a clone's border do most of the fighting. They tend to be smaller and carry more and larger acrorhagi, the stinging bulbs used in fights, than clonemates inside.

Source: [UC Davis Grosberg Lab (Animal Behaviour, 2005)](https://grosberglab.faculty.ucdavis.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/453/2017/05/2005-Ayre-D.-J.-R.-K.-Grosberg.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.aggregating.border-warriors` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Grosberg lab paper: border 'warriors' tend to be smaller, carry more and larger acrorhagi, and invest less in growth and reproduction than interior clonemates.</sub>

### dd-0350 · medium · unverified

**How do aggregating anemones form dense patches of genetically identical individuals?**

- A. By budding off swimming larvae
- B. **By splitting in two** ✓
- C. By fusing with neighbors

An aggregating anemone can divide in two, again and again, building a carpet of identical clones on tide-pool rocks.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/aggregating-anemone)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.aggregating.cloning` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium describes aggregating anemones forming clones by dividing (splitting). Verify wording on the page.</sub>

### dd-0351 · medium · rejected

**What gives the giant green anemone most of its green color?**

- A. Copper in its blood
- B. The green crabs it eats
- C. **Algae living in its tissue** ✓

Some color is the anemone's own pigment, but most comes from microscopic algae in its tissues, which also supply it with nutrients.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-green-anemone)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.giant-green.color` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium says the giant green anemone's color comes partly from its own pigment but mostly from symbiotic zoochlorellae and zooxanthellae in its tissues.</sub>

### dd-0352 · medium · unverified

**Water enters a sponge through many small pores. How does it leave?**

- A. **Through larger openings called oscula** ✓
- B. Through gill slits on its sides
- C. Through two tube-like siphons

A sponge draws water in through tiny pores called ostia and pushes it out through bigger openings called oscula, filtering food along the way.

Source: [Smithsonian Science Education Center (Qrius)](https://qrius.si.edu/taxonomy/term/11906) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/sea-stars/sponges/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.sponge.oscula` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Qrius sponge feeding page: water enters through small inlet pores (ostia) and exits through larger outlet pores (oscula).</sub>

### dd-0353 · easy · unverified

**Which of these does a sponge lack?**

- A. Pores that let water in
- B. **A brain, heart or gut** ✓
- C. A way to take in food

Sponges are animals without organs. They have no brain, heart or gut, yet they filter food from water and many have skeletons of tiny spicules.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/sea-stars/sponges/) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/orange-puffball-sponge)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.sponge.no-organs` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum and Monterey Bay Aquarium say sponges have no organs (and no true tissues); they feed by filtering water.</sub>

### dd-0354 · hard · unverified

**Inside a sponge, what are the cells that beat whip-like flagella to pump water and trap food?**

- A. Nematocysts
- B. Neurons
- C. **Choanocytes** ✓

Choanocytes, or collar cells, line a sponge's internal chambers. Their beating flagella drive water through the body and catch food particles.

Source: [Smithsonian Science Education Center (Qrius)](https://qrius.si.edu/taxonomy/term/11906)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.sponge.choanocytes` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Qrius: flagellated choanocytes drive the water current and form the filter-feeding apparatus, trapping suspended food particles.</sub>

### dd-0355 · hard · unverified

**How have some sponges been seen clearing waste from their inlet pores?**

- A. **By 'sneezing' out mucus** ✓
- B. By shedding their outer skin
- C. By scraping them clean with spines

Researchers found that sponges can 'sneeze,' slowly pushing out mucus that carries trapped particles away from their inlet pores.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9473484/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.sponge.sneeze` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Peer-reviewed study titled 'Sponges sneeze mucus to shed particulate waste from their seawater inlet pores.'</sub>

### dd-0356 · easy · unverified

**What are the skeletons of glass sponges made of?**

- A. Calcium carbonate
- B. **Silica, the stuff of glass** ✓
- C. Hardened protein fibers

Glass sponges build skeletons from silica spicules, using silicon drawn from seawater. The spicules can fuse into an intricate, rigid lattice.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/glass-sponge.html) · second source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/ocean-fact/glass-sponges/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.glass-sponge.silica` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Service and NOAA Ocean Exploration say glass sponge skeletons are made of silica spicules.</sub>

### dd-0357 · hard · unverified

**The deep-sea glass sponge Monorhaphis chuni anchors itself with one huge glass spicule. About how long can it be?**

- A. About 3 centimeters
- B. About 30 meters
- C. **About 3 meters** ✓

The single anchoring spicule of Monorhaphis chuni can reach about 3 meters long and 1 cm thick, built from hundreds of thin layers of silica.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3166767/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.monorhaphis.giant-spicule` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: PMC paper: the giant basal spicule of Monorhaphis chuni reaches about 3 m length and 10 mm width.</sub>

### dd-0358 · easy · unverified

**The giant barrel sponge of Caribbean reefs is nicknamed what, for its size and long life?**

- A. **The redwood of the reef** ✓
- B. The bulldozer of the reef
- C. The lighthouse of the reef

Giant barrel sponges grow very large and can live for a very long time, so scientists call them the 'redwoods of the reef.'

Source: [NOAA National Centers for Coastal Ocean Science](https://coastalscience.noaa.gov/data_reports/patterns-population-structure-dispersal-long-lived-redwood-coral-reef-giant-barrel-sponge-xestospongia-muta/) · second source: [University of North Carolina Wilmington (Marine Biology, 2008)](https://people.uncw.edu/pawlikj/2008MarBiolMcMurray.pdf)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.giant-barrel.nickname` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA NCCOS calls Xestospongia muta the long-lived 'redwood' of the coral reef; a UNC Wilmington paper is titled 'Redwood of the reef'.</sub>

### dd-0359 · medium · rejected

**Much of the white sand on Hawaii's famous beaches comes from what?**

- A. Salt crystals from dried seawater
- B. **Parrotfish droppings** ✓
- C. Eroded volcanic glass

Parrotfish scrape algae and bits of coral with beak-like teeth, grind the hard material and excrete it as fine white sand.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/sand.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/tough-teeth-and-parrotfish-poop)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.parrotfish.sand` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA 'How does sand form?' says Hawaii's famous white-sand beaches come largely from parrotfish poop; Smithsonian describes parrotfish excreting ground coral as sand.</sub>

### dd-0360 · medium · rejected

**How does the crown-of-thorns starfish eat coral?**

- A. It cracks the skeleton with its spines
- B. It sucks out polyps with a tube
- C. **It pushes its stomach out onto the coral** ✓

The starfish pushes its stomach out over the coral and releases enzymes that digest the living polyps, leaving behind a bare white skeleton.

Source: [NOAA Pacific Islands Fisheries Science Center](https://pifsc-www.irc.noaa.gov/cred/crown-of-thorns_seastar.php) · second source: [Australian Institute of Marine Science](https://www.aims.gov.au/research-topics/marine-life/crown-thorns-starfish)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.crown-of-thorns.feeding` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA PIFSC says when consuming coral it extrudes its stomach and releases digestive enzymes; AIMS notes it retracts its stomach when done.</sub>

### dd-0361 · hard · rejected

**How can muddy, sediment-filled water harm coral spawning?**

- A. **It makes egg bundles sink** ✓
- B. It dissolves the eggs
- C. It stops polyps opening at all

Suspended sediment can stick to rising egg-and-sperm bundles and weigh them down, so fewer reach the surface where fertilization happens.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4761919/)

Fact id: `corals-reefs.spawning.sediment-sinking` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PMC study: suspended sediments can prevent the ascent of coral egg bundles, reducing fertilization chances.</sub>

## jellies (55)

### dd-0362 · easy · unverified

**Roughly how much of a jellyfish's body is water?**

- A. About 50 percent
- B. About 75 percent
- C. **About 95 percent** ✓

A jellyfish's body is about 95 percent water. NOAA describes jellyfish as pretty simple critters.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/jellyfish.html)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.water-content` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA's 'What are jellyfish made of?' page says jellyfish bodies are about 95 percent water and calls them simple animals.</sub>

### dd-0363 · easy · unverified

**Jellyfish have no brain. What do they have instead?**

- A. **A network of nerves spread through the body** ✓
- B. A small brain at the top of the bell
- C. A separate brain inside each tentacle

Instead of a brain, jellyfish have a net of nerves spread through the body, yet they can still sense and respond to their surroundings.

Source: [NOAA](https://www.noaa.gov/stories/fact-vs-fiction-3-things-you-thought-you-knew-about-jellyfish)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.nerve-net` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's fact-vs-fiction story says jellyfish lack a brain and instead have a network of nerves, with no central nervous system.</sub>

### dd-0364 · easy · unverified

**Despite the name, what kind of animal is a jellyfish?**

- A. **An invertebrate, not a fish** ✓
- B. A fish that has lost its bones
- C. A sea snail without a shell

Jellyfish are invertebrates, animals without a backbone, and they have no bones at all, so the 'fish' in their name is misleading.

Source: [NOAA](https://www.noaa.gov/stories/fact-vs-fiction-3-things-you-thought-you-knew-about-jellyfish)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.not-a-fish` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's fact-vs-fiction story addresses the myth that jellyfish are fish: they are invertebrates and lack bones.</sub>

### dd-0365 · medium · unverified

- A. June 8
- B. April 22
- C. **November 3** ✓

World Jellyfish Day falls on November 3. NOAA Ocean Exploration uses the day to share facts about these brainless, mostly-water drifters.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://archive.oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/worldjellyfishday.html) · second source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/expedition-feature/okeanos-ex1907-logs-nov3/)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.world-jellyfish-day` · risk: none

### dd-0366 · medium · unverified

**What are the rhopalia of a jellyfish?**

- A. **Sense organs that detect light and movement** ✓
- B. Stinging cells on its tentacles
- C. Reproductive organs inside its bell

Rhopalia are sensory structures that can detect light, chemicals and movement, helping a brainless jellyfish respond to its world.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://archive.oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/worldjellyfishday.html)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.rhopalia` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA's World Jellyfish Day page describes rhopalia as sensory structures that detect light, chemicals and movement.</sub>

### dd-0367 · medium · unverified

**How many eyes does the box jellyfish Tripedalia cystophora have?**

- A. Two
- B. Eight
- C. **Twenty-four** ✓

Its 24 eyes come in four types and sit in four sensory clusters of six. Only some of them have lenses; the rest are simpler light-sensing pits and slits.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825319/) · second source: [PubMed (NCBI)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20147327/)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.tripedalia-eye-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: The Tripedalia paper describes its 24 eyes as four morphological types, two being lens eyes; the Chiropsella bronzie paper notes six eyes on each of four rhopalia.</sub>

### dd-0368 · medium · unverified

**Some box jellyfish have complex eyes. Which structures found in human eyes do they have?**

- A. **A lens and a retina** ✓
- B. Eyelids
- C. Tear glands

Some box jellyfish have eyes with lenses, corneas and retinas, though scientists still don't know how these brainless animals interpret what they see.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://archive.oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/worldjellyfishday.html)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.lens-eyes` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA's World Jellyfish Day page says some cubozoan (box) jellyfish have complex eyes with lenses, corneas and retinas, and that how they interpret images is unknown.</sub>

### dd-0369 · medium · unverified

**Experiments on the box jellyfish Tripedalia cystophora show it uses its eyes for what?**

- A. Sensing the body heat of prey
- B. **Steering around obstacles** ✓
- C. Judging the water temperature

In lab tests, Tripedalia cystophora used vision to avoid obstacles, turning away from objects in its path instead of bumping into them.

Source: [PubMed (NCBI)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17921163/?amp=&itool=EntrezSystem2.PEntrez.Pubmed.Pubmed_ResultsPanel.Pubmed_RVDocSum&ordinalpos=1) · second source: [PubMed (NCBI)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21832123/)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.tripedalia-obstacle-avoidance` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: PubMed abstract 'Visually guided obstacle avoidance in the box jellyfish Tripedalia cystophora and Chiropsella bronzie'; a second paper covers visual control of steering in Tripedalia.</sub>

### dd-0370 · hard · unverified

**The small box jellyfish Copula sivickisi hunts at night. What does research suggest helps it see its prey?**

- A. Heat given off by its prey
- B. A glowing lure on its own tentacles
- C. **Light from bioluminescent organisms** ✓

Researchers found the eyes of Copula sivickisi suit dim light and proposed that it hunts by the glow of bioluminescent organisms at night.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4812875/)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.copula-bioluminescent-hunting` · risk: disputed-check

### dd-0371 · medium · unverified

**Where in Australia does the box jellyfish Chironex fleckeri live?**

- A. Cool waters around Tasmania
- B. **Tropical coastal waters of the north** ✓
- C. The deep sea off Sydney

Chironex fleckeri lives in tropical Australian coastal waters, from Western Australia through the Northern Territory to Queensland.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/jellyfish/boxjellyfish/)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.chironex-range` · risk: species-specific

### dd-0372 · medium · unverified

**Australian first-aid advice for a sting from the box jellyfish Chironex fleckeri is to flood the sting with what?**

- A. Fresh water
- B. Rubbing alcohol
- C. **Vinegar** ✓

Call emergency services, then flood the sting with vinegar for at least 30 seconds. Vinegar stops stinging cells that haven't fired yet from discharging.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/jellyfish/boxjellyfish/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12448702/)

Fact id: `jellies.box-jelly.chironex-vinegar` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum advises flooding the sting with vinegar for at least 30 seconds and calling emergency services; an evidence review says vinegar prevents further discharge but does not neutralise venom already injected.</sub>

### dd-0373 · medium · unverified

**About how wide is the bell of the Irukandji jellyfish Carukia barnesi?**

- A. **About 2 centimetres** ✓
- B. About 30 centimetres
- C. About 1 metre

Carukia barnesi has a bell only about 2 centimetres across. Its small size and transparency make it almost impossible to spot in the water.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/jellyfish/irukandji-carukia-barnesi/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9352007/)

### dd-0374 · medium · unverified

**Irukandji syndrome, a severe illness caused by stings from some small box jellyfish, is named after what?**

- A. The scientist who first described it
- B. **The Irukandji people of the Cairns region** ✓
- C. A beach in Western Australia

Toxicologist Hugo Flecker first documented the syndrome in 1952. It was named after the Irukandji people, who lived near Cairns in Queensland.

Source: [NCBI Bookshelf (StatPearls)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK562264/)

Fact id: `jellies.irukandji.syndrome-name` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: StatPearls says the syndrome was first documented by Hugo Flecker in 1952 and named after the Irukandji people near Cairns, Queensland.</sub>

### dd-0375 · hard · unverified

**A study titled 'Dangerous jellyfish blooms are predictable' linked Irukandji jellyfish arrivals in Queensland mainly to what?**

- A. The phase of the moon
- B. **A weakening of the trade winds** ✓
- C. The colour of the seawater

Irukandji jellyfish tended to appear when the prevailing trade winds slackened, which suggests risky days for swimmers could be forecast.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4032527/)

### dd-0376 · medium · unverified

**When injured or starving, what can the so-called 'immortal' jellyfish Turritopsis dohrnii do?**

- A. Split into two identical adults
- B. Hatch new adults straight from eggs
- C. **Revert to its earlier polyp stage** ✓

Turritopsis dohrnii can turn back into a polyp, which later produces new jellyfish, restarting its life cycle. That is where the 'immortal' nickname comes from.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/immortal-jellyfish-secret-to-cheating-death.html) · second source: [American Museum of Natural History](https://www.amnh.org/explore/news-blogs/immortal-jellyfish)

Fact id: `jellies.turritopsis.reverts-to-polyp` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NHM and AMNH pages describe Turritopsis dohrnii reverting from the medusa to the polyp stage when damaged or stressed, resetting its life cycle.</sub>

### dd-0377 · medium · unverified

**Turritopsis dohrnii's life-cycle reversal relies on specialised cells changing into other cell types. What is this process called?**

- A. Photosynthesis
- B. **Transdifferentiation** ✓
- C. Bioluminescence

In transdifferentiation, cells that already have one job become a different cell type. This lets the adult medusa rebuild itself as a polyp.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8480191/)

Fact id: `jellies.turritopsis.transdifferentiation` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: The Turritopsis gene-expression paper describes the reversal as passing through a cyst stage where cellular transdifferentiation occurs.</sub>

### dd-0378 · hard · unverified

**On its way back to being a polyp, what does Turritopsis dohrnii briefly become?**

- A. **A cyst-like mass of cells** ✓
- B. A free-swimming larva
- C. A hard-shelled egg

The medusa settles and shrinks into a cyst-like blob of cells, which starts to show polyp features within about one to three days.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8480191/)

Fact id: `jellies.turritopsis.cyst-stage` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Paper says the medusa settles and becomes a cyst-like mass of cells that shows polyp features within 24 to 72 hours.</sub>

### dd-0379 · medium · unverified

**Why is 'immortal' a misleading word for the jellyfish Turritopsis dohrnii?**

- A. It lives only a few hours as an adult
- B. **It can still die from predators or disease** ✓
- C. It cannot reproduce at all

Turritopsis dohrnii can reset its life cycle instead of simply ageing and dying, but it can still be eaten or killed by disease.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/immortal-jellyfish-secret-to-cheating-death.html) · second source: [American Museum of Natural History](https://www.amnh.org/explore/news-blogs/immortal-jellyfish)

Fact id: `jellies.turritopsis.not-invulnerable` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NHM and AMNH pages put 'immortal' in quotes; the reverted animal can still be killed, so it avoids death by resetting rather than being truly immortal.</sub>

### dd-0380 · medium · unverified

**Comb jellies look like jellyfish. Which group do they actually belong to?**

- A. Cnidarians, along with true jellyfish
- B. Tunicates, along with sea squirts
- C. **Their own group, the ctenophores** ✓

Comb jellies form their own phylum, Ctenophora. Despite their jelly-like bodies, they are not closely related to true jellyfish.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/jellyfish-and-comb-jellies) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/comb_jelly/)

Fact id: `jellies.comb-jelly.own-phylum` · risk: none

### dd-0381 · medium · unverified

**Comb jellies don't sting. How do many of them catch their prey?**

- A. **With sticky cells called colloblasts** ✓
- B. By injecting venom through spines
- C. By stunning prey with electricity

Instead of the stinging cells jellyfish use, many comb jellies trap prey with sticky cells called colloblasts.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/comb_jelly/)

Fact id: `jellies.comb-jelly.colloblasts` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search result: unlike jellyfish, comb jellies do not sting; they catch prey with sticky cells called colloblasts.</sub>

### dd-0382 · medium · unverified

**How many rows of beating, comb-like cilia do most comb jellies have?**

- A. Two
- B. **Eight** ✓
- C. Thirty

Most comb jellies have eight rows of cilia (tiny hairs) that beat in rhythm, paddling the animal along. These 'combs' give the group its name.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/multimedia/daily-image-media-20211027/) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/comb_jelly/)

Fact id: `jellies.comb-jelly.eight-comb-rows` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search result: most species have eight rows of cilia that beat rhythmically and refract light as they paddle through the water.</sub>

### dd-0383 · medium · review

**What creates the rainbow shimmer that ripples along a comb jelly's body?**

- A. Its own bioluminescent glow
- B. Coloured pigments in its skin
- C. **Light scattering off its beating cilia** ✓

The flickering rainbows come from light scattering off the moving cilia. They are not light the animal makes itself.

Source: [New England Aquarium](http://galleries.neaq.org/2015/03/local-species-mnemiopsis-jellies.html)

Fact id: `jellies.comb-jelly.rainbow-is-scattered-light` · risk: none

- A. **Produce their own light** ✓
- B. Sting with venomous cells
- C. Change colour to match the seabed

Many comb jellies are bioluminescent and make their own light. That glow is separate from the rainbow effect caused by light hitting their cilia.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/comb_jelly/)

Fact id: `jellies.comb-jelly.sea-walnut-bioluminescence` · risk: disputed-check

**The comb jelly Mnemiopsis leidyi became notorious in the 1980s after it was accidentally introduced to which sea?**

- A. **The Black Sea** ✓
- B. The Red Sea
- C. The Coral Sea

Probably carried in ships' ballast water, Mnemiopsis leidyi spread through the Black Sea, where its boom was linked to a sharp decline in fisheries.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/jellyfish-and-comb-jellies)

### dd-0388 · medium · unverified

**Salps look like see-through jellies. What are they actually?**

- A. **Tunicates, relatives of sea squirts** ✓
- B. Young jellyfish without tentacles
- C. Comb jellies that lost their combs

Salps are pelagic tunicates: open-water filter feeders that are not jellyfish at all, despite their clear, jelly-like bodies.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/oceanus/feature/tiny-drifters-massive-impact-salps-climate-carbon/) · second source: [NOAA Institutional Repository](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/46504/noaa_46504_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `jellies.salp.tunicate` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: WHOI feature on salps and a NOAA repository review on pelagic tunicates treat salps as tunicates.</sub>

### dd-0389 · medium · unverified

**Why are salps important for moving carbon into the deep ocean?**

- A. They release carbon as bubbles of gas
- B. **Their fast-sinking fecal pellets carry it down** ✓
- C. They build carbon-rich shells that pile up

Salp fecal pellets can sink up to about 1,000 metres a day, carrying carbon into deep water where it can stay for decades or centuries.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/oceanus/feature/tiny-drifters-massive-impact-salps-climate-carbon/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9894854/)

Fact id: `jellies.salp.fast-sinking-pellets` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: WHOI reports salp pellets can sink up to about 1,000 m per day, strengthening the biological carbon pump; a Southern Ocean study ties salp blooms to increased carbon export.</sub>

### dd-0390 · hard · unverified

**Near the Antarctic Peninsula, salps produced more fecal-pellet carbon than krill, yet both added equally to carbon sinking past 300 metres. Why?**

- A. Krill pellets sank ten times faster
- B. Salp pellets floated back to the surface
- C. **Most salp pellets broke apart near the surface** ✓

Salps made about four times more pellet carbon than krill, but roughly 80 percent of salp pellets fragmented and stayed in the surface layer.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8660819/)

Fact id: `jellies.salp.krill-pellet-comparison` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Study found salps produced about four times more pellet carbon than krill, but about 80% of salp pellets stayed in the mixed layer because they fragment, so both contributed equally at 300 m.</sub>

### dd-0391 · medium · unverified

**How do salps swim?**

- A. **By pumping water through their bodies like a jet** ✓
- B. By beating rows of comb-like hairs
- C. By flapping a pair of side fins

Salps move by jet propulsion, pushing water through their barrel-shaped bodies. WHOI researchers used dye to make those jets visible.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/oceanus/feature/dye-sheds-light-on-jet-propelled-salps/)

Fact id: `jellies.salp.jet-propulsion` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: WHOI feature 'Dye Sheds Light on Jet-Propelled Salps' describes salps swimming by jet propulsion, studied with dye.</sub>

### dd-0392 · easy · unverified

**How does the by-the-wind sailor (Velella velella) travel across the ocean?**

- A. It swims by pulsing its bell
- B. **It is pushed by the wind on a small sail** ✓
- C. It rides currents deep below the surface

Velella floats at the surface with a stiff, triangular sail set diagonally across its body, so the wind carries it. It does not steer on purpose.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service (Point Reyes National Seashore)](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/nature/otherinvertebrates-velella.htm)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.wind-sail` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS Point Reyes describes a stiff triangular sail set diagonally; Velella do not actively navigate and are at the mercy of the winds.</sub>

### dd-0393 · hard · rejected

**By-the-wind sailors have sails angled either left or right. Which form is usually found in the Northern Hemisphere?**

- A. Right-handed
- B. **Left-handed** ✓
- C. Both in equal numbers

Because of prevailing winds, left-handed Velella are usually found in the Northern Hemisphere and right-handed ones in the Southern Hemisphere.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/by_the_wind_sailor) · second source: [U.S. National Park Service (Point Reyes National Seashore)](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/upload/sitebulletin-velella-velella.pdf)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.sail-handedness` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Search result: due to prevailing winds, left-handed Velella are usually found in the Northern Hemisphere, right-handed ones in the Southern Hemisphere. Exact page not pinned; verifier should confirm.</sub>

### dd-0394 · easy · rejected

**Why do by-the-wind sailors sometimes wash up on beaches by the thousands?**

- A. They come ashore to lay eggs
- B. They are fleeing predators offshore
- C. **Shifting winds drive them toward the coast** ✓

Velella can't steer, so when prevailing winds shift, such as during storms, they are driven toward the coast and stranded in great numbers.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service (Point Reyes National Seashore)](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/nature/otherinvertebrates-velella.htm)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.mass-strandings` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS Point Reyes: when prevailing winds shift, e.g. during a storm, Velella are driven toward the coast and stranded on beaches in great numbers.</sub>

**How dangerous is the sting of a by-the-wind sailor to people?**

- A. **Rarely harmful** ✓
- B. Often life-threatening
- C. Worse than a man o' war's

Velella has stinging cells for catching food, but unlike those of the Portuguese man o' war, they are rarely harmful to humans.

Source: [Oregon Sea Grant (Oregon State University)](https://seagrant.oregonstate.edu/visitor-center/found-beach/velella-velella) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/by_the_wind_sailor)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.sting-harmless` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search result: Velella's stinging cells are rarely harmful to humans, as opposed to those of the Portuguese man o' war.</sub>

- A. **Tiny drifting crustaceans and other plankton** ✓
- B. Seaweed scraped from floating debris
- C. Small fish that it chases down

Drifting at the surface, Velella uses its short stinging tentacles to catch tiny floating crustaceans and other plankton.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/by_the_wind_sailor)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.diet` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search result: Velella feeds on tiny floating crustaceans, using tentacles with stinging cells.</sub>

### dd-0397 · easy · unverified

**Where do by-the-wind sailors live?**

- A. **On the surface of temperate and tropical seas** ✓

- C. Frozen into polar sea ice

By-the-wind sailors drift at the sea surface in temperate and tropical waters, mainly in the Pacific and Atlantic oceans.

Source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/species/wind-sailor-velella-velella) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/by_the_wind_sailor)

Fact id: `jellies.velella.range` · risk: none

### dd-0398 · easy · unverified

**What is a Portuguese man o' war?**

- A. A single giant jellyfish
- B. **A colony of many specialised individuals** ✓
- C. A floating type of sea anemone

The man o' war is a siphonophore: a colony of genetically identical individuals called zooids, each specialised for a job such as floating or feeding.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/portuguese-man-o-war.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/holding-tank/images-hide/siphonophores)

Fact id: `jellies.man-o-war.colony` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA NOS says the man o' war is a siphonophore, a colony of specialised, genetically identical zooids; Smithsonian says siphonophores resemble jellyfish but are colonies.</sub>

### dd-0399 · medium · unverified

**According to the U.S. National Park Service, a Portuguese man o' war colony is made of how many types of polyps?**

- A. Two
- B. **Four** ✓
- C. Twelve

The colony has four types of polyps that split the work: one forms the float, and others catch prey, digest food or reproduce.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service (Fire Island National Seashore)](https://www.nps.gov/fiis/learn/news/portuguese-man-o-war-reported.htm) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/science/five-facts-portuguese-man-of-war/)

- A. No, it is harmless once out of water

Park officials warn beachgoers not to touch stranded man o' war, because their tentacles can still deliver a painful sting after washing ashore.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service (Fire Island National Seashore)](https://www.nps.gov/fiis/learn/news/portuguese-man-o-war-reported.htm) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/science/five-facts-portuguese-man-of-war/)

Its float resembles an 18th-century Portuguese warship under full sail, which inspired the name.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/portuguese-man-o-war.html)

Fact id: `jellies.man-o-war.name-origin` · risk: disputed-check

**What keeps a Portuguese man o' war at the sea surface?**

- A. **A gas-filled float** ✓
- B. A droplet of oil inside its body
- C. Constant pulsing of a bell

One member of the colony forms a gas-filled float that rides above the water, so the whole colony drifts with the wind and currents.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/portuguese-man-o-war.html) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/science/five-facts-portuguese-man-of-war/)

### dd-0403 · medium · unverified

**Roughly how long have the tentacles of the largest lion's mane jellyfish (Cyanea capillata) been reported to grow?**

- A. About 30 centimetres (1 foot)
- B. About 1 metre (3 feet)
- C. **About 30 metres (100 feet)** ✓

Most lion's mane jellies have tentacles a couple of metres long, but the largest reported specimens, from cold northern waters, had tentacles of around 30 metres.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/lions_mane_jelly) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/what-is-a-jellyfish.html)

### dd-0404 · medium · unverified

**When a moon jelly (Aurelia aurita) polyp divides into a stack of discs, what does each disc become?**

- A. A swimming larva called a planula
- B. **A young medusa called an ephyra** ✓
- C. A new polyp attached nearby

In a process called strobilation, the polyp splits into stacked discs that are released one by one as tiny jellies called ephyrae, which grow into adults.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10433978/)

Fact id: `jellies.moon-jelly.ephyrae` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Aurelia aurita microbiome paper describes strobilation: each polyp segment develops into a young medusa, the ephyra, released in sequence.</sub>

### dd-0405 · hard · unverified

**A 2023 study found moon jelly (Aurelia aurita) polyps struggle to release baby jellyfish when they lack what?**

- A. Contact with adult jellies
- B. Bright sunlight
- C. **Their natural bacteria** ✓

Polyps without their native microbes produced and released few ephyrae, and adding the microbes back after the process had started did not fix it.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10433978/)

Fact id: `jellies.moon-jelly.strobilation-needs-microbes` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Paper reports strobilation is severely compromised without the natural polyp microbiome, and supplying microbes after onset did not restore it.</sub>

### dd-0406 · hard · unverified

**Life-cycle reversal, made famous by the 'immortal' jellyfish, has also been documented in which animal?**

- A. **A moon jelly (Aurelia)** ✓
- B. The Portuguese man o' war
- C. A salp

Researchers found that degenerating young medusae of a moon jelly, Aurelia sp.1, could form new polyps, so this trick is not unique to Turritopsis.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4687044/)

Fact id: `jellies.moon-jelly.aurelia-life-cycle-reversal` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Paper 'Life Cycle Reversal in Aurelia sp.1' documents polyps forming from degenerating juvenile medusae and medusa tissue fragments.</sub>

- A. **Four horseshoe-shaped gonads in its bell** ✓
- B. A bright red stripe around its rim
- C. Thick brown tentacles a metre long

Moon jellies have a clear bell through which four horseshoe-shaped reproductive organs, or gonads, are easy to see.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/moon_jelly) · second source: [Georgia Aquarium](https://www.georgiaaquarium.org/animal/moon-jelly/)

Fact id: `jellies.moon-jelly.horseshoe-gonads` · risk: species-specific

### dd-0408 · easy · unverified

**What is the free-swimming, bell-shaped stage of a jellyfish's life called?**

- A. The polyp
- B. **The medusa** ✓
- C. The planula

Many jellyfish alternate between a polyp attached to a surface and the swimming medusa, the bell-shaped form most people picture. The medusa reproduces sexually.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://blog.seattleaquarium.org/marine-animals/facts-jellyfish-life) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10433978/)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.medusa-stage` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium life-cycle post and the Aurelia paper describe sexual reproduction at the medusa stage and a sessile polyp stage.</sub>

### dd-0409 · medium · unverified

**Researchers reported in 2017 that the upside-down jellyfish Cassiopea, which has no brain, shows signs of what?**

- A. Tool use
- B. Hibernation
- C. **Sleep** ✓

At night Cassiopea pulses more slowly and responds more sluggishly, and it is drowsier after a disturbed night, meeting key tests for a sleep-like state.

Source: [Caltech Authors (California Institute of Technology)](https://authors.library.caltech.edu/records/psp7j-nmv35) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/jellyfish-and-comb-jellies)

Fact id: `jellies.cassiopea.sleep` · risk: surprising

### dd-0410 · medium · rejected

**Where does the upside-down jellyfish Cassiopea get much of its food?**

- A. Mud it filters from the seafloor
- B. Dead fish it scavenges
- C. **Algae living inside its tissues** ✓

Cassiopea hosts photosynthetic algae in its tissues, and their photosynthesis supplies a significant share of the jellyfish's carbon needs.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12091945/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7779505/)

Fact id: `jellies.cassiopea.algae-food` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Cassiopea photosymbiosis papers: symbiotic algae (Symbiodiniaceae) photosynthesis supplies the host with a significant portion of its organic carbon.</sub>

### dd-0411 · hard · unverified

- A. **Tiny clusters of stinging cells released into the water** ✓
- B. Venom the jellyfish sprays from its arms
- C. An electric charge given off by its bell

Cassiopea releases cassiosomes, tiny self-contained structures packed with stinging cells that can go on stinging after leaving the jellyfish.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10826341/)

Fact id: `jellies.cassiopea.cassiosomes` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Paper title describes cassiosomes as the autonomous stinging-cell structures of Cassiopea, which survive long-term; the 'stinging water' link to waders is from my background knowledge, verifier should confirm.</sub>

- A. A firefly
- B. **A jellyfish** ✓
- C. A glowing mushroom

Osamu Shimomura first isolated green fluorescent protein (GFP) from a jellyfish. Scientists now use it to tag and watch processes inside living cells.

Source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/chemistry/2008/popular-information/) · second source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/chemistry/2008/press-release/)

Fact id: `jellies.crystal-jelly.gfp-nobel` · risk: none

**What colour does the rim of the crystal jelly Aequorea victoria glow when it is disturbed?**

- A. Deep red
- B. **Green** ✓
- C. White

The crystal jelly's outer edge glows green when agitated. The protein behind that glow, GFP, is now widely used to tag and track processes in living cells.

Source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/chemistry/2008/popular-information/)

Fact id: `jellies.crystal-jelly.green-rim` · risk: species-specific
