### dd-0414 · hard · unverified

**In Hydra, a tiny freshwater relative of jellyfish, how long does a stinging capsule take to fire?**

- A. **Within about 3 milliseconds** ✓
- B. About half a second
- C. About 5 seconds

Stinging-capsule discharge is among the fastest mechanical processes in nature. In Hydra it is over within about 3 milliseconds.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4321713/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9205923/)

Fact id: `jellies.nematocyst.hydra-firing-speed` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Search result: nematocyst discharge is among the fastest mechanical processes in nature, completed within 3 ms in Hydra; initial phase as fast as 700 ns.</sub>

### dd-0415 · medium · unverified

**How many times can a single nematocyst, a cnidarian's stinging capsule, fire?**

- A. About ten times
- B. As often as the animal needs
- C. **Only once** ✓

Each stinging capsule fires just once, so stinging is costly, and cnidarians tightly control when their capsules go off.

Source: [PubMed Central (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7250568/)

Fact id: `jellies.nematocyst.single-use` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: 'A molecular filter for the cnidarian stinging response' notes each nematocyst fires once, making stinging energetically expensive and tightly regulated.</sub>

### dd-0416 · easy · unverified

**Why are jellyfish fossils rare?**

- A. Jellyfish appeared only recently
- B. **They have no hard parts to preserve** ✓
- C. They lived only in the deep sea

With no bones or shells, jellyfish rarely fossilise. Even so, jellyfish fossils about 505 million years old have been found in Utah.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://archive.oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/facts/worldjellyfishday.html)

Fact id: `jellies.jellyfish.fossils-rare` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA World Jellyfish Day page says jellyfish fossils are rare because they have no hard parts, but some about 505 million years old were found in Utah.</sub>

## mollusks (44)

### dd-0417 · medium · unverified

**What does a cone snail use as a harpoon to inject venom into its prey?**

- A. A spine on the edge of its shell
- B. **A hollow, modified tooth** ✓
- C. A stinger at the tip of its foot

Cone snails fire a hollow, harpoon-like tooth from their radula, the toothed feeding ribbon, and push venom through it to paralyze prey.

Source: [Toxins (via PubMed Central, NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10892987/)

Fact id: `mollusks.cone-snail.harpoon-tooth` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Review describes cone snails using a modified radula tooth shaped like a hollow harpoon, delivered ballistically, to inject a cocktail of peptide neurotoxins into prey.</sub>

### dd-0418 · medium · unverified

**The geographic cone snail of the Indo-Pacific is best known for what?**

- A. **A sting that can be fatal to people** ✓
- B. A shell that glows in the dark
- C. Swimming by jet propulsion

The geographic cone's venom is potent enough to kill a person, which is why divers and beachcombers are warned not to handle live cone snails.

Source: [StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf, NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK470586/) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/geographic_cone_snail)

Fact id: `mollusks.geographic-cone.deadly-sting` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: StatPearls cone snail toxicity page and Aquarium of the Pacific geographic cone page came up for cone snail venom; the search summary did not quote the human-fatality line, so verifier should confirm Conus geographus is named as dangerous/fatal to humans.</sub>

### dd-0419 · medium · unverified

**Cone snail venom is a mix of many small nerve-blocking molecules known as what?**

- A. Saxitoxins
- B. Tetrodotoxins
- C. **Conotoxins** ✓

Conotoxins are short peptides, small chains of amino acids, that jam the signals nerves use, quickly paralyzing a cone snail's prey.

Source: [Toxins (via PubMed Central, NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10892987/) · second source: [StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf, NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK470586/)

Fact id: `mollusks.cone-snail.conotoxins` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Sources say cone snail venom contains hundreds of different compounds called conotoxins that interfere with nerve impulses and paralyze prey.</sub>

### dd-0420 · medium · unverified

**Ziconotide, a medicine made as a copy of a cone snail venom molecule, is approved to treat what?**

- A. High blood pressure
- B. **Severe chronic pain** ✓
- C. Bacterial infections

Ziconotide, sold as Prialt, is a lab-made version of a toxin from the magician cone snail. It is reserved for severe pain when other treatments fail.

Source: [StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf, NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK459151/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2654521/)

Fact id: `mollusks.cone-snail.ziconotide-pain` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: StatPearls: ziconotide (Prialt) is a synthetic version of omega-conotoxin MVIIA from the fish-hunting cone snail Conus magus, FDA-approved for severe chronic pain when other options fail.</sub>

### dd-0421 · hard · unverified

**Unlike morphine, the cone-snail-derived painkiller ziconotide works by blocking what?**

- A. **Calcium channels in nerve cells** ✓
- B. Opioid receptors in the brain
- C. Histamine release in the skin

Ziconotide plugs N-type calcium channels, tiny gates that pain-sensing nerves need to pass on signals, and it does not act on opioid receptors.

Source: [PubMed (NIH)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15578997/) · second source: [StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf, NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK459151/)

Fact id: `mollusks.cone-snail.ziconotide-mechanism` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: PubMed title calls ziconotide a neuronal calcium channel blocker; StatPearls says it blocks N-type calcium channels in the spinal cord and does not bind opioid receptors.</sub>

### dd-0422 · hard · unverified

**How does the fish-hunting magician cone snail (Conus magus) detect a nearby fish?**

- A. By sensing body heat
- B. By echolocation clicks
- C. **By sensing chemicals in the water** ✓

It picks up chemical cues from the fish, then extends its long proboscis and stabs the fish with a venom-filled, harpoon-like tooth.

Source: [Toxins (via PubMed Central, NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10892987/)

Fact id: `mollusks.magician-cone.chemical-detection` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Review says species such as Conus magus detect fish with chemosensors, then extend the proboscis to impale the fish with a hollow, venom-filled harpoon-like tooth.</sub>

### dd-0423 · medium · unverified

**In 2001 the white abalone became the first marine invertebrate to be what?**

- A. **Listed under the U.S. Endangered Species Act** ✓
- B. Declared extinct in the wild
- C. Bred on a commercial farm

So few white abalone were left that NOAA listed the species as endangered in 2001, the first marine invertebrate protected under the Act.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/white-abalone) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/reportcard/info/white_abalone)

Fact id: `mollusks.white-abalone.first-esa-invertebrate` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA and Aquarium of the Pacific: white abalone (Haliotis sorenseni) was the first marine invertebrate listed under the ESA, final rule effective June 2001.</sub>

### dd-0424 · medium · unverified

**What was the main cause of the white abalone's collapse?**

- A. Oil spills along the coast
- B. **Commercial overfishing in the 1970s** ✓
- C. Predation by invasive crabs

Heavy commercial harvest in the 1970s left survivors too scattered to recover, and numbers kept falling even after California closed the fishery in 1996.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/white-abalone) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/reportcard/info/white_abalone)

Fact id: `mollusks.white-abalone.overfishing-decline` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Commercial harvesting in the 1970s drove the population to very low levels and fragmented it; a 1996 California fishery closure and 2001 listing did not reverse the decline.</sub>

### dd-0425 · easy · unverified

**How are scientists working to rebuild wild white abalone populations?**

- A. Moving adults into freshwater lakes
- B. Hand-feeding wild adults kelp
- C. **Breeding them and releasing the young** ✓

Partners including NOAA and the Aquarium of the Pacific raise young white abalone in captivity and release, or 'outplant', them into the wild.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/conservation/research/abalone_breeding) · second source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/press/info/endangered_species_to_get_a_helping_hand_as_white_abalones_will_be_released_to_the_wild_after_years_of_research_by_aquarium_and_partners/newsroom)

Fact id: `mollusks.white-abalone.captive-breeding-release` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific pages describe captive breeding of white abalone and releasing them into the wild; a 2024 workshop with NOAA planned the next five years of outplanting.</sub>

### dd-0426 · easy · unverified

**Where does the endangered white abalone live?**

- A. Coastal Hawaii
- B. The Gulf of Maine
- C. **Southern California and Baja California** ✓

Its range runs from Point Conception, California, south to Punta Abreojos in Baja California, Mexico.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/white-abalone)

Fact id: `mollusks.white-abalone.range` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA says the white abalone listing covers its range from Point Conception, California, to Punta Abreojos, Baja California, Mexico.</sub>

### dd-0427 · medium · unverified

**Where does an adult true giant clam (Tridacna gigas) get most of its food?**

- A. **From algae living in its mantle** ✓
- B. From small fish it traps
- C. From algae it scrapes off rocks

Single-celled algae living in the clam's fleshy mantle use sunlight to make sugars, and adult clams get most of their food from what the algae share.

Source: [Australian Museum (Lizard Island Field Guide)](https://lifg.australian.museum/Group.html?groupId=wuDqZWyv)

Fact id: `mollusks.giant-clam.algae-food` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Lizard Island Field Guide: giant clams host dinoflagellate algae in mantle tissue; adults get most of their food from sugars the algae produce, while filter feeding matters more for juveniles.</sub>

### dd-0428 · easy · unverified

**How wide can the true giant clam (Tridacna gigas) grow?**

- A. About 30 centimeters (1 foot)
- B. About 10 meters (33 feet)
- C. **More than 1 meter (over 3 feet)** ✓

The true giant clam is the biggest of the giant clam species, with a shell that can stretch more than a meter across.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/true-giant-clam)

Fact id: `mollusks.true-giant-clam.size` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA true giant clam page says Tridacna gigas is the largest species, reaching over a meter wide.</sub>

### dd-0429 · hard · unverified

**How does a young giant clam acquire its algae partners?**

- A. Inherited inside the egg from its mother
- B. **Taken in from the surrounding seawater** ✓
- C. Produced from its own body cells

Giant clams aren't born with their algae. Juveniles take them in from the water around the time they settle, and the algae spread through tubes into the mantle.

Source: [Australian Museum (Lizard Island Field Guide)](https://lifg.australian.museum/Group.html?groupId=wuDqZWyv) · second source: [Australian Museum (Lizard Island Field Guide)](https://lifg.australian.museum/Group.html?groupId=Ok7OQ2sH)

Fact id: `mollusks.giant-clam.algae-acquired-from-water` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Lizard Island guide: algae sit in tubules branching from the gut through the mantle, and the clam takes the algae in from the water when it settles as a juvenile.</sub>

### dd-0430 · medium · rejected

**Like corals, giant clams can 'bleach'. What happens when they do?**

- A. **They lose the algae living in their tissues** ✓
- B. Their shells dissolve and turn white
- C. They shed their whole mantle

Heat stress and intense light can drive out the food-making algae in a giant clam's mantle, much as happens to bleaching corals.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/s3/2024-07/StatusReview-GiantClams-508.pdf) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries (hosted petition)](https://media.fisheries.noaa.gov/dam-migration/clam_petition_2016_opr3.pdf)

Fact id: `mollusks.giant-clam.bleaching` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary: mantle algae density dropped sharply under prolonged high light, and the 1998 bleaching event cut algae in T. gigas about 30-fold (petition). Status review expected to discuss clam bleaching; verifier check.</sub>

### dd-0431 · medium · unverified

**Under good conditions, roughly how much water can one adult oyster filter in a day?**

- A. About 2 gallons
- B. About 2,000 gallons
- C. **About 50 gallons** ✓

A single adult oyster can strain around 50 gallons of water a day, pulling out algae and particles, which is why oyster reefs help keep coastal water clear.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/national/habitat-conservation/oyster-reef-habitat) · second source: [NOAA National Centers for Coastal Ocean Science](https://coastalscience.noaa.gov/news/water-cleaning-capacity-of-oysters-could-mean-extra-income-for-chesapeake-bay-growers-video/)

Fact id: `mollusks.oyster.filter-50-gallons` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA oyster reef habitat page: one adult oyster can filter 50 gallons a day under certain conditions; NCCOS Chesapeake item says more than 50 gallons a day.</sub>

### dd-0432 · medium · unverified

**Oyster farms can improve coastal water by removing which nutrient pollutant?**

- A. Mercury
- B. Chlorine
- C. **Nitrogen** ✓

Oysters filter out algae fed by excess nitrogen and lock some of that nitrogen into their shells and tissues, removing it from the water.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/s3/2022-03/Fact-Sheet-Aquaculture-Provides-Beneficial-Ecosystem-Services.pdf) · second source: [NOAA Sea Grant](https://seagrant.noaa.gov/Woods-Hole-Sea-Grant-research-documents-first-nitrogen-removal-by-oysters-in-local-waters/)

Fact id: `mollusks.oyster.nitrogen-removal` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA aquaculture fact sheet 'Removing Nitrogen, Improving Water Quality': oysters remove nitrogen by incorporating it into shells and tissues; Woods Hole Sea Grant documented nitrogen removal by oysters.</sub>

### dd-0433 · easy · unverified

**Why do coastal programs collect used oyster shells from restaurants?**

- A. To grind them into road salt
- B. **To return them to the water to build new reefs** ✓
- C. To use them as fishing bait

Young oysters need a hard surface to settle on, and old shells are ideal, so recycled shells help rebuild reefs that protect coastlines.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/gulf-coast-oyster-shell-recycling-key-sustainable-seafood-and-coastal-protection)

Fact id: `mollusks.oyster.shell-recycling-reefs` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Gulf Coast feature on oyster shell recycling as key to sustainable seafood and coastal protection (title); verifier should confirm shells are returned to build reef habitat for young oysters.</sub>

### dd-0434 · medium · unverified

**What mainly focuses the image in a scallop's eye?**

- A. **A curved mirror at the back of the eye** ✓
- B. A thick crystal lens at the front
- C. A tiny pinhole opening

Scallop eyes work like reflecting telescopes: a concave mirror at the back of each eye reflects light onto the retina to form an image.

Source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aam9506) · second source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/content/article/hubble-bubble-scallop-eyes-act-just-tiny-telescopes)

Fact id: `mollusks.scallop.mirror-eyes` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Palmer et al. 2017 (Science): each scallop eye uses a concave mirror to focus light; Science news compares scallop eyes to tiny telescopes.</sub>

### dd-0435 · hard · unverified

**The mirror inside each scallop eye is built from tiny square tiles of what?**

- A. Silica glass
- B. **Guanine crystals** ✓
- C. Calcium carbonate

The tiles are crystals of guanine, one of the building blocks of DNA, laid out in a mosaic of squares that reduces blurring.

Source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aam9506) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9898273/)

Fact id: `mollusks.scallop.guanine-mirror` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Science paper: the mirror is built from guanine crystals arranged as a mosaic of square tiles, which reduces optical aberrations; later study notes square guanine crystals are unusual.</sub>

### dd-0436 · medium · unverified

**How many eyes can a single scallop have?**

- A. Just two
- B. Several thousand
- C. **Up to about 200** ✓

Depending on the species and the individual, a scallop can have anywhere from about 30 to 200 tiny eyes.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3726758/) · second source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aam9506)

Fact id: `mollusks.scallop.eye-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Transcriptome study: each scallop has between 30 and 200 non-cephalic eyes; Science paper describes a system of up to 200 eyes. Pecten maximus described as having 'hundreds'.</sub>

### dd-0437 · medium · unverified

**Where are a scallop's eyes located?**

- A. On two stalks on its head
- B. Inside its gills
- C. **Along the edge of its mantle** ✓

A scallop's eyes line the fleshy edge of its mantle, so they peek out from between the two shells when it gapes open.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3726758/) · second source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aam9506)

Fact id: `mollusks.scallop.eye-location` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Transcriptome study describes scallop eyes as non-cephalic (not on a head); verifier should confirm the pages state they sit along the mantle edge.</sub>

### dd-0438 · medium · unverified

**Aeolid nudibranchs eat stinging animals like anemones. What happens to the prey's stinging cells?**

- A. They are fully digested for energy
- B. **They are stored in the slug's back for defense** ✓
- C. They are released to attract mates

Unfired stinging cells pass through the slug's gut to sacs at the tips of its cerata, the fingerlike projections on its back, ready to fire at attackers.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/how-sea-slugs-steal-defenses-their-prey) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/opalescent-nudibranch)

Fact id: `mollusks.aeolid-nudibranch.stolen-stinging-cells` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: aeolid nudibranchs eat stinging animals; stinging cells pass through the digestive gland and collect in sacs at the cerata tips, and the slugs shoot them out when threatened.</sub>

### dd-0439 · easy · unverified

**What does the name 'nudibranch' mean?**

- A. **Naked gills** ✓
- B. Soft body
- C. Sea flower

Many nudibranchs carry their gills openly on their backs as feathery plumes rather than tucked inside a shell, which gave the group its name.

Source: [Seattle Aquarium](https://www.seattleaquarium.org/stories/getting-to-know-nudibranchs/)

Fact id: `mollusks.nudibranch.name-naked-gills` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Seattle Aquarium: nudibranch means 'naked gills', referring to the external, feathery gills many species display on their backs.</sub>

### dd-0440 · easy · unverified

**How did the sea hare get its name?**

- A. It moves across the seafloor in hops
- B. **Its head has two projections that look like rabbit ears** ✓
- C. Its skin is covered in fine, furlike hairs

Early observers thought the two rolled sensory organs on its head, called rhinophores, looked like a hare's ears.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/foma/learn/nature/sea-hares.htm) · second source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/molluscs/sea-hare/)

Fact id: `mollusks.sea-hare.name-origin` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS and Australian Museum pages: the 'rabbit ears' are rhinophores, and the head projections are the origin of the common name because they resemble a hare's ears.</sub>

### dd-0441 · medium · unverified

**What do a sea hare's 'rabbit ears', its rhinophores, actually do?**

- A. Pick up sounds in the water
- B. Release heat from the body
- C. **Sense chemicals, much like a nose** ✓

Rhinophores 'smell' chemicals in the water, and they help sea hares track down mates during breeding season.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://home.nps.gov/cabr/blogs/species-spotlight-california-brown-sea-hare.htm) · second source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/foma/learn/nature/sea-hares.htm)

Fact id: `mollusks.sea-hare.rhinophores-smell` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS pages: rhinophores are sensory organs for smell rather than hearing, described as the sea hare's equivalent of a nose and tongue, and they help find mates in breeding season.</sub>

### dd-0442 · easy · unverified

**What does a California brown sea hare release when it is attacked?**

- A. **A cloud of purple ink** ✓
- B. A flash of blue light
- C. A burst of air bubbles

The purple cloud can act as a smoke screen, and it is irritating and bad-tasting, coating a predator's senses and mouth.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/california_brown_sea_hare) · second source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://home.nps.gov/cabr/blogs/species-spotlight-california-brown-sea-hare.htm)

Fact id: `mollusks.california-brown-sea-hare.ink-defense` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Sources describe sea hare purple ink as a defensive cloud that distracts or blinds predators, irritating and unpalatable, coating predators' sensory structures and mouth in Aplysia.</sub>

### dd-0443 · medium · unverified

**Where does the color of a sea hare's purple ink come from?**

- A. **Pigments from the red algae it eats** ✓
- B. Iron compounds in its blood
- C. Bacteria living in its gills

Sea hares graze on seaweed, and pigments from the red algae in their diet end up in their ink, giving it its vivid purple color.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://home.nps.gov/cabr/blogs/species-spotlight-california-brown-sea-hare.htm) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/sea-hare)

Fact id: `mollusks.sea-hare.ink-color-red-algae` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary of the NPS/aquarium results: the purple color comes from pigments derived from the red algae the animal eats.</sub>

### dd-0444 · easy · unverified

**Research on the sea slug Aplysia helped Eric Kandel win a Nobel Prize for discoveries about what?**

- A. How hearts keep their rhythm
- B. **How memories form at connections between nerve cells** ✓
- C. How the immune system fights infection

Kandel showed that learning strengthens synapses, the junctions between nerve cells, and that long-term memory needs new proteins. He shared the 2000 Nobel Prize.

Source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2000/press-release/) · second source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2000/ceremony-speech/)

Fact id: `mollusks.aplysia.kandel-nobel-memory` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Nobel 2000 press release: Kandel used Aplysia to show how synapse efficiency is modified in learning; long-term memory required new protein synthesis.</sub>

### dd-0445 · hard · unverified

**Which simple reflex in the sea slug Aplysia did Eric Kandel study to learn how memory works?**

- A. **Pulling in its gill when touched** ✓
- B. Squirting ink when grabbed
- C. Flipping over when turned upside down

Aplysia's gill-withdrawal reflex grows stronger after a strong stimulus, and Kandel traced that simple form of learning to changes at the synapses.

Source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2000/press-release/)

Fact id: `mollusks.aplysia.gill-withdrawal-reflex` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Nobel press release: Aplysia has a gill-withdrawal reflex; amplification of the reflex was treated as learning and traced to strengthened synapses between sensory and motor neurons.</sub>

### dd-0446 · medium · unverified

**Why was the sea slug Aplysia a good choice for studying learning?**

- A. It lives for several decades
- B. **It has relatively few nerve cells, and many are large** ✓
- C. Its nerve cells glow under ultraviolet light

With only about 20,000 nerve cells, many of them unusually large, Aplysia let researchers trace the exact circuits behind simple learning.

Source: [NobelPrize.org](https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2000/press-release/)

Fact id: `mollusks.aplysia.few-large-neurons` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Nobel press release: Kandel chose Aplysia because its nervous system has comparatively few nerve cells (around 20,000), many of which are rather large.</sub>

### dd-0447 · easy · unverified

**How many overlapping plates make up a chiton's shell?**

- A. Two
- B. Twenty
- C. **Eight** ✓

A chiton's shell is a row of eight overlapping plates held together by a tough girdle, an arrangement that makes chitons easy to recognize.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/places/chiton.htm)

Fact id: `mollusks.chiton.eight-plates` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS chiton page: chitons' shells are made of eight overlapping plates.</sub>

### dd-0448 · hard · unverified

**Some chitons have tiny eyes built into their shell plates. What are the lenses made of?**

- A. **Aragonite, the same mineral as the shell** ✓
- B. Clear protein, like a human lens
- C. Iron oxide, like their teeth

These chitons see through lenses of aragonite, a form of calcium carbonate, and the lenses can focus simple images.

Source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/content/article/rock-eyes-help-mollusk-see-above-water) · second source: [MIT News](https://news.mit.edu/2015/sea-creature-armor-plating-transparent-ceramic-eyes-1119)

Fact id: `mollusks.chiton.aragonite-eye-lenses` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Speiser et al. 2011 (reported in Science): chiton shell-eye lenses are aragonite rather than protein and can focus images; MIT work notes lenses and shell are both mainly aragonite.</sub>

### dd-0449 · hard · unverified

**What trade-off comes with the eyes built into some chitons' shells?**

- A. The eyes make the shell much heavier
- B. **The eye areas are weak spots in the armor** ✓
- C. The eyes only work in total darkness

Tests found the lens-bearing parts of the shell break more easily, a cost of building eyes directly into protective armor.

Source: [MIT News](https://news.mit.edu/2015/sea-creature-armor-plating-transparent-ceramic-eyes-1119) · second source: [Science (AAAS)](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.350.6263.899)

Fact id: `mollusks.chiton.shell-eyes-weak-spots` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: MIT News and Science: mechanical tests found the lens-containing sections of chiton shell are weaker, creating weak spots in the armor.</sub>

### dd-0450 · medium · unverified

**What is the hard coating on a chiton's teeth made of?**

- A. **Magnetite, an iron mineral** ✓
- B. Nacre, like pearls
- C. Keratin, like fingernails

Chitons take in iron from their food and cap their teeth with magnetite, letting them scrape algae from bare rock.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7850002/)

Fact id: `mollusks.chiton.magnetite-teeth` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Chiton genome paper: chitons take in iron from their diet and keep high iron levels in hemolymph to make new teeth capped with magnetite.</sub>

### dd-0451 · medium · unverified

**What happens as a chiton's teeth wear down from scraping rock?**

- A. The chiton switches to filter feeding
- B. **New teeth move forward from behind to replace them** ✓
- C. Minerals from seawater re-coat the worn teeth

A chiton's radula works like a conveyor belt: fresh teeth form at the back and move forward as the front ones wear away.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7850002/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8611077/)

Fact id: `mollusks.chiton.teeth-replaced` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Genome paper: chitons maintain iron supplies to make new teeth; search summary notes cusps are replaced as they wear with new teeth moving forward from behind; radula paper says radula is constantly regenerated from the back.</sub>

Fact id: `mollusks.gumboot-chiton.hidden-plates` · risk: species-specific

### dd-0454 · easy · unverified

**Despite its name, what kind of animal is a shipworm?**

- A. A segmented worm
- B. **A clam** ✓
- C. A sea snail

Shipworms are wood-boring bivalves, relatives of clams, with long, wormlike bodies that tunnel through submerged wood.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4250168/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8561940/)

Fact id: `mollusks.shipworm.is-a-clam` · risk: none

### dd-0455 · hard · unverified

**Where do the bacteria that help shipworms digest wood live?**

- A. In the shipworm's stomach
- B. On the surface of the wood
- C. **Inside cells in the shipworm's gills** ✓

The bacteria live in special gill cells and make wood-digesting enzymes that are shipped to the gut, which is nearly bacteria-free where wood is broken down.

Source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4250168/) · second source: [PubMed Central (NIH)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8561940/)

Fact id: `mollusks.shipworm.gill-bacteria` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: 2014 study: symbionts live inside specialized gill cells; their wood-degrading enzymes are translocated from gill to gut, and the wood-digesting caecum is nearly bacteria-free. 2021 study traced the transport path.</sub>

### dd-0456 · medium · unverified

**How does a moon snail get at the clam inside a closed shell?**

- A. It crushes the shell with powerful jaws
- B. **It drills a hole through the shell** ✓
- C. It pries the shells apart with its foot

A moon snail wraps a clam in its large foot, then bores a neat hole with its toothed radula and acid, and feeds through the opening.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/calo/learn/nature/mollusks.htm) · second source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/science/drill-holes-in-fossil-shells-point-to-bigger-predators-picking-on-small-prey/)

Fact id: `mollusks.moon-snail.drills-shells` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS: Atlantic moon snail covers the clam with its expanded foot and drills into the shell with its radula; Florida Museum: moon snails use radula teeth and acids to penetrate shells.</sub>

### dd-0457 · hard · unverified

**What can the width of a drill hole in a fossil shell tell paleontologists?**

- A. **How big the predator that drilled it was** ✓
- B. How old the prey was when it died
- C. How warm the sea was at the time

A study of 556 measurements found that bigger drilling predators, such as predatory snails, make wider holes, so fossil holes hint at the hunters' size.

Source: [Florida Museum of Natural History](https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/science/drill-holes-in-fossil-shells-point-to-bigger-predators-picking-on-small-prey/)

Fact id: `mollusks.fossil-drill-holes.predator-size` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Florida Museum: compiled 556 measurements of predator sizes and drill-hole diameters and found a strong correlation between predator size and hole diameter.</sub>

- A. About 1 year
- B. **A few decades** ✓
- C. More than 200 years

NOAA says queen conch generally reach 25 to 30 years old, though estimates vary, and they take about 3.5 to 4 years to mature.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/species/queen-conch) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries Southeast Fisheries Science Center](https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/data/oceans/coris/library/NOAA/CRCP/project/1873/conserve_conch_lesson_plans_st_john_usvi.pdf)

Fact id: `mollusks.queen-conch.lifespan` · risk: disputed-check

### dd-0459 · easy · unverified

**What is a snail's radula?**

- A. A trapdoor that seals its shell
- B. A breathing tube
- C. **A tongue-like ribbon covered in tiny teeth** ✓

Snails use the radula, a ribbon lined with rows of tiny teeth, to scrape or cut food before swallowing it.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/snails-rainbow-radula)

Fact id: `mollusks.snail.radula` · risk: none

### dd-0460 · medium · unverified

**Over time, what can large numbers of grazing periwinkles do to a rocky shore?**

- A. **Slowly wear down the rock** ✓
- B. Turn the rock a deep purple
- C. Make the rock magnetic

Periwinkles scrape algae off rocks with thousands of microscopic teeth, and the scraping of many snails over time can wear down the rock itself.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/periwinkles-fast-facts.htm)

Fact id: `mollusks.periwinkle.erodes-rock` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NPS Periwinkles Fast Facts: periwinkles scrape algae with thousands of microscopic teeth, and the combined scraping of many individuals can wear down rock over time.</sub>

## echinoderms (55)

### dd-0461 · easy · unverified

**Starfish, also called sea stars, belong to which group of animals?**

- A. **Echinoderms** ✓
- B. Bony fish
- C. Mollusks

Despite the name, starfish aren't fish at all. They're echinoderms, cousins of sea urchins and sand dollars, which is why scientists prefer the name sea star.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/starfish.html)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.not-a-fish` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA says starfish are not fish but echinoderms, related to sea urchins and sand dollars, and are also called sea stars.</sub>

### dd-0462 · medium · unverified

**Sea stars have no blood like ours. What fills the canals that work their tube feet?**

- A. A copper-based blue blood
- B. Air held in hollow tubes
- C. **Mostly seawater** ✓

Sea stars have a water vascular system: a network of canals filled with a seawater-based fluid. Changing the pressure in it extends and retracts the tube feet.

Source: [NOAA National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/starfish.html) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/bat-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.water-vascular-system` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: NOAA states sea water, instead of blood, is used to pump nutrients through their bodies via a water vascular system. MBA profiles also mention a water vascular system instead of a traditional circulatory system.</sub>

### dd-0463 · hard · unverified

**Sea star tube feet grip with a sticky secretion. How do they let go?**

- A. They shed the tip of each foot
- B. **They release a second secretion** ✓
- C. They wait for the glue to dry

Each tube foot uses one secretion to stick to a surface and another to unstick, so a sea star can grip firmly yet keep walking.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6122182/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.tube-feet-release` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian says sea stars walk on thousands of tube feet, each using adhesive secretions to grip and a second secretion to let go. The PMC paper describes strong but temporary attachment by the tube foot disc.</sub>

### dd-0464 · medium · unverified

**Roughly how fast can a sunflower sea star move across the seafloor?**

- A. About 1 centimeter per hour
- B. **About 1 meter per minute** ✓
- C. About 10 meters per minute

Most sea stars creep, but the sunflower sea star can reach about a meter (40 inches) a minute, quick enough to catch scuttling crabs.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives) · second source: [NOAA Central Library](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/56479/noaa_56479_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.speed` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: sunflower sea star can reach speeds up to 40 inches per minute, fast enough to snag crabs. NOAA bibliography: adults can move at one meter per minute.</sub>

### dd-0465 · hard · unverified

**About how many tube feet does an adult sunflower sea star move on?**

- A. About 150
- B. About 15 million
- C. **About 15,000** ✓

An adult sunflower sea star glides on roughly 15,000 tube feet, which is how it manages such a brisk pace for a sea star.

Source: [NOAA Central Library](https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/56479/noaa_56479_DS1.pdf)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.tube-foot-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: The NOAA bibliography says adult sunflower sea stars move at about one meter per minute using 15,000 tube feet.</sub>

### dd-0466 · easy · unverified

**What can a sea star do if a predator damages one of its arms?**

- A. **Drop the arm and grow a new one** ✓
- B. Pull the arm inside its body
- C. Harden the arm into a shell

Many sea stars can detach a damaged arm, sometimes to escape a predator, and then slowly grow a healthy replacement.

Source: [Smithsonian Science Education Center](https://ssec.si.edu/stemvisions-blog/all-about-starfish) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.arm-autotomy` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: SSEC: if an arm is damaged by a predator such as a crab or another starfish, the starfish can detach it and grow a new one. Smithsonian Ocean: most species can regrow lost arms and some drop an arm to escape.</sub>

### dd-0467 · easy · unverified

**How do many sea stars start digesting a meal like a mussel?**

- A. They grind it with hard teeth
- B. They soak it up through their skin
- C. **They push their stomach out onto it** ✓

Many sea stars push their stomach out of their body and over the prey, digesting it outside before pulling the stomach back in.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/starfish-feeding-mussels)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.external-stomach` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: some sea stars push their stomachs out over prey and digest it externally before pulling the stomach back in.</sub>

### dd-0468 · medium · unverified

**How does a sea star get into a clam or mussel that is shut tight?**

- A. It drills a hole with a rasping tongue
- B. **It pulls the shells apart with its tube feet** ✓
- C. It cracks the shell against a rock

A sea star suctions its tube feet onto both halves of the shell and slowly pulls them apart.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/creaturefeatureacadiaseastar.htm)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.prying-bivalves` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NPS says sea stars use their tube feet to suction onto the shells of bivalves (animals with two shells) and pry them apart.</sub>

### dd-0469 · medium · unverified

**How long can it take an ochre sea star to digest a meal?**

- A. **Two to three days** ✓
- B. Two to three minutes
- C. Two to three months

Ochre sea stars release enzymes that turn prey tissue to liquid, and the whole digestion process can take two to three days.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/ochre-sea-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.ochre-star.digestion-time` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: ochre sea stars release digestive enzymes that liquefy prey tissues; digestion can take two to three days.</sub>

### dd-0470 · medium · unverified

**How many stomachs does an ochre sea star have?**

- A. One
- B. Four
- C. **Two** ✓

The ochre sea star has two: a cardiac stomach that it pushes out through its mouth onto prey, and an internal pyloric stomach.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/ochre-sea-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.ochre-star.two-stomachs` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific describes a cardiac stomach that extends out through the mouth and an internal pyloric stomach.</sub>

### dd-0471 · medium · unverified

**Where are a bat star's light-sensing eyespots?**

- A. Around its mouth
- B. **At the tip of each arm** ✓
- C. In the middle of its upper side

Like other sea stars, bat stars have a simple eyespot at the end of each arm. It senses light rather than forming sharp images and helps the star find its way.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/bat_star) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/bat-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.bat-star.eyespot-location` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: bat stars have eye spots at the end of each arm to aid in finding food. MBA also mentions light-detecting eyespots.</sub>

### dd-0472 · easy · unverified

**Why is the bat star called a bat star?**

- A. **Webbing between its arms looks batlike** ✓
- B. It feeds only at night
- C. It hangs upside down in sea caves

The bat star's short, triangular arms are joined by webbing, giving it the look of a bat's wing. It's a scavenger found from Alaska to Baja California.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/bat-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.bat-star.name-origin` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: MBA: bat star has webbing between its short, triangular arms giving it a batlike look; scavenger found from Alaska to Baja California.</sub>

### dd-0473 · medium · unverified

**What does a sunflower sea star do with a sea urchin it catches?**

- A. Cracks it open with its arms
- B. Drags it back to a burrow
- C. **Swallows it whole, then spits out the shell** ✓

The sunflower sea star can swallow an entire sea urchin, digest it inside its body and then expel the empty, spiny shell.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/sunflower-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.swallows-urchin` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: MBA: a sunflower star can swallow an entire sea urchin, digest it internally and then expel the urchin's test.</sub>

### dd-0474 · medium · review

**In 2021, how did the IUCN Red List classify the sunflower sea star?**

- A. **Critically endangered** ✓
- B. Least concern
- C. Near threatened

After a disease outbreak wiped out most of the population, the IUCN listed the sunflower sea star as critically endangered in 2021.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/noaa-fisheries-weighs-protection-sunflower-sea-star-native-west-coast) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11961252/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.iucn-status` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: NOAA Fisheries reports that in August 2021 the IUCN added the sunflower sea star to its Red List as critically endangered. One other NOAA-affiliated page gave 2020; the question says 2021 per the NOAA Fisheries feature.</sub>

### dd-0475 · medium · unverified

**Sea star wasting disease killed roughly what share of sunflower sea stars from 2013 to 2017?**

- A. About 10 percent
- B. **More than 90 percent** ✓
- C. About 40 percent

The wasting outbreak killed more than 90 percent of sunflower sea stars between 2013 and 2017, and recovery in the wild has been slow.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/noaa-fisheries-proposes-listing-sunflower-sea-star-threatened-under-endangered-species) · second source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/noaa-fisheries-weighs-protection-sunflower-sea-star-native-west-coast)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.wasting-die-off` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA results said the outbreak killed more than 90 percent of sunflower sea stars from 2013 to 2017. The search summary did not say which of the two NOAA Fisheries pages carried this line; verifier should confirm on either.</sub>

### dd-0476 · easy · unverified

**What happens to a sea star with sea star wasting syndrome?**

- A. It turns white and floats away
- B. It grows extra arms that wither
- C. **It gets lesions, loses arms and falls apart** ✓

Affected sea stars become sluggish, develop lesions and lose their arms, and within days can disintegrate into a gooey mass.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/noaa-fisheries-weighs-protection-sunflower-sea-star-native-west-coast) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sickly-starfish-qa-dr-chris-mah)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.wasting-symptoms` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA result: stars with the syndrome become lethargic, develop lesions, lose their arms and within days disintegrate into gooey masses. Smithsonian describes a condition that causes tissue to decay. Verifier: confirm which NOAA page holds the symptom wording.</sub>

### dd-0477 · hard · review

**Where did a recent study find surviving groups of large adult sunflower sea stars?**

- A. Coral reefs off Hawaii
- B. **Fjords in British Columbia** ✓
- C. The Gulf of Mexico

Conditions in British Columbia's fjords appear to have sheltered remnant populations of large adults, which may help efforts to bring the species back.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11961252/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sunflower-star.fjord-refuge` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: The study found remnant populations of large adult Pycnopodia helianthoides in British Columbia fjords, which act as a refuge.</sub>

### dd-0478 · medium · unverified

**Ochre and sunflower sea stars are called keystone species. What does that mean?**

- A. **They strongly shape the community around them** ✓
- B. They are the most numerous animals on the shore
- C. They live only on rocky arches

A keystone species has an outsized effect on its ecosystem. Remove these predatory sea stars and the mix of animals around them changes dramatically.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sickly-starfish-qa-dr-chris-mah) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-star.keystone-species` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian results: species such as the ochre and sunflower sea stars are known as keystone species, whose presence affects the structure and dynamics of the animals around them profoundly. Summary merged Smithsonian pages; verifier should confirm which page has it.</sub>

### dd-0479 · easy · unverified

**What does the crown-of-thorns sea star feed on?**

- A. Seagrass
- B. **Coral** ✓
- C. Jellyfish

The crown-of-thorns sea star eats living coral, so when its numbers boom it can damage large areas of reef.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/plague-sea-stars)

Fact id: `echinoderms.crown-of-thorns.diet` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: the crown-of-thorns species eats coral; its population booms are linked to nutrient runoff that feeds the larvae.</sub>

### dd-0480 · hard · review

**How does the deep-sea sunstar of the northeastern Pacific get its food?**

- A. It grazes on bacterial mats
- B. It filters drifting plankton
- C. **It speeds over the seafloor stalking prey** ✓

This many-armed deep-sea star moves quickly over the bottom, hunting crustaceans, mollusks and even fish, at depths from about 30 to 3,500 meters.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/deep-sea-sunstar)

Fact id: `echinoderms.deep-sea-sunstar.hunting` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: MBA: the deep-sea sunstar (Rathbunaster californicus) lives in the northeastern Pacific at about 30 to 3,500 m, speeds across the seafloor and stalks crustaceans, molluscs and fishes.</sub>

### dd-0481 · hard · unverified

**How does the deep-sea fire star (Hymenodiscus) catch its food?**

- A. **Its spiny arms trap drifting plankton** ✓
- B. It pries open clams on the seafloor
- C. It scrapes algae off rocks

Unlike sea stars that hunt clams, the fire star holds up long arms covered in spines that snag plankton from the water.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/fire-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.fire-star.plankton-spines` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: MBA: the deep-sea fire star (Hymenodiscus sp.) has spines covering each long arm that help it trap plankton.</sub>

### dd-0482 · medium · unverified

**A sea urchin's jaw is known as whose 'lantern'?**

- A. Archimedes'
- B. Pythagoras'
- C. **Aristotle's** ✓

The name comes from Aristotle, who more than 2,000 years ago compared a sea urchin to a lantern. It was later attached to the urchin's five-toothed jaw apparatus.

Source: [NOAA Fisheries](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/feature-story/meet-oonee-sea-urchin-ranchers-oregon) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-urchins-strange-and-spiny-wonders-of-the-ocean.html)

Fact id: `echinoderms.urchin.aristotles-lantern-name` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search result attributed to the NOAA OoNee profile: Aristotle noticed the structure resembled a lantern frame more than 2,000 years ago. NHM calls the urchin's chewing apparatus Aristotle's lantern.</sub>

### dd-0483 · medium · unverified

**How many teeth does a sea urchin's jaw (Aristotle's lantern) hold?**

- A. Two
- B. Thirty-two
- C. **Five** ✓

Like much of an urchin's body, its jaw has five-part symmetry: five pyramid-shaped sections, each holding a single tooth.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4727832/) · second source: [Smithsonian Qrius](https://qrius.si.edu/browse/object/10008964)

Fact id: `echinoderms.urchin.lantern-tooth-count` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: PMC review: Aristotle's lantern has five-fold symmetry with five pyramids, each holding one tooth. Smithsonian Qrius describes the teeth used for chewing and scraping.</sub>

### dd-0484 · hard · unverified

**What is unusual about a sea urchin's teeth?**

- A. **They keep growing throughout its life** ✓
- B. They are made of the same enamel as ours
- C. They form only once, in the larva

Each urchin tooth keeps growing for the animal's whole life, replacing the material it wears away scraping algae and rock.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4759641/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4727832/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.urchin.teeth-keep-growing` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: PMC4759641: each pyramid houses a single tooth that keeps growing throughout the urchin's life. PMC4727832: teeth wear down from abrasion and the body keeps replacing the material.</sub>

**How do purple sea urchins make hiding places on rocky shores?**

- A. They borrow empty snail shells
- B. **They grind hollows into the rock** ✓
- C. They glue pebbles into a wall

Purple sea urchins can slowly grind into solid rock with their teeth and spines, carving snug hollows that shelter them from waves and predators.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/purple-sea-urchin)

Fact id: `echinoderms.purple-urchin.rock-boring` · risk: species-specific

- A. A carapace
- B. A mantle
- C. **A test** ✓

An urchin's spines sit on a rigid, rounded skeleton called a test. Its parts are arranged in five matching sections.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/purple-sea-urchin) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/sea-urchins-strange-and-spiny-wonders-of-the-ocean.html)

Fact id: `echinoderms.urchin.test-name` · risk: none

Sea otters eat sea urchins, and by keeping their numbers down they help protect the giant kelp the urchins would otherwise devour.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://montereybayaquarium.org/animals/animals-a-to-z/fragile-pink-sea-urchin)

### dd-0494 · hard · unverified

- A. It has a tiny eye at each spine tip
- B. It senses light only through its mouth
- C. **Its whole body works like one big eye** ✓

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/amazing-eyes-vision-champions.html)

### dd-0496 · easy · unverified

**What covers the body of a living sand dollar?**

- A. A smooth, bare shell
- B. **Tiny spines that look like velvet** ✓
- C. A coat of sticky slime

A living sand dollar is covered in short, hair-like spines that give it a fuzzy, velvety look.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/sand-dollar)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sand-dollar.velvet-spines` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: MBA/Smithsonian results: live sand dollars are covered in tiny short, hair-like spines that make the surface look fuzzy or velvety.</sub>

### dd-0497 · hard · unverified

**How do young sand dollars avoid being swept away by rough water?**

- A. They anchor with a sticky thread
- B. **They swallow sand grains as ballast** ✓
- C. They hold onto adults' spines

Young sand dollars swallow heavy sand grains that act like a weight belt, while adults lie flat or burrow when the water gets rough.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/sand-dollar)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sand-dollar.sand-ballast` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: MBA: in rough water sand dollars lie flat or burrow, and young ones swallow sand grains as ballast.</sub>

### dd-0498 · medium · unverified

**How does the Pacific sand dollar often position itself to feed?**

- A. **Standing on edge, partly buried in sand** ✓
- B. Floating upside down at the surface
- C. Clinging to the underside of kelp

The Pacific sand dollar can dig in and stand upright, catching drifting algae, tiny crustacean larvae and other bits of food from the water.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/pacific_sand_dollar)

Fact id: `echinoderms.pacific-sand-dollar.feeding-on-edge` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: the Pacific sand dollar (Dendraster excentricus) digs in to stand vertically and suspension-feeds on algae, larval crustaceans and detritus.</sub>

### dd-0499 · medium · unverified

**Up to how long can a Pacific sand dollar live in the wild?**

- A. About 15 weeks
- B. About 150 years
- C. **More than a decade** ✓

Pacific sand dollars are surprisingly long-lived for their size: they can survive for more than a decade in the wild.

Source: [Aquarium of the Pacific](https://www.aquariumofpacific.org/onlinelearningcenter/species/pacific_sand_dollar)

Fact id: `echinoderms.pacific-sand-dollar.lifespan` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Aquarium of the Pacific: wild Pacific sand dollars can live up to about 15 years.</sub>

### dd-0500 · medium · unverified

**What can many sea cucumbers do when attacked?**

- A. Shed a hard outer shell
- B. Release a cloud of dark ink
- C. **Expel their internal organs and regrow them** ✓

Many sea cucumbers can push out some or all of their internal organs to distract a predator, then regenerate them afterward.

Source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9332576/) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/johnsons-sea-cucumber)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-cucumber.evisceration` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: PMC review: many sea cucumber species can expel most or all internal organs when threatened and regenerate them. MBA Johnson's sea cucumber page: expelled organs can distract a predator.</sub>

### dd-0501 · medium · unverified

**How do many sea cucumbers take in water to breathe?**

- A. **Through their anus** ✓
- B. Through gill slits on their sides
- C. Through a blowhole on top

Many sea cucumbers pump seawater in and out through the anus into branched organs called respiratory trees, where they absorb oxygen.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/pearlfish-sea-cucumber) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/sea-stars-urchins-and-relatives)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-cucumber.anal-breathing` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian pearlfish page: a sea cucumber breathes by taking in water through its anus. Smithsonian echinoderms page: sea cucumbers absorb oxygen by forcing water through hollow branched organs called respiratory trees.</sub>

### dd-0502 · medium · unverified

**Which slender fish is known for living inside sea cucumbers?**

- A. The clownfish
- B. **The pearlfish** ✓
- C. The pufferfish

Pearlfish wait for a sea cucumber to open its rear end to breathe, then slip inside to shelter. Some species even eat their host's organs.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/pearlfish-sea-cucumber)

Fact id: `echinoderms.pearlfish.lives-in-sea-cucumber` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: pearlfish are slender, eel-shaped fish that often live inside sea cucumbers, swimming in when the cucumber opens to breathe; in some species the pearlfish is a parasite that eats the host's gonads and other organs.</sub>

### dd-0503 · medium · unverified

**Some sea cucumbers defend themselves by shooting out what?**

- A. Sharp, needle-like spines
- B. **Sticky threads that tangle attackers** ✓
- C. Jets of stinging bubbles

Some sea cucumbers expel sticky threads called Cuvierian tubules that can tangle or distract a predator while the sea cucumber gets away.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/what-is-a-sea-cucumber.html)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-cucumber.cuvierian-tubules` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search summary (citing NHM and MBA): expelled organs, often sticky Cuvierian tubules, can tangle or distract a predator, giving the animal time to escape.</sub>

### dd-0504 · easy · unverified

**What important job do many sea cucumbers do on the seafloor?**

- A. **They recycle waste by eating sediment** ✓
- B. They build reefs from their skeletons
- C. They pollinate seagrass meadows

Many sea cucumbers swallow sediment, digest the organic bits and leave cleaner sand behind, recycling ocean waste as they crawl along.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/what-is-a-sea-cucumber.html) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9879929/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.sea-cucumber.seafloor-recyclers` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NHM: sea cucumbers move slowly over the sea floor recycling ocean waste products. Research results: deposit feeders ingest sediment and organic material, reducing organic waste.</sub>

### dd-0505 · medium · unverified

**How do some feather stars swim?**

- A. By flapping a tail-like fin
- B. By squirting water from a siphon
- C. **By waving their feathery arms** ✓

Some feather stars can lift off and swim by beating their arms up and down, though most swimmers only do so for short bursts.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/feather-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.feather-star.swimming` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian: some species of feather stars are capable of using their arms to swim; most which swim do so only for a short duration.</sub>

### dd-0506 · medium · unverified

**What do feather stars use to cling to the seafloor or a coral?**

- A. **Small leg-like hooks called cirri** ✓
- B. Suction cups on their arm tips
- C. A cement they produce from their mouth

Feather stars hold on with cirri, short hooked 'legs' under the body that grip rocks, corals or sponges while the arms catch food.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/sea-stars/orange-feather-star/) · second source: [PMC (NCBI)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9297031/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.feather-star.cirri` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum: little 'legs' called cirri keep feather stars attached to the sea bottom. PMC paper: the centrodorsal bears hook-like cirri for clinging to the seafloor or other invertebrates such as corals and sponges.</sub>

### dd-0507 · medium · unverified

**How does a stalked crinoid, or sea lily, feed?**

- A. It hunts crabs on the seafloor
- B. **It holds its arms up to catch drifting food** ✓
- C. It scrapes algae off nearby rocks

Anchored in place, a stalked crinoid spreads its arms into the water column and catches food particles carried past by the current.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/multimedia/stalked-crinoid/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/feather-star)

Fact id: `echinoderms.stalked-crinoid.feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Exploration describes a stalked crinoid anchored with arms extended into the water column for feeding. Smithsonian: crinoids are suspension (filter) feeders. 'Sea lily' is the common name for stalked crinoids; verifier should confirm the page uses it or drop the alias.</sub>

### dd-0508 · medium · unverified

**When do basket stars usually climb up and spread their arms to feed?**

- A. At midday
- B. At dawn only
- C. **At night** ✓

Basket stars tend to hide in crevices by day, then climb to a high spot at night and unfurl their branching arms to catch plankton.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Today](https://oceantoday.noaa.gov/creaturesofthedeep_basketstar/) · second source: [NOAA Ocean Today](https://oceantoday.noaa.gov/fullmoon-basketstar/welcome.html)

Fact id: `echinoderms.basket-star.night-feeding` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Today: basket stars usually hide in crevices during the day and then climb to an elevated point to feed at night, extending arms into the current to catch planktonic animals.</sub>

**What helps a basket star hold onto the tiny animals it catches?**

- A. **Small hooks along its arms** ✓
- B. Stinging cells on its arm tips
- C. A sticky net of silk

Basket star arms bear tiny hooks that help them grab zooplankton such as copepods as the arms sweep through the current.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/multimedia/basket-star-2/)

Fact id: `echinoderms.basket-star.arm-hooks` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Exploration: each basket star arm has several hooks that let it efficiently grab a meal; it feasts mainly on zooplankton.</sub>

- A. Jellyfish
- B. **Sea cucumber** ✓
- C. Octopus

Sea cucumbers are echinoderms, in the same group as sea stars, sea urchins and sand dollars, despite their soft, sausage-shaped bodies.

- B. A cloud of ink
- C. Mild electric shocks

Scattered among a purple urchin's spines are tiny pincers. Together with the spines, they help it fend off threats and grab bits of food.

## deep-sea (55)

### dd-0516 · medium · unverified

**What produces the glow in the lure of a deep-sea anglerfish?**

- A. **Bacteria living inside the lure** ✓
- B. Chemicals from the fish's last meal
- C. Heat given off by the fish's body

Glowing bacteria take up residence in the lure, called the esca, turning it into a living light for attracting prey.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/meet-tiny-bacteria-give-anglerfishes-their-spooky-glow) · second source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/animal/deep-sea-anglerfish/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.lure-glow-source` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian result: tiny glowing bacteria (Photobacterium) live in the anglerfish's esca and provide its glow, not the fish itself.</sub>

### dd-0517 · medium · unverified

**The deep-sea anglerfish's fishing lure is a modified version of which body part?**

- A. A front tooth
- B. A gill flap
- C. **A dorsal fin ray** ✓

Over millions of years, the front of the anglerfish's dorsal fin evolved into a rod tipped with a glowing lure.

Source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/animal/deep-sea-anglerfish/) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/anglerfish-lure-prey-throughout-ocean)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.lure-modified-fin` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Results said the lure is a modified fin ray; the front elements of the fin evolved into the luring apparatus.</sub>

### dd-0518 · hard · unverified

**How do the genomes of the glowing bacteria in deep-sea anglerfish lures compare with those of their free-living relatives?**

- A. About twice the size
- B. **About half the size** ✓
- C. About ten times the size

A 2018 study found these lure bacteria have genomes about 50% smaller than their free-living relatives, and the shrinking is still under way.

Source: [mBio (via NCBI PMC)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6020299/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.symbiont-genome-reduction` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: The 2018 mBio paper reported deep-sea anglerfish symbiont genomes are reduced by about 50% relative to free-living relatives; title calls it ongoing genome reduction.</sub>

### dd-0519 · medium · unverified

**What do the glowing bacteria get from living in a deep-sea anglerfish's lure?**

- A. **A safe home and nutrients** ✓
- B. A ride up to the sunlit surface
- C. Protection from freezing water

It's a two-way deal: the bacteria get shelter and food from the fish, and the anglerfish gets a glowing lure to hunt with.

Source: [NOAA National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2025/sanctuary-symbioses.html) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/fish/meet-tiny-bacteria-give-anglerfishes-their-spooky-glow)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.bacteria-mutualism` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Result said the bacteria gain a safe habitat and nutrients from the anglerfish, while the fish gets an effective hunting tool.</sub>

### dd-0520 · medium · unverified

**How are tiny male deep-sea anglerfish thought to track down females in the dark?**

- A. By listening for the female's calls
- B. By sensing the female's body heat
- C. **By following the female's pheromones** ✓

Males have large nostrils and a keen sense of smell, and are thought to follow chemical signals (pheromones) given off by females.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/bizarre-love-life-of-the-anglerfish.html) · second source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/animal/deep-sea-anglerfish/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.male-finds-female-by-smell` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: Search summary: tiny males have a strong sense of smell and follow pheromones to find females. The summary did not make clear which of the NHM/MBARI pages states this; verifier should check both.</sub>

### dd-0521 · easy · unverified

**In deep-sea anglerfish, how do males compare in size with females?**

- A. Males are much larger
- B. **Males are much smaller** ✓
- C. Both are about the same size

The males are tiny next to the females, and in some species they attach themselves to their much larger mates.

Source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/animal/deep-sea-anglerfish/) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/bizarre-love-life-of-the-anglerfish.html)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.male-size` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Results described tiny males that attach to much larger females, and MBARI noted that only some species have parasitic males.</sub>

### dd-0522 · hard · unverified

**How does a male soft leftvent angler (Haplophryne mollis) attach to a female?**

- A. **He bites her and his mouth fuses to her** ✓
- B. He wraps his fins tightly around her tail
- C. He glues himself on with sticky mucus

A free-living male bites the female, and over time the skin of his mouth fuses with hers.

Source: [Australian Museum](https://australian.museum/learn/animals/fishes/soft-leafvent-angler-haplophryne-mollis-brauer-1902/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.soft-leftvent-bite-fusion` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Australian Museum: when a free-living male encounters a female it bites her, and the skin of his mouth eventually fuses with hers.</sub>

### dd-0523 · medium · unverified

**In deep-sea anglerfish species where males fuse permanently to females, what happens to the male's eyes?**

- A. They grow much larger
- B. They begin to glow
- C. **They degenerate** ✓

Once attached he no longer needs them, so his eyes degenerate, and eventually his bloodstream links up with the female's.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/anglerfish/) · second source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/bizarre-love-life-of-the-anglerfish.html)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.fused-male-loses-eyes` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Result said fused males lose their eyes because they no longer need them and the male's bloodstream eventually connects with hers.</sub>

### dd-0524 · hard · unverified

**Why don't some female deep-sea anglerfish reject the males that fuse to their bodies?**

- A. The males are clones of the females
- B. **They lack key immune system genes** ✓
- C. The males numb the female with venom

Fused tissue would normally trigger rejection, but these anglerfish have lost working genes for critical parts of the adaptive immune system.

Source: [NCBI PMC (peer-reviewed review)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8780861/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.fusion-immune-genes` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Isakov 2022 review: fusion avoids graft rejection; the mating strategy coevolved with loss of functional genes for critical adaptive immune components.</sub>

- A. **Swimming upside down** ✓
- B. Swimming tail-first
- C. Spinning in tight circles

The anglerfish was seen hanging upside down, and scientists suggested the odd posture might be a way of hunting prey.

Source: [Natural History Museum, London](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/news/2023/december/upside-down-deep-sea-anglerfish-might-be-hunting-prey.html)

Fact id: `deep-sea.anglerfish.upside-down-filmed` · risk: surprising

- B. Live fish it hunts with its arms
- C. **Drifting particles called marine snow** ✓

Fact id: `deep-sea.vampire-squid.eats-marine-snow` · risk: none

It drifts with a filament extended like a fishing line, up to eight times as long as its body, then reels in whatever sticks.

Fact id: `deep-sea.vampire-squid.filament-length` · risk: numeric

- A. **Mucus** ✓
- B. Silk
- C. Seaweed

It draws the filament through its arms, packs the particles into a ball of mucus and then eats the whole package.

Source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/news/mbari-researchers-discover-what-vampire-squids-eat-its-not-what-you-think/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.vampire-squid.mucus-food-balls` · risk: none

- C. **A cloud of glowing mucus** ✓

Light organs on its arm tips release glowing clouds of mucus, which scientists think distract predators while the squid gets away.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/vampire-squid) · second source: [MBARI](https://www.mbari.org/animal/vampire-squid/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.vampire-squid.glowing-mucus-defense` · risk: none

- A. Bloodsucking cuttlefish
- B. **Vampire squid from hell** ✓
- C. Night-dwelling octopus

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/invertebrates/vampire-squid-hell) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/vampire-squid)

Fact id: `deep-sea.vampire-squid.name-meaning` · risk: none

- B. A true squid closely related to the giant squid
- C. A jellyfish that looks like a squid

The vampire squid is no squid at all: scientists place it in its own group of cephalopods, separate from true squids.

### dd-0532 · medium · unverified

**The vampire squid thrives in deep water that is unusually low in what?**

- A. Salt
- B. Pressure
- C. **Oxygen** ✓

It lives in the oxygen minimum zone, where its slow, low-energy way of feeding works well and few predators can follow.

- C. **Hemoglobin** ✓

Hemoglobin in the worm's blood ferries sulfide from the plume to the bacteria living inside its body.

Source: [NOAA PMEL](https://www.pmel.noaa.gov/eoi/nemo2003/logbook/sep06.html) · second source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/oceanus/feature/deep-sea-tubeworms-get-versatile-inside-help/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.tube-worm.hemoglobin-sulfide` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Search summary: hemoglobin in the blood carries the sulfide to the bacteria in the trophosome (attributed to the NOAA PMEL NeMO 2003 log).</sub>

### dd-0569 · easy · unverified

**Food webs at deep-sea hydrothermal vents are built mainly on which process?**

- A. Photosynthesis
- B. **Chemosynthesis** ✓
- C. Fermentation

With no sunlight, microbes at vents make food from chemicals in the vent fluid, a process called chemosynthesis.

Source: [NOAA Ocean Exploration](https://oceanexplorer.noaa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/vent-food-web.pdf) · second source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/oceanus/feature/deep-sea-tubeworms-get-versatile-inside-help/)

Fact id: `deep-sea.hydrothermal-vent.chemosynthesis` · risk: disputed-check

<sub>Research note: NOAA Ocean Exploration document titled 'Introduction: Chemosynthesis at Hydrothermal Vents' (vent food web). Search summary did not quote its body text; verifier should confirm.</sub>

**Deep-sea hydrothermal vent communities were first discovered in 1977 near which islands?**

- A. **The Galápagos Islands** ✓
- B. The Hawaiian Islands
- C. The Canary Islands

In 1977 scientists diving near the Galápagos Islands found vents teeming with life, an ecosystem fueled by chemicals rather than sunlight.

Source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution (Mullineaux 2013)](https://website.whoi.edu/gfd/wp-content/uploads/sites/14/2018/10/Mullineaux_2013_MCEC_17_205305.pdf)
