## seabirds (27)

### dd-0571 · easy · unverified

**Through the Antarctic winter, which emperor penguin parent keeps the egg warm on its feet?**

- A. **The father** ✓
- B. The mother
- C. Both parents, swapping daily

The female hands the egg over and heads to sea to feed, while the male balances it on his feet under a warm fold of skin for about two months.

Source: [Australian Antarctic Division](https://www.antarctica.gov.au/about-antarctica/animals/penguins/emperor-penguin/breeding-cycle/) · second source: [Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution](https://www.whoi.edu/ocean-learning-hub/ocean-topics/ocean-life/sea-birds/emperor-penguins/)

Fact id: `seabirds.emperor-penguin.male-incubates-egg` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: AAD breeding-cycle page: emperors build no nest; the male balances the egg on his feet under his brood pouch for 60 to 64 days while the female feeds at sea. WHOI page says males incubate on their feet for two months.</sub>

### dd-0572 · medium · unverified

**While fasting through incubation, male emperor penguins can lose about how much of their body weight?**

- A. Up to about a tenth
- B. **Up to about half** ✓
- C. Almost none

Males can't feed while holding the egg, so the long winter fast takes a heavy toll: they can lose up to around 40 to 45 percent of their body weight.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/penguins)

Fact id: `seabirds.emperor-penguin.male-weight-loss` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Ocean penguins page says incubating males often lose as much as half their body weight. AAD Mawson station notes say fathers have fasted for about 4 months by the time chicks appear.</sub>

### dd-0573 · hard · unverified

**Besides the emperor penguin, which penguin also incubates its single egg on its feet instead of in a nest?**

- A. Gentoo penguin
- B. Little penguin
- C. **King penguin** ✓

Like emperors, king penguins build no nest; the single egg is kept warm balanced on top of a parent's feet.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/penguins)

Fact id: `seabirds.king-penguin.incubates-on-feet` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search summary of the Smithsonian penguins page noted the egg-on-feet incubation is shared by emperor and king penguins; a king penguin study in the results also said king penguins build no nest and incubate a single egg on their feet. Gentoos build stone nests and little penguins use burrows.</sub>

### dd-0574 · easy · unverified

**How do male emperor penguins get through the Antarctic winter while incubating eggs?**

- A. **Huddling together in large groups** ✓
- B. Sheltering in burrows dug in snow
- C. Taking turns feeding at sea every few days

Incubating males crowd together in tight huddles, slowly shuffling so birds take turns in the warm middle, especially in the coldest, windiest weather.

Source: [British Antarctic Survey](https://www.bas.ac.uk/blogpost/halley-diary-august-2004/) · second source: [Australian Antarctic Division](https://www.antarctica.gov.au/about-antarctica/animals/penguins/emperor-penguin/breeding-cycle/)

Fact id: `seabirds.emperor-penguin.winter-huddle` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: British Antarctic Survey Halley diary describes the male emperors spending all winter huddled through all temperatures and winds; AAD pages say males cannot feed while carrying the egg.</sub>

### dd-0575 · hard · unverified

**In 2022, which Antarctic penguin received protection under the US Endangered Species Act?**

- A. Adélie penguin
- B. **Emperor penguin** ✓
- C. Chinstrap penguin

The US Fish and Wildlife Service extended Endangered Species Act protection to the emperor penguin in October 2022.

Source: [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://www.fws.gov/press-release/2022-10/emperor-penguin-gets-endangered-species-act-protections)

Fact id: `seabirds.emperor-penguin.esa-listing-2022` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: US Fish and Wildlife Service press release (URL dated 2022-10) titled 'Emperor Penguin Gets Endangered Species Act Protections'.</sub>

### dd-0576 · medium · unverified

**About how wide can a wandering albatross's wings stretch, tip to tip?**

- A. About 1.5 m (5 ft)
- B. About 7 m (23 ft)
- C. **About 3.5 m (11.5 ft)** ✓

The wandering albatross has the largest wingspan of any living bird, about 3.5 meters (11.5 feet) from tip to tip.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/wandering-albatross) · second source: [British Antarctic Survey](https://legacy.bas.ac.uk/about_antarctica/wildlife/birds/albatross.php)

Fact id: `seabirds.wandering-albatross.wingspan` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Ocean: wandering albatross wingspan 3.5 m (11.5 ft), largest of any bird. BAS: wingspan over three meters, greatest of any living bird. AAD gives 2.5 to 3.5 m.</sub>

### dd-0577 · medium · unverified

**What happens to an Atlantic puffin's colorful bill after the breeding season?**

- A. **It sheds its bright outer plates** ✓
- B. It grows a hard knob on top
- C. It turns from gray to bright orange

Both sexes grow bright plates on the bill each spring for courtship, then shed them, leaving a smaller, duller bill for winter at sea.

Source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/field-guide/bird/atlantic-puffin) · second source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/news/art-seduction-some-birds-exotic-breeding-attire-goes-beyond-flashy-feathers)

Fact id: `seabirds.atlantic-puffin.bill-plates-shed` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Audubon field guide: in winter the bill is smaller and less colorful after the outer plates are shed. Audubon 'Art of Seduction' article: bright plates grow in early spring and are shed after breeding.</sub>

### dd-0578 · hard · unverified

**What happens when ultraviolet light shines on a breeding Atlantic puffin's beak?**

- A. The whole beak turns black
- B. **Streaks on it glow cyan-green** ✓
- C. The beak's colors fade away

Streaks on the breeding bill fluoresce a bright cyan-green under UV light, which may act as a signal to potential mates.

Source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/news/puffins-amp-their-sex-appeal-glowing-beaks)

Fact id: `seabirds.atlantic-puffin.uv-glowing-bill` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Audubon news article reports that if you shine ultraviolet light on a puffin in breeding plumage, streaks on its beak glow a bright cyan-green; the plates are UV-reactive.</sub>

### dd-0579 · medium · unverified

**How does an Atlantic puffin keep hold of a beakful of fish while it catches more?**

- A. It stores the fish in a stretchy throat pouch
- B. It skewers the fish on its hooked bill tip
- C. **Its tongue pins fish against spines in its mouth** ✓

A puffin's tongue presses each catch against spines on the roof of its mouth, so it can hold ten or more small fish at once.

Source: [Audubon Project Puffin](https://projectpuffin.audubon.org/birds/puffin-faqs) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/puffin-mouthful-fish)

Fact id: `seabirds.atlantic-puffin.spiny-tongue-holds-fish` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Project Puffin FAQ: average catch around 10 fish per trip, held by the tongue pressing fish against spines on the palate. Smithsonian: spiny tongues let Atlantic puffins hold ten or more small fish at once.</sub>

### dd-0580 · medium · unverified

**A tufted puffin bringing food to its chick routinely carries how many fish at once?**

- A. **About 5 to 20** ✓
- B. Only 1 or 2
- C. More than 100

Tufted puffins line up small fish crosswise in their bills, routinely carrying 5 to 20 at a time back to the burrow.

Source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Tufted_Puffin/overview) · second source: [National Audubon Society](https://audubon.org/news/tufted-puffins-are-getting-boost-pacific-northwest)

Fact id: `seabirds.tufted-puffin.fish-per-load` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: All About Birds: tufted puffins routinely carry 5 to 20 fish at a time. Audubon Pacific Northwest article: up to 20 fish crosswise in the specialized beak.</sub>

### dd-0581 · medium · unverified

**Which pelicans catch fish by plunge-diving from the air?**

- A. American white and Australian pelicans
- B. **Brown and Peruvian pelicans** ✓
- C. Every pelican species

The brown pelican and its close cousin the Peruvian pelican are the only plunge-divers; other pelicans feed while sitting on the water's surface.

Source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/chis/learn/nature/brown-pelican.htm) · second source: [U.S. National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/guis/learn/nature/brown-pelican.htm)

Fact id: `seabirds.brown-pelican.plunge-diving-species` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NPS brown pelican page (search summary) said the brown pelican and the Peruvian pelican are the only pelican species that plunge dive; all others feed by sitting on the surface. Verifier: the sentence may be on the Gulf Islands page rather than Channel Islands.</sub>

### dd-0582 · hard · unverified

**Just before hitting the water, a diving brown pelican rotates slightly to protect what?**

- A. Its eyes
- B. Its flight feathers
- C. **Its windpipe and esophagus** ✓

Pelicans twist slightly to the left as they hit the water, shielding the windpipe and esophagus, which run along the right side of the neck.

Source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/magazine/10-fun-facts-about-brown-pelican)

Fact id: `seabirds.brown-pelican.dive-rotation` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Audubon '10 Fun Facts' says pelicans rotate their bodies slightly to the left just before impact to protect the esophagus and trachea, on the right side of the neck.</sub>

- A. **His bright blue feet** ✓
- B. An inflated red throat pouch
- C. A crest of long head plumes

Males strut in front of females, lifting their blue feet high one at a time and spreading them to show them off.

Source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Blue-footed_Booby/lifehistory) · second source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Blue-footed_Booby/overview)

Fact id: `seabirds.blue-footed-booby.foot-display` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: All About Birds: during courtship males spread their blue feet and march around lifting their feet high; females also show feet to potential partners.</sub>

### dd-0584 · medium · unverified

**In one study, blue-footed booby chicks were healthiest when their fathers had what?**

- A. The longest wings
- B. **The bluest feet** ✓
- C. The loudest calls

Vivid blue feet seem to signal a well-fed male; one study found chicks of the most intensely blue-footed fathers were the healthiest.

Source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Blue-footed_Booby/overview)

Fact id: `seabirds.blue-footed-booby.blue-feet-fathers-healthy-chicks` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: All About Birds overview: bluer feet help attract mates, and one study found chicks with intensely blue-footed fathers were the healthiest, possibly reflecting the father's feeding ability.</sub>

### dd-0585 · hard · review

**What color are the feet of a young blue-footed booby?**

- A. Bright blue
- B. Red
- C. **Brown** ✓

Despite the name, young blue-footed boobies have brownish feet; the famous blue comes with adulthood.

Source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Blue-footed_Booby/id)

Fact id: `seabirds.blue-footed-booby.juvenile-brown-feet` · risk: species-specific

- B. Tuna
- C. Salmon

Fact id: `seabirds.blue-footed-booby.galapagos-sardine-decline` · risk: none

### dd-0587 · easy · unverified

**How does the brown booby catch most of its food?**

- A. Wading through shallow water
- B. **Plunging headfirst into the water from flight** ✓
- C. Stealing fish from other seabirds

Brown boobies mostly plunge headfirst from flight, usually diving at an angle and from fairly low above the water.

Source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/field-guide/bird/brown-booby)

Fact id: `seabirds.brown-booby.plunge-foraging` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Audubon field guide for brown booby: forages mostly by plunging headfirst into water from flight, usually diving at an angle and from fairly low above the surface.</sub>

### dd-0588 · medium · unverified

**Why do magnificent frigatebirds rarely land on the ocean?**

- A. Their wings are too short for takeoff
- B. Their legs are too long to swim
- C. **Their feathers aren't waterproof** ✓

Unlike nearly all other seabirds, frigatebirds have feathers that aren't waterproof, so they can't float well and seldom settle on the sea.

Source: [National Audubon Society](https://www.audubon.org/news/why-frigatebirds-cant-float) · second source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Magnificent_Frigatebird/overview)

Fact id: `seabirds.magnificent-frigatebird.not-waterproof` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Audubon 'Why Frigatebirds Can't Float': frigatebird feathers, unlike those of nearly all other seabirds, aren't waterproof. All About Birds: they rarely land on water.</sub>

### dd-0589 · easy · unverified

**What does a male magnificent frigatebird inflate to attract a mate?**

- A. **A bright red throat pouch** ✓
- B. A blue air sac on its back
- C. A yellow crest of feathers

Courting males puff up a scarlet throat pouch like a balloon and use it to make a drumming sound.

Source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (Bird Academy)](https://academy.allaboutbirds.org/an-inflatable-throat-magnificent-frigatebird-mating-display/) · second source: [Cornell Lab of Ornithology (All About Birds)](https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Magnificent_Frigatebird/overview)

Fact id: `seabirds.magnificent-frigatebird.red-throat-pouch` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Cornell Bird Academy and All About Birds: males inflate a bright red gular (throat) pouch in courtship and use it to produce a drumming sound.</sub>

### dd-0590 · hard · unverified

**When great frigatebirds sleep while flying, how much of the brain can be asleep?**

- A. None; they never sleep aloft
- B. **One half, or both halves at once** ✓
- C. Only a tiny region near the spine

Brain recordings from frigatebirds flying over the ocean showed they can sleep with one hemisphere or both, mostly while circling in rising air.

Source: [Nature Communications (via PubMed Central)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4976198/) · second source: [Nature Communications](https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms12468)

Fact id: `seabirds.great-frigatebird.unihemispheric-flight-sleep` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: 2016 Nature Communications study (Rattenborg et al.): great frigatebirds in flight can sleep with either one hemisphere at a time or both simultaneously, mainly while circling in rising air currents.</sub>

### dd-0591 · medium · unverified

**About how much did great frigatebirds sleep per day while flying, in a 2016 study?**

- A. About 6 hours
- B. About 12 hours
- C. **Less than an hour** ✓

In flight they slept only about 0.69 hours a day, just 7.4% of the sleep they got back on land.

Source: [Nature Communications (via PubMed Central)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4976198/) · second source: [Nature Communications](https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms12468)

Fact id: `seabirds.great-frigatebird.flight-sleep-amount` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Same study: total sleep in flight was 0.69 hours per day, 7.4% of time spent sleeping on land.</sub>

### dd-0592 · medium · unverified

**About how fast can a northern gannet be moving when it plunges into the sea?**

- A. **About 100 km/h (60 mph)** ✓
- B. About 15 km/h (10 mph)
- C. About 400 km/h (250 mph)

Gannets fold their wings and plunge from around 30 meters up, reaching roughly 97 km/h (60 mph) before hitting the water.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/diving-gannets)

Fact id: `seabirds.northern-gannet.dive-speed` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 'The Diving Gannets': gannets plunge from about 30 m (100 ft) and reach about 97 km/h (up to 60 mph) during the dive.</sub>

### dd-0593 · medium · unverified

**What cushions a northern gannet's body when it slams into the water?**

- A. A thick fat layer on its skull
- B. **Air sacs in its face and chest** ✓
- C. Its outstretched wings

Air sacs under the skin of the face and chest work like a car's airbags, absorbing the shock of a high-speed dive.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/diving-gannets)

Fact id: `seabirds.northern-gannet.air-sacs-cushion` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 'The Diving Gannets': air sacs in the face and chest cushion the impact, like airbags in a car.</sub>

### dd-0594 · hard · unverified

**Unlike most birds, northern gannets lack external nostrils. How do they breathe?**

- A. Through holes behind the eyes
- B. Through pores in the skin
- C. **Through openings inside the mouth** ✓

Gannets breathe through nostrils inside the mouth rather than through openings on the bill like most birds.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/ocean-life/seabirds/diving-gannets)

Fact id: `seabirds.northern-gannet.no-external-nostrils` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian 'The Diving Gannets': unlike most birds, gannets do not have external nostrils but breathe through nostrils inside their mouths.</sub>

Fact id: `seabirds.northern-gannet.bass-rock-colony` · risk: none

### dd-0596 · medium · unverified

**What devastated the Bass Rock northern gannet colony in 2022?**

- A. A major oil spill
- B. **An avian flu outbreak** ✓
- C. A volcanic eruption

Highly pathogenic avian flu swept through the Bass Rock colony in 2022, killing many thousands of gannets.

Source: [Edinburgh Napier University](https://www.napier.ac.uk/about-us/news/bass-rock-gannet-drone-survey-research)

### dd-0597 · hard · review

**A study of plunge-diving northern gannets found that individual birds tend to do what?**

- A. Dive only in synchronized pairs
- B. Close one eye just before impact
- C. **Favor rolling to one particular side** ✓

Like left- or right-handed people, individual gannets showed a consistent preferred roll direction as they twisted into their dives.

Source: [PubMed Central (peer-reviewed)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10480694/)

## kelp-plankton (11)

### dd-0598 · medium · unverified

**Kelp can look like an underwater tree. What kind of organism is it?**

- A. A flowering plant
- B. **A large brown alga** ✓
- C. A colony of tiny animals

Despite its leafy look, kelp isn't a plant at all. It's a large brown alga, grouped with the other algae we call seaweeds.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/kelp-and-kelp-forests)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp.not-a-plant` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary attributed to this page the statement that kelp are not plants; they are grouped within the algae (the seaweeds) and are brown algae.</sub>

### dd-0599 · hard · unverified

**Which pigment gives kelp its golden-brown color?**

- A. **Fucoxanthin** ✓
- B. Phycoerythrin
- C. Melanin

Kelp has chlorophyll like other photosynthesizers, but a pigment called fucoxanthin masks the green and gives it its brown tint.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/kelp-and-kelp-forests)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp.fucoxanthin-pigment` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: Search summary said kelp are brown algae in the order Laminariales whose color comes from the pigment fucoxanthin. The summary did not name the page for this sentence explicitly; the Smithsonian kelp page was the top result. Verifier: confirm fucoxanthin is on this page, and that the 'masks the green' wording is supported, or trim the explanation.</sub>

### dd-0600 · easy · unverified

**What is the main job of a kelp's holdfast, the root-like part at its base?**

- A. Absorbing nutrients like a root
- B. Storing gas to float the blades
- C. **Gripping rock to anchor the kelp** ✓

The holdfast's finger-like grips cling to rock and anchor the kelp. Unlike a plant root, it doesn't take up nutrients.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/bull-kelp) · second source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/nature/marineplants.htm)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp.holdfast-anchor` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium bull kelp page describes the holdfast as a root-like structure with finger-like projections that grip rock. An NPS page in the results says holdfasts work somewhat like roots but do not absorb nutrients (the summary did not say which NPS page; Point Reyes or Glacier Bay kelp-forest page are the candidates).</sub>

### dd-0601 · easy · unverified

**What do the gas-filled bladders on kelp, called pneumatocysts, do?**

- A. Store food for the winter
- B. **Keep the blades floating near the surface** ✓
- C. Release spores into the water

These little gas-filled floats lift the kelp's blades up toward the sunlit surface, where they can photosynthesize.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/nature/marineplants.htm)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp.pneumatocyst-buoyancy` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary: pneumatocysts are gas-filled bladders that provide buoyancy and hold the blades near the surface; blades are where photosynthesis happens (NPS Point Reyes).</sub>

### dd-0602 · medium · unverified

**How does bull kelp hold its blades up near the surface?**

- A. **With one large float at the top of its stalk** ✓
- B. With a small float at the base of every blade
- C. With hollow blades filled with oil

Bull kelp carries a single big gas bladder topped with a cluster of blades. Giant kelp, by contrast, has a small float at the base of each blade.

Source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/pore/learn/nature/marineplants.htm) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/bull-kelp)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.bull-kelp.single-float` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NPS Point Reyes: giant kelp has one pneumatocyst on each blade, while bull kelp has a single pneumatocyst supporting many blades. Monterey Bay Aquarium: bull kelp's canopy sits at the top, while giant kelp has blades along the whole stipe.</sub>

### dd-0603 · hard · unverified

**The gas inside a bull kelp's float can contain a surprising amount of which gas?**

- A. Helium
- B. Methane
- C. **Carbon monoxide** ✓

Surprisingly, the gas in a bull kelp's float can include a significant share of carbon monoxide, which the kelp itself produces.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/bull-kelp)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.bull-kelp.carbon-monoxide-float` · risk: surprising

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium bull kelp page says the gas in the pneumatocyst can contain up to 10 percent carbon monoxide (the search summary said the page links it to photosynthesis). No second source was found. Verifier: confirm the exact figure and whether the page says 'byproduct'.</sub>

### dd-0604 · medium · unverified

**Bull kelp can grow from a microscopic spore into a towering adult in about how long?**

- A. **A single year** ✓
- B. About ten years
- C. About thirty years

Bull kelp is an annual: it goes from spore to full size within one year, and the Smithsonian says it lives at most about 18 months.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/bull-kelp) · second source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/kelp-and-kelp-forests)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.bull-kelp.annual-life-cycle` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: Monterey Bay Aquarium: bull kelp grows from a spore to maturity within a single year. Smithsonian Ocean: bull kelp are annuals, living a maximum of 18 months.</sub>

### dd-0605 · medium · unverified

**Under ideal conditions, giant kelp can grow up to about how much in a single day?**

- A. Two inches
- B. **Two feet** ✓
- C. Twenty feet

In good conditions giant kelp can add roughly two feet (about 60 cm) a day, though its usual pace is slower.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/pgallery/pgchannel/living/living_9.html) · second source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals/habitats/kelp-forest)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.giant-kelp.max-growth-rate` · risk: numeric

<sub>Research note: NOAA Channel Islands page gives 50-60 cm (20-25 inches) per day for giant kelp. Monterey Bay Aquarium says giant kelp can grow up to two feet a day under ideal conditions, typically 10-12 inches daily in Monterey Bay (the summary did not say which of the aquarium's two kelp-forest URLs had this).</sub>

### dd-0606 · easy · rejected

**In Alaska's kelp forests, what tends to happen when sea otters disappear?**

- A. Kelp grows so thick that fish leave
- B. Seals move in and eat the kelp
- C. **Urchins multiply and graze the kelp away** ✓

Sea otters eat sea urchins. Without otters, urchins can mow down a kelp forest and leave a bare 'urchin barren' behind.

Source: [Smithsonian Ocean](https://ocean.si.edu/kelp-and-kelp-forests) · second source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/science/sentinel-site-program/monterey-bay/wildlife-health.html)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp-forest.urchin-barren` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Smithsonian Ocean: otters keep Alaskan kelp forests balanced; without them urchins would mow down the kelp and an 'urchin barren' would emerge. NOAA Monterey Bay: when sea otters decline, urchin numbers explode and impact kelp. A 2024 PNAS study notes the effect is weaker in some places (San Nicolas Island), so the question is limited to Alaska and says 'tends to'.</sub>

### dd-0607 · medium · rejected

**Which sea star is considered a keystone predator of West Coast kelp forests because it eats several sea urchins a day?**

- A. Crown-of-thorns sea star
- B. **Sunflower sea star** ✓
- C. Chocolate chip sea star

The sunflower sea star can eat several urchins a day, which helps keep urchins from overgrazing the kelp along the West Coast.

Source: [NOAA Office of National Marine Sanctuaries](https://sanctuaries.noaa.gov/news/2026/restoring-balance.html)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.sunflower-sea-star.urchin-predator` · risk: species-specific

<sub>Research note: NOAA page says sunflower sea stars are considered a keystone predator essential for the survival of kelp forests along the West Coast because they can consume several urchins per day. Distractors are tropical Indo-Pacific species that do not live in West Coast kelp forests.</sub>

### dd-0608 · medium · unverified

**Algin, a substance extracted from kelp, is added to foods like ice cream for what purpose?**

- A. As a sweetener
- B. As a red food dye
- C. **As a thickener and stabilizer** ✓

Algin from kelp helps thicken, bind and stabilize foods and cosmetics, from ice cream to toothpaste.

Source: [Monterey Bay Aquarium](https://www.montereybayaquarium.org/animals-the-ocean/animals-a-to-z/giant-kelp) · second source: [National Park Service](https://www.nps.gov/places/000/sign-kelp-forest.htm)

Fact id: `kelp-plankton.kelp.algin-food-use` · risk: none

<sub>Research note: Search summary: kelp-derived algin is an emulsifying and binding agent used in many foods and cosmetics, like ice cream, cereal and toothpaste (likely the Monterey Bay Aquarium giant kelp page). An NPS page says algin is used as a suspending, stabilizing, emulsifying, gel-producing and film-forming additive in more than 70 commercial products; the summary did not say which of three NPS pages (Sign: Kelp Forest, Cherry Canyon Stop 8, Sea Lion Rookery Overlook) carried this. Verifier: confirm attribution.</sub>
